Generalized Green functions and unipotent classes
for finite reductive groups, IV

Abstract
. In this paper, we formulate the notion of split elements of a unipotent class in a connected reductive group G G . Generalized Green functions of G G can be computed by using Lusztig’s algorithm, if split elements exist for any unipotent class. The existence of split elements is reduced to the case where G G is a simply connected, almost simple group. We show, in the case of classical groups, that there exist split elements, which is a refinement of previous results. In the case of exceptional groups, we show the existence of split elements, possibly except one class for G G of type E 7 E_{7} .



0.
Introduction

This paper is a continuation of a series of papers

[
Sh5
, I, II, III]
.
Let
G
be a connected reductive group defined over a finite field

\mathbf{F}_{q}
of characteristic
p
, with Frobenius map
F:G\to G
,
and
G_{\operatorname{uni}}
the unipotent variety of
G
.
The aim of these papers is to determine the generalized Green functions
of
G
. This problem is reduced, by Lusztig’s algorithm (
[
L3
, IV]
),
to the computation of certain functions
Y_{j}
on the space of

G^{F}
-invariant functions on
G_{\operatorname{uni}}^{F}
. Since
Y_{j}
is written as

Y_{j}=\gamma_{j}Y_{j}^{0}
for a certain scalar
\gamma_{j}
, where
Y_{j}^{0}
is a function
explicitly computable, the above problem is reduced to the determination
of those scalars
\gamma_{j}
. On the other hand, the determination of generalized Green functions
is reduced to the case where
G
is a simply connected, almost simple group.
In
[
Sh5
, II,III]
,
\gamma_{j}
was determined in the case of classical groups,
by an inductive formula.
In this paper, we give a closed formula for
\gamma_{j}
in the case of classical groups.
Also we determine
\gamma_{j}
in the case of exceptional groups (except
one class in
G=E_{7}
).
Thus the generalized Green functions are computable for all the cases, modulo
the above exception.

Here we give some historical backgrounds. The Green functions
Q^{G}_{T}
of
G^{F}
, where

T
is an
F
-stable maximal torus of
G
, were
introduced by Deligne-Lusztig
[
DL
]
in 1976,
in connection with the representation theory of finite reductive groups
G^{F}
over
\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{l}
.
Springer
[
Spr
]
defined certain functions, by making use of the Springer
representations of Weyl groups (also constructed in that paper), and conjectured
that those functions coincide with Deligne-Lusztig’s Green functions. This conjecture
was proved by Kazhdan
[
K
]
under the assumption that
p
and
q
are large enough.
The algorithm of computing Springer’s Green functions was given by

[
Sh2
]
for exceptional groups of type
F_{4}
, and by
[
Sh3
]
for classical groups.
Based on this algorithm, Green functions for exceptional groups were computed,
by
[
Sh2
]
for type
F_{4}
, and by Beynon-Spaltenstein
[
BS
]
for types
E_{6},E_{7}
and
E_{8}
,
under the assumption that
p
is a good prime (no restriction on
q
). The case
G_{2}
was
already computed by Springer
[
Spr
]
.

The generalized Green functions
Q_{L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0},\varphi_{0}}^{G}
were introduced
by Lusztig
[
L3
]
in the study of character sheaves (see 1.2 for the definition),
based on the theory of generalized Springer correspondence (
[
L2
]
), where

L
is an
F
-stable Levi subgroup of a parabolic subgroup of
G
.
The special case where
L=T
is a maximal torus is nothing but Springer’s Green functions,
which corresponds to the case of Springer correspondence (see (1.1.4)).
The coincidence of those Green functions with Deligne-Lusztig’s Green functions
(and its generalization to the case of generalized Green functions)
was proved in
[
L4
]
in 1990 for a large enough
q
(for any
p
).
This restriction on
q
was removed in
[
Sh4
]
in the case where

G
has a connected center, by applying the theory of Shintani descent.

In 2020, Green functions for exceptional groups in bad characteristic
were computed by Geck
[
G2
]
, except for type
E_{8}
. He reduced in
[
G1
]

the computation for the case of
\mathbf{F}_{q}
to the case of the prime field
\mathbf{F}_{p}
,
and computed the Green functions in that case by using CHEVIE,
by making use of the representation theory of
G^{F}
(note that the computation
of Green functions is reduced to the case of adjoint groups, and by the previous results
those Green functions coincide with Deligne-Lusztig’s Green functions).
Recently, by extending the method of Geck, one of the authors computed in
[
Lü
]

the Green functions of type
E_{8}
in bad characteristic.
Hence all the Green functions were computed.
However, as far as the generalized Green functions are
concerned, no results in the case of exceptional groups are known.

As mentioned above, the theory of generalized Green functions is based on the
theory of generalized Springer correspondence. In the case of exceptional groups,
the generalized Springer correspondence was determined by Spaltenstein
[
Sp5
]
in 1985,
except for a few cases. The remaining cases are one unipotent class in
G
, of type
E_{6}

with
p\neq 3
, and of type
E_{8}
with
p=3
. In 2019, Lusztig
[
L5
]
solved
this problem in the case of
E_{6}
, by using a geometric method.
Finally in 2023, Hetz
[
H
]
solved it in the case of
E_{8}
, by computing values
of certain unipotent characters of
G^{F}
at unipotent elements by the aide of CHEVIE
(here Deligne-Lusztig’s Green functions and their generalization were used).
Hence the generalized Springer correspondence is now determined completely.

In this paper, we compute the generalized Green functions for exceptional groups.
The main ingredient for the proof is the Frobenius version of the restriction
formula, as in the case of
[
Sh5
, II, III]
.
Also the notion of split elements plays a crucial role.
This notion already appeared in the previous papers, in ad hoc way.
In this paper, we give a notion of split elements, in a uniform way, which
is applicable for reductive groups in general.
If split elements exist for any unipotent class in
G
, then

\gamma_{j}
is determined explicitly, thus generalized Green functions are computable.
In the case where
F
is non-split,
our result gives a more precise description for
\gamma_{j}
. In particular, in the case where
G

is a special linear group with
F
non-split, we obtain a closed formula
for
\gamma_{j}
which improves the results in
[
Sh5
, I, II]
.
In the case of other classical groups, in particular, of spin groups,

[
Sh5
, III]
contains some errors. In this paper we have corrected them.
In the case of exceptional groups, we show that split elements exist in
any unipotent class
C
in
G
, possibly except one class in
E_{7}
.
This determines
\gamma_{j}
except for a few cases.

In the computation of generalized Green functions for exceptional groups,
we have made use of the results on the computation of Green functions.

Contents

0. Introduction

1. Split elements in reductive groups

2. Special linear groups

3. Classical groups

4. Preliminaries on exceptional groups

5. The case
E_{7}

6. The case
E_{8}

7. The case
F_{4}

8. The case
G_{2}

9. The case
E_{6}

10. The case
{}^{3}D_{4}

1.
Split elements in reductive groups

1.1

This paper is a continuation of
[
Sh5
, I,II,III]
.
First we review the definition of generalized Springer correspondence
and generalized Green functions.
Let
G
be a connected reductive group defined over a finite field
\mathbf{F}_{q}

with Frobenius map
F
. Let
\mathbf{k}
be an algebraic closure of
\mathbf{F}_{q}
with

\operatorname{ch}\mathbf{k}=p
.
Let
\mathscr{M}_{G}
be the set of triples
(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})
, up to
G
-conjugacy,
where
L
is a Levi subgroup of a parabolic subgroup
P
of
G
, and

\mathscr{E}_{0}
is a cuspidal local system on a unipotent class
C_{0}
of
L
.
As in (1.2.2) in
[
Sh5
, I]
, one can define a semisimple perverse sheaf
K
on
G

associated to the
triple
(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\in\mathscr{M}_{G}
. Let
\mathscr{W}_{L}=N_{G}(L)/L
. Then

\mathscr{W}_{L}
is a Coxeter group, and
\operatorname{End}K\simeq\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{l}[\mathscr{W}_{L}]
. Hence
K
is decomposed as

(1.1.1)

K\simeq\bigoplus_{E\in\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\wedge}}E\otimes K_{E},

where
K_{E}\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(E,K)
is a simple perverse sheaf on
G

associated to
E\in\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\wedge}
.

Let
G_{\operatorname{uni}}
be the unipotent variety of
G
, and let
\mathscr{N}_{G}
be the set of
(C,\mathscr{E})
,
where
C
is a unipotent class of
G
, and
\mathscr{E}
is a
G
-equivariant
simple local system on
C
.
Let
Z_{L}
be the center of
L
, and put
d=\dim Z_{L}
. It is known that

K[-d]|_{G_{\operatorname{uni}}}
is a semisimple perverse sheaf on
G_{\operatorname{uni}}
, equipped with
\mathscr{W}_{L}
-action,
and is decomposed as

(1.1.2)

K[-d]|_{G_{\operatorname{uni}}}\simeq\bigoplus_{(C,\mathscr{E})\in\mathscr{N}_{G}}V_{(C,\mathscr{E})}\otimes\operatorname{IC}(\overline{C},\mathscr{E})[\dim C],

where the multiplicity space
V_{(C,\mathscr{E})}
has a structure of
an irreducible
\mathscr{W}_{L}
-module if it is non-zero.
It follows from (1.1.1) and (1.1.2) that
K_{E}|_{G_{\operatorname{uni}}}
is expressed as

(1.1.3)

K_{E}|_{G_{\operatorname{uni}}}\simeq\operatorname{IC}(\overline{C},\mathscr{E})[\dim C+\dim Z_{L}]

for some
(C,\mathscr{E})\in\mathscr{N}_{G}
, and we have a map
\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\wedge}\to\mathscr{N}_{G},E\mapsto(C,\mathscr{E})

such that
E\simeq V_{(C,\mathscr{E})}
.
The generalized Springer correspondence (
[
L2
, Thm. 6.5]
)
asserts that this map gives a bijection

(1.1.4)

\bigsqcup_{(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\in\mathscr{M}_{G}}\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\wedge}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\mathscr{N}_{G}.

In the case where
L=T
is a maximal torus, then

(T,\{1\},1)\in\mathscr{M}_{G}
, where
\{1\}
is the trivial class in
T
, and

1=\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{l}
is the constant sheaf on
\{1\}
.
\mathscr{W}_{L}
corresponding to

(T,\{1\},1)
is the Weyl group
W=N_{G}(T)/T
of
G
,
and the injection
W^{\wedge}\to\mathscr{N}_{G}
gives the Springer correspondence.

If we choose
u\in C
, then
\mathscr{E}
corresponds bijectively to
\rho\in A_{G}(u)^{\wedge}
,
in such a way that the stalk
\mathscr{E}_{u}
has a natural structure of
A_{G}(u)
-module
isomorphic to
\rho
. (In
[
Sh5
, III]
,
\rho^{*}
was used instead of
\rho
.
This is just a problem of the parametrization.) In this way, the pair

(C,\mathscr{E})
is in bijection with the
G
-orbit of the pair
(u,\rho)
.
We write it as
(u,\rho)\leftrightarrow(C,\mathscr{E})\in\mathscr{N}_{G}
, and
denote the representation as
V_{(u,\rho)}=V_{(C,\mathscr{E})}
.

1.2.

Let
Z_{G}
be the center of
G
. The generalized
Springer correspondence (1.1.4) has a partition in terms of

Z_{G}^{\wedge}
as follows (see
[
L2
, 14.2]
,
[
Sh5
, I, 1.9]
).
For
\chi\in Z_{G}^{\wedge}
, let
\mathscr{N}_{\chi}
be the subset of
\mathscr{N}_{\chi}

consisting of
(u,\rho)\leftrightarrow(C,\mathscr{E})
such that
Z_{G}
acts on

\mathscr{E}
according to
\chi
. Let
\mathscr{M}_{\chi}
be the subset of

\mathscr{M}_{G}
consisting of
(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})
such that
Z_{G}
acts on

\mathscr{E}_{0}
according to
\chi
. Then we have partitions

\mathscr{N}_{G}=\bigsqcup_{\chi\in Z_{G}^{\wedge}}\mathscr{N}_{\chi}
,
\mathscr{M}_{G}=\bigsqcup_{\chi\in Z_{G}^{\wedge}}\mathscr{M}_{\chi}
,
and (1.1.4) restricts to a bijection, for each
\chi\in Z_{G}^{\wedge}
,

(1.2.1)

\bigsqcup_{(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\in\mathscr{M}_{\chi}}\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\wedge}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\mathscr{N}_{\chi}.

1.3.

F
acts naturally on the set
\mathscr{N}_{G}
and
\mathscr{M}_{G}
by

(C,\mathscr{E})\mapsto(F^{-1}(C),F^{*}\mathscr{E})
,

(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\mapsto(F^{-1}(L),F^{-1}(C_{0}),F^{*}\mathscr{E}_{0})
.
The map in (1.1.4) is compatible with this
F
-action.
We choose
(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\in\mathscr{M}_{G}^{F}
. Here we may assume that

L
is an
F
-stable Levi subgroup of a (not necessarily
F
-stable)
parabolic subgroup
P
of
G
,
and that
F(C_{0})=C_{0},F^{*}\mathscr{E}_{0}\simeq\mathscr{E}_{0}
.
Since
\mathscr{E}_{0}
is a simple local system, the isomorphism
\varphi_{0}:F^{*}\mathscr{E}_{0}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\mathscr{E}_{0}

is unique up to scalar.
We choose
\varphi_{0}
by the condition that
the induced map on the stalk of
\mathscr{E}_{0}
at any point in
C_{0}^{F}
is of finite order.

\varphi_{0}
induces a natural isomorphism
\varphi:F^{*}K\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,K
.
The generalized Green function
Q^{G}_{L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0},\varphi_{0}}
is defined as
the restriction
of the characteristic function
\chi_{K,\varphi}
on
G^{F}
to
G^{F}_{\operatorname{uni}}
(cf.
[
L3
, II]
),
which is a
G^{F}
-invariant function on
G^{F}_{\operatorname{uni}}
with values in
\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{l}
.
In the case where
(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})=(T,\{1\},1)\in\mathscr{M}_{G}
,
Q^{G}_{L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0},\varphi_{0}}

(for the canonical isomorphism
\varphi_{0}:F^{*}\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{l}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{l}
)
is called the Green function of
G^{F}
, and is denoted by
Q^{G}_{T}
.

1.4.

Let
B
be an
F
-stable Borel subgroup of
G
, and
T
an
F
-stable maximal torus
of
G
contained in
B
.
We assume that
P
is an
F
-stable parabolic subgroup of
G
containing
B
, and

L
an
F
-stable Levi subgroup of
P
containing
T
.

F
acts naturally on
\mathscr{W}_{L}
, which induces a Coxeter group automorphism

\sigma
of order
c
. We consider the semidirect product
\widetilde{\mathscr{W}}_{L}=\operatorname{\!\langle\!}\sigma\operatorname{\!\rangle\!}\ltimes\mathscr{W}_{L}
.
Let
(\mathscr{W}_{L})^{\wedge}_{\operatorname{ex}}
be the set of (isomorphism classes of)
irreducible representations of
\mathscr{W}_{L}
which are extendable to
\widetilde{\mathscr{W}}_{L}
.
For each
E\in(\mathscr{W}_{L})^{\wedge}_{\operatorname{ex}}
, we choose a specific extension
\widetilde{E}
to
\widetilde{\mathscr{W}}_{L}
,
called the preferred extension of
E
(for the definition, see 1.6),
and let
\sigma_{E}:E\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,E
be the action of
\sigma
on
\widetilde{E}
.
By using the decomposition (1.1.1), one sees, for each
E\in(\mathscr{W}_{L})^{\wedge}_{\operatorname{ex}}
, that
there exists a unique isomorphism
\varphi_{E}:F^{*}K_{E}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,K_{E}
such that

\sigma_{E}\otimes\varphi_{E}:F^{*}(E\otimes K_{E})\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,E\otimes K_{E}
gives the restriction of

\varphi:F^{*}K\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,K
.
By making use of the isomorphism (1.1.3), one can define an isomorphism

\psi:F^{*}\mathscr{E}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\mathscr{E}
induced from
\varphi_{E}:F^{*}K_{E}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,K_{E}

by the condition that
q^{(a_{0}+r)/2}\psi
corresponds to the map

F^{*}(\mathscr{H}^{a_{0}}K_{E})\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\mathscr{H}^{a_{0}}K_{E}
induced from
\varphi_{E}
, where

(1.4.1)

\displaystyle a_{0}

\displaystyle=-\dim Z_{L}-\dim C,

\displaystyle r

\displaystyle=\dim G-\dim L+\dim(C_{0}\times Z_{L}).

Note that
\mathscr{H}^{a_{0}}K_{E}|_{C}=\mathscr{E}
and
r=\dim\operatorname{supp}\,K_{E}
. Also note the relation

(1.4.2)

a_{0}+r=(\dim G-\dim C)-(\dim L-\dim C_{0}).

The generalized Green function
Q^{G}_{L^{\prime},C^{\prime}_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0}^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime}_{0}}
defined in 1.3
(for an
F
-stable Levi subgroup
L^{\prime}
of a not necessarily
F
-stable parabolic
subgroup
P^{\prime}
of
G
) is obtained from this
L
as

Q^{G}_{L^{w},C_{0}^{w},\mathscr{E}_{0}^{w},\varphi_{0}^{w}}=\chi_{K,w\circ\varphi}
, where
(L^{w},C_{0}^{w},\mathscr{E}_{0}^{w},\varphi_{0}^{w})

are systems obtained from
(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0},\varphi_{0})
by twisting
w\in\mathscr{W}_{L}

(see
[
L3
, II, 10.6]
). In the case of Green functions, it is denoted by

Q^{G}_{T_{w}}
, where
T_{w}
is an
F
-stable maximal torus twisted from
T
by
w\in W
.

We denote by
I
the set
\mathscr{N}_{G}
. For
i=(C,\mathscr{E})\in I^{F}
belonging to

(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\in\mathscr{M}_{G}^{F}
, let
E_{i}\in(\mathscr{W}_{L})^{\wedge}_{\operatorname{ex}}
be the representation
defined by
E_{i}=V_{(C,\mathscr{E})}
. We define a function
X_{i}
on
G^{F}_{\operatorname{uni}}
by

(1.4.3)

X_{i}(g)=\sum_{a}(-1)^{a+a_{0}}\operatorname{Tr}\,(\varphi_{E_{i}},\mathscr{H}^{a}_{g}K_{E_{i}})q^{-(a_{0}+r)/2}

for
g\in G^{F}_{\operatorname{uni}}
, where
\varphi_{E_{i}}:F^{*}K_{E_{i}}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,K_{E_{i}}
is
as in 1.4, and
a_{0}+r
is defined with respect to
C
for
i=(C,\mathscr{E})
.
Then
Q^{G}_{L^{w},C_{0}^{w},\mathscr{E}_{0}^{w},\varphi_{0}^{w}}(g)
can be written as

(1.4.4)

Q^{G}_{L^{w},C_{0}^{w},\mathscr{E}_{0}^{w},\varphi_{0}^{w}}(g)=\sum_{i\in I^{F}}\operatorname{Tr}\,(w\sigma_{E_{i}},\widetilde{E}_{i})X_{i}(g)(-1)^{(a_{0})_{i}}q^{(a_{0}+r)_{i}/2},

where
(a_{0})_{i},(a_{0}+r)_{i}
denote
a_{0},a_{0}+r
with respect to
i=(C,\mathscr{E})
.

For each
i\in I^{F}
, we define a function
Y_{i}
on
G^{F}_{\operatorname{uni}}
by

(1.4.5)

Y_{i}(g)=\begin{cases}\operatorname{Tr}\,(\psi,\mathscr{E}_{g})&\quad\text{ if }g\in C^{F},\\
0&\quad\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

Then
\{Y_{i}\mid i\in I^{F}\}
gives rise to a basis of the
\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{l}
-space
of
G^{F}
-invariant functions of
G^{F}_{\operatorname{uni}}
. In
[
L3
, V]
, Lusztig gave
an algorithm of computing
X_{i}
as an explicit linear combination of
those functions
Y_{j}
. In view of (1.4.4), the determination of generalized
Green functions is reduced to the determination of the functions
Y_{i}
.
But the computation of
Y_{i}
is very subtle.

1.5.

Since
\mathscr{E}
is a simple local system, the isomorphism
\psi:F^{*}\mathscr{E}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\mathscr{E}

is unique up to scalar. Among them, the typical isomorphism
\psi_{0}:F^{*}\mathscr{E}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\mathscr{E}

is given as follows; choose
u\in C^{F}
, and put
A_{G}(u)=Z_{G}(u)/Z_{G}^{0}(u)
.
Then
F
acts on
Z_{G}(u)
, and acts on
A_{G}(u)
as a permutation of connected components
of
Z_{G}(u)
.
Let
\tau
be the action of
F
on
A_{G}(u)
, and consider the semidirect product

\widetilde{A}_{G}(u)=\operatorname{\!\langle\!}\tau\operatorname{\!\rangle\!}\ltimes A_{G}(u)
.
Let
A_{G}(u)^{\wedge}_{\operatorname{ex}}
be the set of irreducible representations of
A_{G}(u)
(up to isomorphism)
which are extendable to an irreducible representation of
\widetilde{A}_{G}(u)
.
Assume that
(u,\rho)\leftrightarrow(C,\mathscr{E})\in\mathscr{N}_{G}^{F}
, then

\rho\in A_{G}(u)^{\wedge}_{\operatorname{ex}}
. The condition

F^{*}\mathscr{E}\simeq\mathscr{E}
is equivalent to saying that there exists an extension

\widetilde{\rho}
of
\rho
such that the stalk
\mathscr{E}_{u}
has the structure of
\widetilde{A}_{G}(u)
-module
corresponding to
\widetilde{\rho}
.

We define
\psi_{0}:F^{*}\mathscr{E}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\mathscr{E}
by the condition that the induced map
on
\mathscr{E}_{u}
coincides with the action of
\tau
on
\mathscr{E}_{u}=\widetilde{\rho}
, and define
a function
Y_{i}^{0}
on
G_{\operatorname{uni}}^{F}
in a similar way as
Y_{i}
, but by replacing
\psi
by
\psi_{0}
.
Note that the set of
G^{F}
-conjugacy classes in
C^{F}
is in bijection with
the set of
A_{G}(u)
-conjugacy classes in the coset
A_{G}(u)\tau\subset\widetilde{A}_{G}(u)
.
We denote by
u_{a}
the
G^{F}
-class corresponding to the class
a\tau\in A_{G}(u)\tau
.
Then
Y_{i}^{0}
is explicitly given as follows;

(1.5.1)

Y_{i}^{0}=\begin{cases}\operatorname{Tr}\,(a\tau,\widetilde{\rho})&\quad\text{ if $g$ is $G^{F}$-conjugate to $u_{a}$, }\\
0&\quad\text{ if $g\notin C^{F}$.}\end{cases}

Note that
Y_{i}^{0}
is computable by the formula (1.5.1). Since
\psi=\gamma\psi_{0}

for some
\gamma\in\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{l}^{*}
, we have
Y_{i}=\gamma Y_{i}^{0}
. Thus the computation of

Y_{i}
is reduced to the determination of
\gamma
.
We choose
u_{0}\in C_{0}^{F}
, and consider
\widetilde{A}_{L}(u_{0})=\operatorname{\!\langle\!}\tau_{0}\operatorname{\!\rangle\!}\ltimes A_{L}(u_{0})
,
where
\tau_{0}
is the permutation action of
F
on
A_{L}(u_{0})
.
Let
\rho_{0}\in A_{L}(u_{0})^{\wedge}_{\operatorname{ex}}
be the one corresponding to
\mathscr{E}_{0}
, and

\widetilde{\rho}_{0}
its extension to
\widetilde{A}_{L}(u_{0})
.
We can define
\varphi_{0}:F^{*}\mathscr{E}_{0}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\mathscr{E}_{0}
by the condition that
the induced map on
(\mathscr{E}_{0})_{u_{0}}
coincides with the action of
\tau_{0}
on

\widetilde{\rho}_{0}
.
Thus
\gamma
depends on the choice of
(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u,\widetilde{\rho})
, which we denote by

\gamma=\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u,\widetilde{\rho})
.
Note that
\varphi_{0}:F^{*}\mathscr{E}_{0}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\mathscr{E}_{0}
satisfies the requirement of
\varphi_{0}

in 1.3, since the order of
\tau_{0}
is finite.

1.6.

Here we review the definition of the preferred extensions following

[
L3
, V, 17.2]
. Let
(W,S)
be a Coxeter system of a Weyl group
W
,
and
\sigma:W\to W
the Coxeter group automorphism of order
c
. We denote by

\widetilde{W}=\operatorname{\!\langle\!}\sigma\operatorname{\!\rangle\!}\ltimes W
the semidirect product of
W
with the cyclic group

\operatorname{\!\langle\!}\sigma\operatorname{\!\rangle\!}
of order
c
. Let
E
be an irreducible
W
-module (over
\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{l}
)
which is extendable to a
\widetilde{W}
-module. Then
E
can be extended in
c
different ways
to a
\widetilde{W}
-module. A particular type of extension, called the preferred extension,

\widetilde{E}
of
E
is defined for each case separately as follows.

(a)
W
is irreducible, and
c=1
, then take
\widetilde{E}=E
.

(b)
W
of type
A_{n}
(
n\geq 2
) or
E_{6}
, and
c=2
.
Define
\widetilde{E}
by the condition that
\sigma:E\to E
acts as
(-1)^{a_{E}}\cdot w_{0}
,
where
w_{0}
is the longest element in
W
, and
a:E\to a_{E}
is the
a
-function
defined in
[
L1
, 4.1]
.

(c)
W
of type
D_{4}
and
c=3
. Define
\widetilde{E}
to be the unique extension of

E
which is defined over
\mathbf{Q}
.

(d)
W
of type
D_{n}
(
n\geq 4
) and
c=2
.
In this case,
\widetilde{W}
is identified with the Weyl group
W_{n}
of type
B_{n}
.
The irreducible representations of
W_{n}
are parametrized by the pairs
(\lambda;\mu)
of
partitions,

\lambda:0\leq\lambda_{1}\leq\cdots\leq\lambda_{r},\mu:0\leq\mu_{1}\leq\cdots\leq\mu_{r}

such that
\sum\lambda_{i}+\sum\mu_{i}=n

(with the same
r
).
We denote by the same symbol
(\lambda;\mu)
the corresponding irreducible
W_{n}
-module.
If
\lambda\neq\mu
, the restriction of
(\lambda;\mu)
gives an irreducible
W
-module,
and in that case,
(\lambda;\mu)
and
(\mu;\lambda)
give isomorphic
W
-modules.
Any extendable
irreducible
W
-module
E
is obtained in this way, and the two extensions to
\widetilde{W}
are given
by
(\lambda;\mu)
and
(\mu;\lambda)
. Now the preferred extension
\widetilde{E}
of
E
is given by

(\lambda;\mu)
, where
\lambda_{i}>\mu_{i}
if
i
is the smallest integer such that
\lambda_{i}\neq\mu_{i}
.

(e) Assume that
W=W_{1}\times W_{2}\times\cdots\times W_{r}
, with
W_{i}
irreducible
Weyl groups mutually isomorphic,
and
\sigma
permutes cyclically the factors
W_{i}
.
Then
E
can be written as an external tensor product

E=E_{1}\boxtimes E_{2}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes E_{r}
, and for
E\in W^{\wedge}_{\operatorname{ex}}
,
the preferred extension
\widetilde{E}
is constructed from the preferred extension
\widetilde{E}_{r}

with respect to the isomorphism
\sigma^{r}:W_{r}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,W_{r}
as in
[
L3
, IV, 17.2]
.

(f) In the general case, one can write uniquely

W=W^{(1)}\times\cdots\times W^{(t)}
and

E=E^{(1)}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes E^{(t)}
,
where
W^{(i)}
are
\sigma
-stable Weyl groups satisfying the condition in (e), and

E^{(i)}
are irreducible
W^{(i)}
-modules. Define the preferred extension

\sigma:\widetilde{E}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\widetilde{E}
by the condition that
\sigma
is an external tensor
product of the maps
\sigma^{(i)}
on
\widetilde{E}^{(i)}
constructed as in (e).

1.7.

Returning to the original setup in 1.4, we consider the generalized Green functions

Q^{G}_{L^{w},C_{0}^{w},\mathscr{E}_{0}^{w},\varphi_{0}^{w}}
associated to
(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\in\mathscr{M}_{G}^{F}
.
Note that these functions are determined from a given isomorphism

\varphi_{0}:F^{*}\mathscr{E}_{0}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\mathscr{E}_{0}
. We choose such an isomorphism
\varphi_{0}
for each

(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\in\mathscr{M}_{G}^{F}
as above, and fix a set

(1.7.1)

\Xi_{G}=\{\varphi_{0}=\varphi_{(C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})}:F^{*}\mathscr{E}_{0}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\mathscr{E}_{0}\mid(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\in\mathscr{M}_{G}^{F}\}.

Hence
\Xi_{G}
determines the set of generalized Green functions

\{Q^{G}_{L^{w},C_{0}^{w},\mathscr{E}_{0}^{w},\varphi_{0}^{w}}\}
.

For each
F
-stable unipotent class
C
of
G
, we shall define a specific
representative
u\in C^{F}
, called a split element.
By induction on
\dim G
, if
L\neq G
, then we may assume that there exists
a split element
u_{0}\in C_{0}^{F}
. Assume that
(C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\leftrightarrow(u_{0},\rho_{0})
.
Then
\varphi_{0}:F^{*}\mathscr{E}_{0}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\mathscr{E}_{0}
determines the extension
\widetilde{\rho}_{0}

of
\rho_{0}
to
\widetilde{A}_{L}(u_{0})
.

We define a split element
u\in C^{F}
, associated to
\Xi_{G}
, for various cases separately.

(a) Assume that
G
is a simply connected, almost simple group, with
F
split.
First consider the case where
G
is not of type
E_{8}
.
If
(C,\mathscr{E})\in\mathscr{N}_{G}^{F}
is not cuspidal, then
(u,\rho)\leftrightarrow(C,\mathscr{E})
belongs to

(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})
with
L\neq G
. Then for
\rho\in A_{G}(u)^{\wedge}_{\operatorname{ex}}
, and
its extension
\widetilde{\rho}
, one can consider
\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u,\widetilde{\rho})
.
In the case where
(C,\mathscr{E})
is cuspidal,
\varphi_{0}:F^{*}\mathscr{E}_{0}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\mathscr{E}_{0}
for
\mathscr{E}_{0}=\mathscr{E}

is given by (1.7.1). This determines an extension
\widetilde{\rho}
of

\rho\in A_{G}(u)^{\wedge}_{\operatorname{ex}}
.
Now
u\in C^{F}
is called a split element if for each
\rho\in A_{G}(u)^{\wedge}_{\operatorname{ex}}
,
there exists an extension
\widetilde{\rho}
of
\rho
such that

(1.7.2)

\displaystyle\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u,\widetilde{\rho})

\displaystyle=1,\quad\text{ if $(u,\rho)$ not cuspidal,}

\displaystyle\gamma(u,\widetilde{\rho},u,\widetilde{\rho})

\displaystyle=1,\quad\text{ if $(u,\rho)$ cuspidal.}

Note that the second condition just requires that the function

\varphi_{0}:F^{*}\mathscr{E}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\mathscr{E}
coincides with
\psi_{0}:F^{*}\mathscr{E}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\mathscr{E}
for
\mathscr{E}=\mathscr{E}_{0}
,
and is automatically satisfied since
\psi_{0}
satisfies the condition for
\varphi_{0}
in 1.3.

Next consider the case where
G
is of type
E_{8}

(for the notation used below, see Section 4). Unless
q\equiv-1\pmod{3}
,
we use the same formula as (1.7.2) for defining a split element in
C^{F}
.
Now assume that
q\equiv-1\pmod{3}
. If
C
is not of type
D_{8}(a_{3})
, (1.7.2) is
also used. So assume that
C
is of type
D_{8}(a_{3})
.
In this case, any
(C,\mathscr{E})
belongs to
(T,\{1\},1)
. For
u\in C
,

A_{G}(u)\simeq S_{3}
. Let
\varepsilon
be the sign representation of

S_{3}
. We define a split element
u\in C^{F}
by the condition that

(1.7.3)

\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u,\rho)=\begin{cases}1&\quad\text{ if $\rho\neq\varepsilon$, },\\
-1&\quad\text{ if $\rho=\varepsilon$,}\end{cases}

where
(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0})=(1,1)
.

(b) Assume that
G
is a simply connected, almost simple group of type
A_{n}
or
E_{6}
,
with
F
non-split. Let
B
be a Borel subgroup of
G
, and for
u\in G_{\operatorname{uni}}
, define

\mathscr{B}_{u}=\{gB\in G/B\mid g^{-1}ug\in B\}
. We put
d_{u}=\dim\mathscr{B}_{u}
.
Take
(C,\mathscr{E})\in\mathscr{N}_{G}^{F}
such that
(C,\mathscr{E})
belongs to

\eta=(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\in\mathscr{M}_{G}^{F}
.
Let
E=V_{(u,\rho)}\in(\mathscr{W}_{L})^{\wedge}_{\operatorname{ex}}
for
(u,\rho)\leftrightarrow(C,\mathscr{E})
.
Let us define an integer
\delta_{E}
by

(1.7.4)

\delta_{E}=\begin{cases}a_{E}-d_{u}&\quad\text{ if $\mathscr{W}_{L}$ : type $A_{m}$ or $E_{6}$, }\\
0&\quad\text{ otherwise. }\end{cases}

We define a split element
u\in C^{F}
by the condition that there exists
an extension
\widetilde{\rho}
of
\rho
such that

(1.7.5)

\displaystyle\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u,\widetilde{\rho})

\displaystyle=\nu_{u,\eta}(-1)^{\delta_{E}},

\displaystyle\text{ if $(u,\rho)$ not cuspidal, with $E=V_{(u,\rho)}$, }

\displaystyle\gamma(u,\widetilde{\rho},u,\widetilde{\rho})

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle\text{ if $(u,\rho)$ cuspidal,}

where
\nu_{u,\eta}
is a root of unity determined by the class
C
and
\eta
.
For the explicit description of
\nu_{u,\eta}
, see Section 2 (resp. Section 9)
for
G=SL_{n}
(resp. for
G=E_{6}
).

(c) Assume that
G
is a simply connected, almost simple group of
type
D_{4}
with
F
non-split such that
F^{3}
is split. In this case,
we define a split element
u\in C^{F}
by the condition (1.7.2).

(d) Assume that
G
is a simply connected, almost simple group of
type
D_{n}
with
F
non-split such that
F^{2}
is split. In this case,
we define a split element
u\in C^{F}
by the condition (1.7.2).

(e) Assume that
G=G_{1}\times G_{2}\times\cdots\times G_{r}
, where

G_{i}
is a simply connected, almost simple group, and

F
permutes cyclically the factors
G_{i}
;
F:G_{1}\to G_{2}\to\cdots\to G_{r}\to G_{1}
.
Hence
F^{r}:G_{1}\to G_{1}
is a Frobenius map on
G_{1}
.
Then a unipotent class
C=C_{1}\times\cdots\times C_{r}
in
G
is
F
-stable
if and only if
F
permutes factors
C_{i}
cyclically, and

u_{1}\mapsto u=(u_{1},F(u_{1}),F^{2}(u_{1}),\cdots F^{r-1}(u_{1}))
gives a bijection

C_{1}^{F^{r}}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,C^{F}
.
We define a split element
u\in C^{F}
by the condition that
u_{1}\in C_{1}^{F^{r}}

is a split element in the sense of (a)
\sim
(d).

(f) Assume that
G=G^{(1)}\times G^{(2)}\times\cdots\times G^{(t)}
, and

G^{(i)}
are
F
-stable such that
(G^{(i)},F)
satisfies the condition in (e) for each
i
.
Let
C=C^{(1)}\times C^{(2)}\times\cdots\times C^{(t)}

be an
F
-stable unipotent class in
G
.
Then
C^{(i)}
is
F
-stable for each
i
. We define a split element

u=(u^{(1)},\dots,u^{(t)})\in C^{F}
by the condition that

u^{(i)}\in(C^{(i)})^{F}
is split for each
i
in the sense of (e).

(g) Assume that
G
is a connected reductive group of general type.
Let
\pi:G\to G/Z_{G}^{0}
be the natural projection, where
G/Z_{G}^{0}
is semisimple.
We define a split element
u\in G^{F}
by the condition that
\pi(u)
is
split in the semisimple group
(G/Z_{G}^{0})^{F}
.

1.8.

Let
G
be connected reductive, and
C
an
F
-stable
unipotent class in
G
. Assume that there exists a split element
u\in C^{F}
.
It is clear that if
u_{1}\in C^{F}
is
G^{F}
-conjugate to
u
,
then
u_{1}
is also split.

We consider the converse of the above situation.
Hence assume that
u,u_{1}\in C^{F}
, and choose
h\in G
such that

u_{1}=huh^{-1}
with
h^{-1}F(h)=\dot{a}\in Z_{G}(u)
, where
\dot{a}
is a representative
of
a\in A_{G}(u)
.
Then
\operatorname{ad}h
gives an isomorphism
A_{G}(u)\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,A_{G}(u_{1})
. It is easy to
check that under this isomorphism, the Frobenius action of
F

on
A_{G}(u_{1})
corresponds to the action of
aF
on
A_{G}(u)
,
where
a
acts as an inner automorphism on
A_{G}(u)
. We now assume that
the isomorphism
A_{G}(u)\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,A_{G}(u_{1})
can be extended to the isomorphism

\widetilde{A}_{G}(u)\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\widetilde{A}_{G}(u_{1})
, via
\tau\mapsto\tau_{1}
. This means that

a\in A_{G}(u)
is a central element.
For each
\rho\in A_{G}(u)^{\wedge}
,
\rho_{1}\in A_{G}(u_{1})^{\wedge}
is determined by
the isomorphism
A_{G}(u)\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,A_{G}(u_{1})
. If
\rho
is extendable, then

\rho_{1}
is also extendable, and an extension
\widetilde{\rho}
determines a unique
extension
\widetilde{\rho}_{1}
of
\rho_{1}
.
The following lemma shows that a split element is unique up to
G^{F}
-conjugate,
if it exists. Thus obtained
G^{F}
-class in
C^{F}
is called the split class.

Lemma 1.9
.

Let
u,u_{1}\in C^{F}
be elements such that

\widetilde{A}_{G}(u)\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\widetilde{A}_{G}(u_{1})
as in 1.8.
Assume that
u,u_{1}
satisfy the condition that

\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u,\widetilde{\rho})=\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u_{1},\widetilde{\rho}_{1})

for any pair
(C,\mathscr{E})\in\mathscr{N}_{G}^{F}
, where
\widetilde{\rho}
and
\widetilde{\rho}_{1}
are
related as in 1.8.
Then
u_{1}
is
G^{F}
-conjugate to
u
.

Proof.

Let
u,u_{1}\in C^{F}
be as in the lemma.
We assume that
u_{1}=huh^{-1}
with
h\in G
such that
h^{-1}F(h)=\dot{a}\in Z_{G}(u)
.
For
i=(C,\mathscr{E})\in\mathscr{N}_{G}^{F}
, let
Y_{i}^{0}
be the function defined in terms
of
u\in C^{F}
, and
Y^{\prime 0}_{i}
the function defined in terms of
u_{1}\in C^{F}
.
Thus
Y_{i}^{0}(x)=\operatorname{Tr}\,(c\tau,\widetilde{\rho})
if
x
is
G^{F}
-conjugate to
u_{c}
,
and
Y^{\prime 0}_{i}(x)=\operatorname{Tr}\,(c_{1}\tau_{1},\widetilde{\rho}_{1})
if
x
is
G^{F}
-conjugate to
(u_{1})_{c_{1}}
.
Since
\dot{c}_{1}\in Z_{G}(u_{1})=hZ_{G}(u)h^{-1}
, one can write as

h^{-1}\dot{c}_{1}h=\dot{c}\in Z_{G}(u)
.
Since
(u_{1})_{c_{1}}=gu_{1}g^{-1}
for
g\in G
such that
g^{-1}F(g)=\dot{c}_{1}
, we have

\dot{c}=h^{-1}\dot{c}_{1}h=h^{-1}(g^{-1}F(g))h=(gh)^{-1}F(gh)\cdot\dot{a}^{-1},

and so
\dot{c}\dot{a}=(gh)^{-1}F(gh)\in Z_{G}(u)
. This means that
(u_{1})_{c_{1}}
is

G^{F}
-conjugate to
u_{ca}
. By assumption, we have

Y_{i}^{0}=\gamma^{-1}Y_{i}=Y_{i}^{\prime 0}
, where

\gamma=\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u,\widetilde{\rho})=\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u_{1},\widetilde{\rho}_{1})
. It follows that

(1.9.1)

\operatorname{Tr}\,(c_{1}\tau_{1},\widetilde{\rho}_{1})=\operatorname{Tr}\,(ca\tau,\widetilde{\rho}).

On the other hand, by the isomorphism
\widetilde{A}_{G}(u)\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\widetilde{A}_{G}(u_{1})
,
we have

(1.9.2)

\operatorname{Tr}\,(c_{1}\tau_{1},\widetilde{\rho}_{1})=\operatorname{Tr}\,(c\tau,\widetilde{\rho}).

By (1.9.1) and (1.9.2), the equality

\operatorname{Tr}\,(ca\tau,\widetilde{\rho})=\operatorname{Tr}\,(c\tau,\widetilde{\rho})
holds for any
\rho\in A_{G}(u)^{\wedge}_{\operatorname{ex}}
.
By using the orthogonality relations of
\tau
-twisted class functions on
A_{G}(u)
, we see
that
ca
is
\tau
-twisted conjugate to
c
for any
c\in A_{G}(u)
, and so

a
is
\tau
-twisted conjugate to 1. This means that
u_{1}
is
G^{F}
-conjugate to

u
. The lemma is proved.
∎

The purpose of this paper is to prove that the following conjecture
holds for a certain choice of
\Xi_{G}
,
possibly except one class in
E_{7}
.
Note that the conjecture implies that the generalized Green
functions
Q^{G}_{L^{w},C_{0}^{w},\mathscr{E}_{0}^{w},\varphi_{0}^{w}}
are computable.

Conjecture 1.10.

Let
G
be a connected reductive group with Frobenius map
F
.
For an
F
-stable unipotent class
C
in
G
, there exists
a split element
u\in C^{F}
. Moreover, for any
\rho\in A_{G}(u)^{\wedge}_{\operatorname{ex}}
,
an extension
\widetilde{\rho}
of
\rho
satisfying the conditions appeared
in (a)
\sim
(g) in 1.7 is uniquely determined, which we call
the split extension of
\rho
.

1.11.

The proof of the conjecture is reduced to the case where

G
is a simply connected, almost simple group.
In the case of exceptional groups, we make use of the computation of
Green functions by
[
Spr
]
,
[
Sh2
]
,
[
BS
]
for the case
p
is good,
and by
[
G2
]
,
[
Lü
]
for the case
p
is bad.

1.12.

The main ingredient for the proof is again
a variant of the restriction theorem as in

[
Sh5
, II,III]
, which we review below.
First recall the original restriction theorem.

Under the setup in 1.1, fix
(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\in\mathscr{M}_{G}
.
Let
P
be a parabolic subgroup of
G

whose Levi subgroup is
L
. We consider another parabolic subgroup
Q\supset P
of
G

with Levi subgroup
M\supset L
and the unipotent radical
U_{Q}
.
Then
\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\prime}=N_{M}(L)/L
is in a natural way
a subgroup of
\mathscr{W}_{L}
.
Let
C
(resp.
C^{\prime}
) be a unipotent class in
G
(resp. in
M
),
and choose
u\in C,u^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}
.
Put

(1.12.1)

\displaystyle Y_{u,u^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\{g\in G\mid g^{-1}ug\in u^{\prime}U_{Q}\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{u,C^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\{gQ\in G/Q\mid g^{-1}ug\in C^{\prime}U_{Q}\}.

We have a map
q:Y_{u,u^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{Q}_{u,C^{\prime}}
by
g\mapsto gQ
.

q
is
Z_{G}(u)
-equivariant under the natural action of
Z_{G}(u)
on them.

Z_{G}(u)\times Z_{M}(u^{\prime})U_{Q}
acts on
Y_{u,u^{\prime}}
, and
we have an isomorphism

(1.12.2)

Y_{u,u^{\prime}}/Z_{M}(u^{\prime})U_{Q}\simeq\mathcal{Q}_{u,C^{\prime}},

which is compatible with the action of
Z_{G}(u)
.

Let
X_{u,u^{\prime}}
be the
set of irreducible components of
Y_{u,u^{\prime}}
with possible maximal dimension

d_{u,u^{\prime}}=(\dim Z_{G}(u)+\dim Z_{M}(u^{\prime}))/2+\dim U_{Q}
. Then

A_{G}(u)\times A_{M}(u^{\prime})
acts on
X_{u,u^{\prime}}
as a permutation of irreducible
components. Denote by
\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}
this permutation representation
of
A_{G}(u)\times A_{M}(u^{\prime})
on
X_{u,u^{\prime}}
.
We consider the generalized Springer correspondence for
M
.
Take
(C^{\prime},\mathscr{E}^{\prime})\in\mathscr{N}_{M}
with
(C^{\prime},\mathscr{E}^{\prime})\leftrightarrow(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
.
Let
V_{(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})}
be
the irreducible representation of
\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\prime}
corresponding to
(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})\in\mathscr{N}_{M}

under (1.1.4).
Then the restriction theorem (
[
Sp2
, 1.2 (II)]
) asserts that

(1.12.3)

\operatorname{\!\langle\!}\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}},\rho\otimes\rho^{\prime*}\operatorname{\!\rangle\!}_{A_{G}(u)\times A_{M}(u^{\prime})}=\operatorname{\!\langle\!}V_{(u,\rho)}|_{\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\prime}},V_{(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})}\operatorname{\!\rangle\!}_{\,\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\prime}},

if
(u,\rho)
and
(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
belong to the same series
(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})
, and
the left hand side is equal to zero if
(u,\rho)
and
(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
belong to
different series.
Moreover, every irreducible representation of

A_{G}(u)\times A_{M}(u^{\prime})
which occurs in
\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}
is obtained in this way.

1.13.

We assume that
L\subset P,M\subset Q
are all
F
-stable.
Consider
Y_{u,u^{\prime}}
in (1.12.1), and assume that
u,u^{\prime}
are
F
-stable.
Then
F
acts naturally on
Y_{u,u^{\prime}}
, and acts on
X_{u,u^{\prime}}
as a permutation
of irreducible components.
Put
A(u,u^{\prime})=A_{G}(u)\times A_{M}(u^{\prime})
, then
F
acts diagonally on
A(u,u^{\prime})
.
We define
\widetilde{A}(u,u^{\prime})=\operatorname{\!\langle\!}\tau\operatorname{\!\rangle\!}\ltimes A(u,u^{\prime})
, where

\operatorname{\!\langle\!}\tau\operatorname{\!\rangle\!}
is an infinite group generated by
\tau
,
and
\tau
acts on
A(u,u^{\prime})
via
F
.
Now
\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}
turns out to be
an
\widetilde{A}(u,u^{\prime})
-module, which we denote by
\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{u,u^{\prime}}
.
In turn, if we choose extensions
\widetilde{\rho},\widetilde{\rho}^{\prime}
of
\rho,\rho^{\prime}
, respectively,
it defines an extension of
\rho\otimes\rho^{\prime*}

to the irreducible
\widetilde{A}(u,u^{\prime})
-module
\widetilde{\rho\otimes\rho^{\prime*}}
.

For
(u,\rho)\in\mathscr{N}_{G}^{F}
, let
\sigma_{(u,\rho)}:V_{(u,\rho)}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,V_{(u,\rho)}

be the isomorphism defined by the condition that
the restriction of
\varphi
on
V_{(u,\rho)}\otimes F^{*}\operatorname{IC}(\overline{C},\mathscr{E})[\dim C]

coincides with
\sigma_{(u,\rho)}\otimes\widetilde{\psi}_{0}
, where
\widetilde{\psi}_{0}
is the isomorphism

F^{*}\operatorname{IC}(\overline{C},\mathscr{E})[\dim C]\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\operatorname{IC}(\overline{C},\mathscr{E})[\dim C]

induced from
\psi_{0}:F^{*}\mathscr{E}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\mathscr{E}
.
(Don’t confuse
\sigma_{(u,\rho)}
with the preferred extension
\sigma_{E}
for
E=V_{(u,\rho)}
.)
Similarly, for the reductive group
M
, and
(C^{\prime},\mathscr{E}^{\prime})\in\mathscr{N}_{M}^{F}
,
we obtain the map
\sigma_{(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})}:V_{(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,V_{(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})}
,
where
(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})\leftrightarrow(C^{\prime},\mathscr{E}^{\prime})
.
We can decompose
V_{(u,\rho)}
as
\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\prime}
-module,

(1.13.1)

V_{(u,\rho)}=\biggl(\bigoplus_{(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})\in\mathscr{N}_{M}^{F}}M_{\rho,\rho^{\prime}}\otimes V_{(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})}\biggl)\oplus V_{(u,\rho)}^{\prime},

where
M_{\rho,\rho^{\prime}}=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\prime}}(V_{(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})},V_{(u,\rho)}|_{\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\prime}})
is the
multiplicity space for
V_{(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})}
, and
V^{\prime}_{(u,\rho)}
is a sum of
V_{(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})}

such that
V_{(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})}\notin(\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\prime})^{\wedge}_{\operatorname{ex}}
.
Thus we can define an isomorphism
\sigma_{\rho,\rho^{\prime}}:M_{\rho,\rho^{\prime}}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,M_{\rho,\rho^{\prime}}

such that the restriction of
\sigma_{(u,\rho)}
on
M_{\rho,\rho^{\prime}}\otimes V_{(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})}

coincides with
\sigma_{\rho,\rho^{\prime}}\otimes\sigma_{(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})}
.

A variant of the restriction theorem (1.12.3)
was proved in
[
Sh5
, II, Cor. 1.9]
.
Under the notation above, the following formula holds.

(1.13.2)

\operatorname{Tr}\,(\sigma_{\rho,\rho^{\prime}},M_{\rho,\rho^{\prime}})=q^{-(\dim C-\dim C^{\prime})/2+\dim U_{Q}}\operatorname{\!\langle\!}\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{u,u^{\prime}},\widetilde{\rho\otimes\rho^{\prime*}}\operatorname{\!\rangle\!}_{A(u,u^{\prime})\tau},

where for representations
V_{1},V_{2}
of
\widetilde{A}(u,u^{\prime})
,

\operatorname{\!\langle\!}V_{1},V_{2}\operatorname{\!\rangle\!}_{A(u,u^{\prime})\tau}
is defined as

\operatorname{\!\langle\!}V_{1},V_{2}\operatorname{\!\rangle\!}_{A(u,u^{\prime})\tau}=|A(u,u^{\prime})|^{-1}\sum_{a\in A(u,u^{\prime})}\operatorname{Tr}\,(a\tau,V_{1})\operatorname{Tr}\,((a\tau)^{-1},V_{2}).

1.14.

In order to obtain an information on
\gamma
, we apply the restriction formula (1.13.2).
Here we consider
F
-stable Levi subgroups
L\subset M\subset G
as in 1.13.
Consider
(u,\rho)\in\mathscr{N}_{G}^{F}
and
(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})\in\mathscr{N}_{M}^{F}
with

u\in C^{F},u^{\prime}\in C^{\prime F}
.
Take
(u_{0},\rho_{0})\in\mathscr{N}_{L}^{F}
with
u_{0}\in C_{0}^{F}
, and fix an extension

\widetilde{\rho}_{0}
of
\rho_{0}
. Assume that an extension

\widetilde{\rho}^{\prime}
of
\rho^{\prime}
is fixed.
Assume that
\rho\otimes\rho^{\prime*}
appears in the decomposition of
\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}
.
Let
\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}(\rho,\rho^{\prime})
be the
\rho\otimes\rho^{\prime*}
isotypic component of
the
A(u,u^{\prime})
-module
\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}
. Then
F
acts naturally on
\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}(\rho,\rho^{\prime})
,
and we regard it as a submodule of
\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{u,u^{\prime}}
.
Assume that
F
acts trivially on
\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}(\rho,\rho^{\prime})
.
Then there exists a unique extension
\widetilde{\rho}
of
\rho

with
\widetilde{\rho\otimes\rho^{\prime*}}=\widetilde{\rho}\otimes\widetilde{\rho}^{\prime*}
such that

(1.14.1)

\operatorname{\!\langle\!}\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{u,u^{\prime}},\widetilde{\rho}\otimes\widetilde{\rho}^{\prime*}\operatorname{\!\rangle\!}_{A(u,u^{\prime})\tau}=\operatorname{\!\langle\!}\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}},\rho\otimes\rho^{\prime*}\operatorname{\!\rangle\!}_{A(u,u^{\prime})}.

On the other hand, let
\widetilde{V}_{(u,\rho)}
(resp.
\widetilde{V}_{(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})}
) be the
preferred extension of
V_{(u,\rho)}
(resp.
V_{(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})}
). We consider
the following condition.

(1.14.2) Let
V_{\rho,\rho^{\prime}}=M_{\rho,\rho^{\prime}}\otimes V_{(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})}
be the

V_{(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})}
-component of
V_{(u,\rho)}
as in (1.13.1). Then the restriction
of
\widetilde{V}_{(u,\rho)}
on
V_{\rho,\rho^{\prime}}
coincides with
\delta\otimes\widetilde{V}_{(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})}
,
where
\delta
is a scalar multiplication by a
c
-th root of unity for
c
as in 1.6.

The following lemma is a refinement of Lemma 4.6 in
[
Sh5
, II]
.

Lemma 1.15
.

Assume that
\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u^{\prime},\widetilde{\rho}^{\prime})=\nu^{\prime}
.
Under the notation of 1.14, assume that
F
acts trivially on

\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}(\rho,\rho^{\prime})
. Let
\widetilde{\rho}
be the extension of
\rho
which
satisfies the condition in (1.14.1).
Then (1.14.2) holds with respect to
V_{(u,\rho)}
and
V_{(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})}
,
and we have
\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u,\widetilde{\rho})=\delta^{-1}\nu^{\prime}
.

Proof.

By (1.13.2) and (1.14.1), we have

(1.15.1)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Tr}\,(\sigma_{\rho,\rho^{\prime}},M_{\rho,\rho^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=q^{-(\dim C-\dim C^{\prime})/2+\dim U_{Q}}\operatorname{\!\langle\!}\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}},\rho\otimes\rho^{\prime*}\operatorname{\!\rangle\!}_{A(u,u^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=q^{-(\dim C-\dim C^{\prime})/2+\dim U_{Q}}\dim M_{\rho,\rho^{\prime}}.

The second equality follows from the original restriction formula (1.12.3).
Since
q^{(\dim C-\dim C^{\prime})/2-\dim U_{Q}}\sigma_{\rho,\rho^{\prime}}
is an automorphism
of finite order on
M_{\rho,\rho^{\prime}}
, (1.15.1) implies that

\sigma_{\rho,\rho^{\prime}}
coincides with the scalar multiplication by

q^{-(\dim C-\dim C^{\prime})/2+\dim U_{Q}}
.
This implies that (1.14.2) holds.

By our assumption, we have
\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u^{\prime},\widetilde{\rho}^{\prime})=\nu^{\prime}
. It follows that

q^{-(a_{0}^{\prime}+r^{\prime})/2}\sigma_{(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})}
gives the preferred extension of
V_{(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})}
,
multiplied by
\nu^{\prime}
, where
a_{0}^{\prime},r^{\prime}
are
defined similarly to (1.4.1) with respect to
C^{\prime}\subset M
.
This implies, by (1.14.2),
that the restriction of
q^{-(a_{0}+r)/2}\sigma_{(u,\rho)}
on

V_{\rho,\rho^{\prime}}
coincides with (the restriction of)
the preferred extension of
V_{(u,\rho)}
, multiplied by
\delta^{-1}\nu^{\prime}
.
Hence
q^{-(a_{0}+r)/2}\sigma_{(u,\rho)}

is the preferred extension of
V_{(u,\rho)}
, multiplied by
\delta^{-1}\nu^{\prime}
,
and so
\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u,\widetilde{\rho})=\delta^{-1}\nu^{\prime}
as asserted.
∎

1.16.

Here we explain more precisely Lusztig’s algorithm in
[
L3
, V]
of expressing
the function
X_{i}
in terms of
Y_{j}
. Under the notation in 1.4,
write
X_{i}
as a linear combination of
Y_{j}
for
j\in I^{F}
as

(1.16.1)

X_{i}=\sum_{j\in I^{F}}p_{ji}Y_{j},\quad(p_{ij}\in\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{l}).

For
i,j\in I^{F}
, we define
\omega_{ij}
as follows.
If
i=(C,\mathscr{E}),j=(C^{\prime},\mathscr{E}^{\prime})
belong to the same series
(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})
,
put

(1.16.2)

\displaystyle\omega_{ij}

\displaystyle=|\mathscr{W}_{L}|^{-1}\sum_{w\in\mathscr{W}_{L}}\operatorname{Tr}\,((w\sigma_{E_{i}})^{-1},\widetilde{E}_{i})\operatorname{Tr}\,(w\sigma_{E_{j}},\widetilde{E}_{j})

\displaystyle\qquad\times\frac{|G^{F}|}{|Z^{0F}_{L^{w}}|}q^{-\dim G}q^{-(a_{0}+a_{0}^{\prime})/2},

where
a_{0}=-\dim C-\dim Z_{L}
,
a_{0}^{\prime}=-\dim C^{\prime}-\dim Z_{L}
.
Furthermore,
E_{i}=V_{(C,\mathscr{E})}\in(\mathscr{W}_{L})^{\wedge}_{\operatorname{ex}}
,
and
\widetilde{E}_{i}
is the preferred extension of
E_{i}
.
We put
\omega_{ij}=0
if
i
and
j
belong to different series.
We define matrices

P=(p_{ij}),\ \Omega=(\omega_{ij})
indexed by
I^{F}
.
It is proved in
[
L3
, V, Thm. 24.4]
the matrices
P
and
\Omega

satisfy the matrix equation,

(1.16.3)

{}^{t}P\varLambda P=\Omega

for a certain matrix
\varLambda=(\lambda_{ij})
.
Moreover, for a suitable choice of the total order on
I^{F}
,

P
turns out to be a block-wise upper triangular matrix where the diagonal blocks
are identity matrices, and
\varLambda
is a non-singular, block-wise diagonal matrix.
Here
\Omega=(\omega_{ij})
is computable if we know the character table of
\widetilde{\mathscr{W}}_{L}
.
Then the matrices
P
and
\Omega
are determined uniquely from the matrix equation
(1.16.3).

Also it was proved in [loc. cit.] that
p_{ij}=0
if
i
and
j
belong to
different series in
\mathscr{M}_{G}
.
Thus if we denote by
I_{\eta}

a subset of
I
consisting of
i
belonging to a fixed
\eta=(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\in\mathscr{M}_{G}^{F}
,
and consider the submatrices
P_{\eta},\varLambda_{\eta},\Omega_{\eta}
indexed by
I^{F}_{\eta}
,
then the matrix equation (1.16.3) can be rewritten more effectively as

(1.16.4)

{}^{t}P_{\eta}\varLambda_{\eta}P_{\eta}=\Omega_{\eta},\qquad(\eta\in\mathscr{M}_{G}^{F}).

1.17.

Assume that
G=SL_{n}
or a simply connected group of type
E_{6}
.
We consider a non-split Frobenius map
F
on
G
.
Let
(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\in\mathscr{M}_{G}
.
In addition to
\mathscr{W}_{L}=W
for
L=T
, we need to consider
\mathscr{W}_{L}\simeq S_{m}

for some
m\geq 1
if
G=SL_{n}
. While in the case
G=E_{6}
,
L

is of type
D_{4}
(
p=2
) or
2A_{2}
(
p\neq 3
), and
\mathscr{W}_{L}\simeq S_{3}
or
isomorphic to the Weyl group
W(G_{2})
of type
G_{2}
, respectively, for
L\neq T
.
In any case
L
is
F
-stable, and

F
induces a non-trivial action on
\mathscr{W}_{L}\simeq S_{m}
, and the trivial action on

\mathscr{W}_{L}\simeq W(G_{2})
.

We also consider another group
G_{0}
of the same type as
G
, but
G_{0}
has
an
\mathbf{F}_{q_{0}}
-structure with a split Frobenius map
F_{0}
, where
q_{0}
is a power
of a prime number
p_{0}
, and
p\neq p_{0}
in general.
We compare the matrix equations (1.16.4)
for
(G,F)
and
(G_{0},F_{0})
.
We assume that

(1.17.1) There exists an embedding
(Z_{G}^{\wedge})^{F}\hookrightarrow(Z_{G_{0}}^{\wedge})^{F_{0}}
.

Let
(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\in\mathscr{M}_{G}
.
Then
L
and
C_{0}
are both
F
and
F_{0}
-stable.
Fix a character
\chi\in(Z_{G}^{\wedge})^{F}
, then by (1.17.1),

\chi
is regarded as an
F_{0}
-stable character of
Z_{G_{0}}
.
We assume that there is a natural bijection
(\mathscr{M}_{G})_{\chi}\simeq(\mathscr{M}_{G_{0}})_{\chi}
.
We consider
\eta=(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\in(\mathscr{M}_{G})_{\chi}
.
Then
\eta
is also regarded as an element in
(\mathscr{M}_{G_{0}})_{\chi}
, and we denote
the corresponding element in
(\mathscr{M}_{G_{0}})_{\chi}
as

(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})
by using the same symbol, by abbreviation.
We assume that
(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})
is
F
and
F_{0}
-stable.

Let
w_{0}
be the longest element in
\mathscr{W}_{L}
.
Note that
|G_{0}^{F_{0}}|
and
|Z_{L_{0}^{w}}^{0F_{0}}|
are regarded as rational functions on
q_{0}
.
Here we use the following notation;
for a rational function
f=f(q_{0})
on
q_{0}
, we denote by
f(-q)

the rational function obtained from
f
by substituting
-q
for
q_{0}
.
We assume that
F
and
F_{0}

satisfy the condition that

(1.17.2)

\displaystyle|G^{F}|

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\dim T}|G_{0}^{F_{0}}|(-q),

\displaystyle|Z_{L^{ww_{0}}}^{0F}|

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\dim Z_{L}}|Z_{L_{0}^{w}}^{0F_{0}}|(-q)

\displaystyle\text{ if $\mathscr{W}_{L}\simeq S_{m}$, }

\displaystyle|Z_{L^{w}}^{0F}|

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\dim Z_{L}}|Z_{L_{0}^{w}}^{0F_{0}}|(-q)

\displaystyle\text{ if , $\mathscr{W}_{L}\simeq W(G_{2})$,}

where
T
is a maximal torus in
G
,
L_{0}^{w}
is a Levi subgroup in
G_{0}
corresponding to
a Levi subgroup
L^{w}
in
G
.
We assume further that there is a natural bijection

(\mathscr{N}_{G})_{\chi}\simeq(\mathscr{N}_{G_{0}})_{\chi}
, and
I_{\eta}^{F}\simeq(I_{0})_{\eta}^{F_{0}}
,
where
I=\mathscr{N}_{G}
and
I_{0}=\mathscr{N}_{G_{0}}
.

Let
\omega_{ij},p_{ij},\lambda_{ij}
(
i,j\in I_{\eta}^{F}
)
be the elements defined in 1.16 for
(G,F)
, and
denote by
\omega_{ij}^{0},p_{ij}^{0},\lambda_{ij}^{0}
(
i,j\in(I_{0})_{\eta}^{F_{0}}
)
the corresponding elements for
(G_{0},F_{0})
.
By (1.16.2),
\omega_{ij}^{0}
is regarded as a rational function on
q_{0}

(note that in the split case the preferred extension is trivial).
Since
p_{ij}^{0},\lambda_{ij}^{0}
are obtained from the matrix equation (1.16.3),
they are also rational functions on
q_{0}
.
Thus
\omega_{ij}^{0}(-q),p_{ij}^{0}(-q),\lambda_{ij}^{0}(-q)
can be defined.

Recall the definition
\delta_{E}
in (1.7.4). Assume that
i=(C,\mathscr{E})
with
u\in C
.
Let
E_{i}=V_{(u,\rho)}\in(\mathscr{W}_{L})^{\wedge}_{\operatorname{ex}}
for
(u,\rho)\leftrightarrow(C,\mathscr{E})
and put
\delta_{i}=\delta_{E_{i}}
.
We show the following.

Lemma 1.18
.

For
i,j\in I^{F}_{\eta}=(I_{0})^{F_{0}}_{\eta}
, we have

(i)

\omega_{ij}=(-1)^{\delta_{i}+\delta_{j}}\omega^{0}_{ij}(-q)
.

(ii)

p_{ij}=(-1)^{\delta_{i}+\delta_{j}}p_{ij}^{0}(-q)
.

(iii)

\lambda_{ij}=(-1)^{\delta_{i}+\delta_{j}}\lambda^{0}_{ij}(-q)
.

Proof.

Assuming that
\mathscr{W}_{L}\neq W(G_{2})
, we prove (i). The preferred extension
\widetilde{E}_{i}

is given by the action of
\sigma_{E_{i}}=(-1)^{a_{E_{i}}}w_{0}
on

E_{i}=V_{(u,\rho)}\in(\mathscr{W}_{L})^{\wedge}_{\operatorname{ex}}
.
Hence, for
i=(C,\mathscr{E}),j=(C^{\prime},\mathscr{E}^{\prime})
, by (1.16.2) we have

(1.18.1)

\displaystyle\omega_{ij}

\displaystyle=|\mathscr{W}_{L}|^{-1}\sum_{w\in\mathscr{W}_{L}}\operatorname{Tr}\,((ww_{0})^{-1},E_{i})\operatorname{Tr}\,(ww_{0},E_{j})

\displaystyle\qquad\times(-1)^{a_{E_{i}}+a_{E_{j}}}\frac{|G^{F}|}{|Z_{L^{w}}^{0F}|}q^{-\dim G}q^{-(a_{0}+a_{0}^{\prime})/2}

\displaystyle=|\mathscr{W}_{L}|^{-1}\sum_{w\in\mathscr{W}_{L}}\operatorname{Tr}\,(w^{-1},E_{i})\operatorname{Tr}\,(w,E_{j})

\displaystyle\qquad\times(-1)^{a_{E_{i}}+a_{E_{j}}}\frac{|G^{F}|}{|Z_{L^{ww_{0}}}^{0F}|}q^{-\dim G}q^{-(a_{0}+a_{0}^{\prime})/2},

where
a_{0}=-\dim C-\dim Z_{L},a_{0}^{\prime}=-\dim C^{\prime}-\dim Z_{L}
.
Since
a_{0}=2d_{u}-\dim G+\dim T-\dim Z_{L}
, we have

(a_{0}+a_{0}^{\prime})/2+\dim G=d_{u}+d_{u^{\prime}}+\dim T-\dim Z_{L}.

Hence by (1.17.2), the last expression in (1.18.1) is equal to

\displaystyle|\mathscr{W}_{L}|^{-1}\sum_{w\in\mathscr{W}_{L}}\operatorname{Tr}\,(w^{-1},E_{i})\operatorname{Tr}\,(w,E_{j})

\displaystyle\qquad\times(-1)^{\delta_{i}+\delta_{j}}\frac{|G_{0}^{F_{0}}|(-q)}{|Z_{L_{0}^{w}}^{0F_{0}}|(-q)}\cdot(-q)^{-\dim G_{0}}(-q)^{-(a_{0}+a_{0}^{\prime})/2}=(-1)^{\delta_{i}+\delta_{j}}\omega^{0}_{ij}(-q).

Hence we obtain the equality in (i). The case where
\mathscr{W}_{L}\simeq W(G_{2})

is dealt similarly (in this case, the preferred extensions are trivial).
Thus (i) holds.

Next we show (ii) and (iii).
Let
\Omega=(\omega_{ij}),P=(p_{ij}),\varLambda=(\lambda_{ij})
be the matrices
associated to
(G,F)
. Similarly, we define the matrices

\Omega^{0}=(\omega_{ij}^{0}),P^{0}=(p_{ij}^{0}),\varLambda^{0}=(\lambda^{0}_{ij})
associated
to
(G_{0},F_{0})
(actually we consider the matrices indexed by

I^{F}_{\eta}
or
(I_{0})^{F_{0}}_{\eta}
as in (1.16.4) for
a fixed
\eta
. But in the computation below, we omit
\eta
).
By (1.16.4), those matrices satisfy the relations

{}^{t}P\varLambda P=\Omega,\qquad{}^{t}P^{0}\varLambda^{0}P^{0}=\Omega^{0}.

Define a diagonal matrix
D
by
D=((-1)^{\delta_{i}})
.
Then
D^{2}=I
, and by (i) we have

\Omega^{0}(-q)=D\Omega D
. It follows that

{}^{t}(DPD)(D\varLambda D)(DPD)=\Omega^{0}(-q)={}^{t}P^{0}(-q)\varLambda^{0}(-q)P^{0}(-q).

Here
DPD
is a block-wise upper triangular matrix, where the diagonal blocks
are identity matrices, and
D\varLambda D
is a
block-wise diagonal matrix. Hence by the uniqueness of the matrix equation (1.16.4),
we have
DPD=P^{0}(-q)
, and
D\varLambda D=\varLambda^{0}(-q)
. This proves (ii) and (iii).
∎

2.
Special linear groups

2.1.

In this section, we assume that
G
is a special linear group.
We prove the existence of split elements for
G
.
Actually, in the case where
F
is split, split elements were
explicitly constructed in
[
Sh5
, I, Thm. 3.4]
.
In the case where
F
is non-split, they were constructed
in
[
Sh5
, I, Thm. 4.4]
, under the assumption that
p
is large enough.
After that, for any
p
in the non-split
F
case,
split elements were constructed in
[
Sh5
, III, Thm. 4.13]
, in a recursive
way by making use of the restriction formula such as Lemma 1.15.
But the proof contains some error. Although the recursive process is
done through the procedures (I), (II), (III) in
[
Sh5
, III, 4.5]
,
there exists a case in (III) where Lemma 1.16 cannot be applied.

The simplest example is as follows. We consider
G=SL_{3}
, and
let
W=S_{3}
be the Weyl group of
G
, and
\sigma:W\to W
the automorphism
of oder 2. The
\sigma
-stable subgroup
W^{\prime}=\{1\}
corresponds to
the Weyl group of
M=T
.
Let
\lambda=(12)
be a partition of 3, and let

C=C_{\lambda}
the corresponding unipotent class in
G
. Take
(u,\rho)\leftrightarrow(C,\mathscr{E})

and consider
E=V_{(u,\rho)}\in S_{3}^{\wedge}
, which is an irreducible representation of
degree 2. We consider the class
C^{\prime}=\{1\}
, and
(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})=(1,1)\in\mathscr{N}_{M}
.
Let
\sigma_{E}:\widetilde{E}\to\widetilde{E}
be the preferred extension of
E
.
Then
\operatorname{Tr}\,(\sigma_{E},E)=0
, and
\sigma_{E}
acts non-trivially on
M_{\rho,\rho^{\prime}}=E

(in the notation of 1.14). In this case, the formula such as (1.14.1) does not
give any information for
\widetilde{\rho\otimes\rho^{\prime*}}
in
\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{u,u^{\prime}}
.

2.2.

In the discussion below, by expanding the method in
[
Sh5
, I, §3]
, we prove
the existence of split elements for any
p
, in a simultaneous way for the case where

F
is split or non-split. In particular, in the non-split case, split
elements are given by an exact formula, not as in the recursive formula in

[
Sh5
, III, §4]
.

Assume that
G=SL_{n}=SL(V)
, with
F
split or non-split.
The unipotent classes of
G
are parametrized by partitions of
n
.
We denote by
C_{\lambda}
the class corresponding to the partition

\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{r})
of
n
with
0\leq\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}\leq\cdots\leq\lambda_{r}
.
Let
n^{\prime}
be the largest divisor of
n
which is prime to
p
, and

n^{\prime}_{\lambda}
the greatest common divisor of
n^{\prime},\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{r}
.
Then
Z_{G}
is a cyclic group of order
n^{\prime}
, and
A_{G}(u)
is a cyclic group
of order
n^{\prime}_{\lambda}
. The natural map
\pi:Z_{G}\to A_{G}(u)
is given by
the surjective map
\mathbf{Z}_{n^{\prime}}\to\mathbf{Z}_{n^{\prime}_{\lambda}}
.

The generalized Springer correspondence (1.2.1) pertaining to
\chi\in Z_{G}^{\wedge}

is given as follows (see
[
LS2
, §5]
).
Let
A_{G}(u)^{\wedge}_{\chi}
be as in 1.2 for
\chi\in Z_{G}^{\wedge}
. Then
for
\chi
of order
d
,
|A_{G}(u)^{\wedge}_{\chi}|=1
if
\lambda_{i}
is divisible by
d

for any
i
, and is 0 otherwise.
In the former case,
A_{G}(u)^{\wedge}_{\chi}
consists of
\rho\in A_{G}(u)^{\wedge}
such that
the pullback of
\pi^{*}(\rho)
of
\rho\in A_{G}(u)^{\wedge}
coincides with
\chi
.
Let
(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\in\mathscr{M}_{\chi}
. Then
L
is a Levi subgroup of type

A_{d-1}+\cdots+A_{d-1}
with
m=n/d
factors, and
C_{0}
is the regular
unipotent class in
L
. If
(u_{0},\rho_{0})\leftrightarrow(C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})
, then

\rho_{0}
is the unique element contained in
A_{L}(u_{0})^{\wedge}_{\chi}
.
In particular,
\mathscr{M}_{\chi}
is a one-point set.
In this case,
\mathscr{W}_{L}\simeq S_{m}
, and
V_{(u,\rho)}
coincides with
the irreducible representation of
S_{m}
of type
\mu=(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{r})

with
\mu_{i}=\lambda_{i}/d
.

2.3.

Any unipotent class
C
in
G
is
F
-stable. For
(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\in\mathscr{M}_{G}
,
we may assume that
L
is an
F
-stable Levi subgroup of an
F
-stable parabolic
subgroup of
G
, and that
C_{0}
is
F
-stable. For each
C=C_{\lambda}
,
we shall define a “split”element
u=u_{\lambda}\in C^{F}
as follows.
First assume that
F
is split. We fix an
F
-stable basis
e_{1},\dots,e_{n}

of
V
, up to
SL(V)
-conjugate. We define
u=u_{\lambda}\in C^{F}
by
using the Jordan basis
\{v_{k,j}\mid 1\leq k\leq r,1\leq j\leq\lambda_{k}\}
of
V

such that
(u-1)v_{k,j}=v_{k,j-1}
for
j\geq 2
, and
(u-1)v_{k,1}=0
for
j=1
.
Here we assume that the Jordan basis is obtained from the basis
e_{1},\dots,e_{n}

in such a way that
v_{1,1},\dots,v_{1,\lambda_{1}},v_{2,1},\dots,v_{2,\lambda_{2}},\dots

coincides with
e_{1},\dots,e_{n}
in this order.

Next assume that
F
is non-split with respect to the
\mathbf{F}_{q}
-structure of
G
.
Then
F^{2}
is a split Frobenius map with respect to the
\mathbf{F}_{q^{2}}
-structure.
Let
e_{1},\dots,e_{n}
be the
\mathbf{F}_{q^{2}}
-basis of
V
, and
V_{0}
be the
\mathbf{F}_{q^{2}}
-subspace
of
V
spanned by
e_{1},\dots,e_{n}
.
Define
u_{\lambda}\in C^{F^{2}}
by applying the above discussion to the split Frobenius
F^{2}
.
We define a sesquilinear form
(\ ,\ )
on
V_{0}
as in (4.7.1) in
[
Sh5
, III]
by

(2.3.1)

(v_{k,j},v_{k^{\prime},j^{\prime}})=\begin{cases}(-1)^{j+a_{k}}&\quad\text{ if }k=k^{\prime},j+j^{\prime}=\lambda_{k},\\
0&\quad\text{ otherwise, }\end{cases}

where
a_{k}=\pm 1
. Then
u_{\lambda}
leaves the form
(\ ,\ )
invariant, and
we have
u_{\lambda}\in C^{F}
with respect to the
\mathbf{F}_{q}
-structure induced from
(\ ,\ )
.
In later discussion, we shall prove that
u_{\lambda}\in C^{F}
actually gives a split element,
under a suitable choice of
\Xi_{G}
(see (1.7.1)).

For
F
: split or non-split, let
P=LU_{P}
be an
F
-stable parabolic subgroup of
G
such that

L
is an
F
-stable Levi subgroup of type
A_{d-1}+\cdots+A_{d-1}

with
(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\in\mathscr{M}_{G}^{F}
.
We define
u^{\prime}_{\lambda}\in C_{0}^{F}
as the projection of
u_{\lambda}\in P^{F}
to
L^{F}
. Explicitly,

u^{\prime}_{\lambda}
is defined by the condition that

(2.3.2)

(u^{\prime}_{\lambda}-1)v_{k,j}=\begin{cases}0&\quad\text{ if }j\equiv 1\pmod{d},\\
v_{k,j-1}&\quad\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

By applying the procedures (e) and (f) in 1.7, one can define
a “split” element
u_{0}\in C_{0}^{F}
, starting from the split element
u^{\prime}\in C^{\prime F}
, where

C^{\prime}
is the regular unipotent class in
SL_{d}
.
Then
u_{0}
coincides with
u^{\prime}_{\lambda}\in C_{0}^{F}
for
\lambda=(n)
, i.e,
u_{\lambda}

is a regular unipotent element in
G^{F}
.

Assume that
F
is non-split.
Assume that
(u_{0},\rho_{0})\leftrightarrow(C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})
.
We have
A_{L}(u_{0})\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{d^{\prime}}
, where
d^{\prime}
is the largest divisor of
d

which is prime to
p
. We consider the extended group
\widetilde{A}_{L}(u_{0})
, where

F
acts on
A_{L}(u_{0})
via
\tau_{0}
.
We choose an isomorphism
\varphi_{0}:F^{*}\mathscr{E}_{0}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\mathscr{E}_{0}

in
\Xi_{G}
as the trivial extension
\widetilde{\rho}_{0}
of

\rho_{0}\in A_{L}(u_{0})^{\wedge}_{\operatorname{ex}}
(note that
A_{L}(u_{0})
is abelian).
For a given
u_{\lambda}\in C^{F}
, let
u^{\prime}_{\lambda}\in C_{0}^{F}
be as above.
Then
u^{\prime}_{\lambda}
is obtained from
u_{0}
by twisting with
c_{\lambda}\in A_{L}(u_{0})
, where

c_{\lambda}\in A_{L}(u_{0})
is determined uniquely, up to
F
-conjugacy.
Since
\rho_{0}\in A_{L}(u_{0})_{\operatorname{ex}}^{\wedge}
,
\rho_{0}(c_{\lambda})=\widetilde{\rho}_{0}(c_{\lambda}\tau_{0})

is determined uniquely, which we denote
by
\nu_{\lambda}=\nu_{\lambda,\eta}
for
\eta=(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\in\mathscr{M}_{G}
.

In the case where
F
is split, for a given
u_{\lambda}\in C^{F}
,
u^{\prime}_{\lambda}\in C_{0}^{F}

is defined similarly to the non-split case. In that case, we have
c_{\lambda}=1
and

\nu_{\lambda,\eta}=1
for any
\lambda
.

Remark 2.4.

The elements
u_{\lambda},u^{\prime}_{\lambda}
and
u_{0}
defined in 2.2 correspond
to
y_{\lambda},y_{1}
and
y_{0}
in
[
Sh5
, I, 4.2]
, and
c_{\lambda},\nu_{\lambda,\eta}

correspond to
c_{1},\eta_{\lambda}
there.

2.5.

Here we recall the construction of the complex
K
on
G
given in 1.1
more precisely. For
(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\in\mathscr{M}_{G}
,
put
\varSigma=Z_{L}^{0}\times C_{0}\subset L
, and let
\overline{\varSigma}
be the closure

\varSigma
in
L
. Put

\displaystyle\widetilde{X}

\displaystyle=\{(x,gP)\in G\times G/P\mid g^{-1}xg\in\overline{\varSigma}U_{P}\},

\displaystyle\widetilde{X}_{0}

\displaystyle=\{(x,gP)\in G\times G/P\mid g^{-1}xg\in\varSigma U_{P}\},

\displaystyle\widehat{X}_{0}

\displaystyle=\{(x,g)\in G\times G\mid g^{-1}xg\in\varSigma U_{P}\},

\displaystyle\overline{Y}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{g\in G}g(\overline{\varSigma}U_{P})g^{-1},

and consider the diagram

\begin{CD}\varSigma @<{\varphi}<{}<\widehat{X}_{0}@>{\psi}>{}>\widetilde{X}_{0}\end{CD}

defined by
\varphi:(x,g)\mapsto\text{ projection of $g^{-1}xg$ to $\varSigma$}
,

\psi:(x,g)\mapsto(x,gP)
.
We define a map
\phi:\widetilde{X}\to\overline{Y}
by
(x,gP)\mapsto x
. Then
\phi
is
a proper map.
Let
\mathscr{E}_{1}=\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{l}\boxtimes\mathscr{E}_{0}
be the
L
-equivariant local system on
\varSigma
.
Then there exists a local system
\overline{\mathscr{E}}_{1}
on
\widetilde{X}_{0}
such that

\varphi^{*}\mathscr{E}_{1}\simeq\psi^{*}\overline{\mathscr{E}}_{1}
. Since
\widetilde{X}_{0}
is open dense in
\widetilde{X}
,
one can consider the complex
K_{\overline{\mathscr{E}}_{1}}=\operatorname{IC}(\widetilde{X},\overline{\mathscr{E}}_{1})[\dim\widetilde{X}]
.
We define a complex
K
on
G
by
K=\phi_{*}K_{\overline{\mathscr{E}}_{1}}
.
Then
K
turns out to be a semisimple perverse sheaf on
G
, equipped with

\mathscr{W}_{L}
-action.

Under the setup in 1.4, assume that
(C,\mathscr{E})\in\mathscr{N}_{G}^{F}
belongs to
the series
(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\in\mathscr{M}_{G}^{F}
.
For
u\in C^{F}
, consider the varieties

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}_{u}

\displaystyle=\{gP\in G/P\mid g^{-1}ug\in C_{0}U_{p}\},

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathscr{P}}_{u}

\displaystyle=\{g\in G\mid g^{-1}ug\in C_{0}U_{P}\},

and the diagram

(2.5.1)

\begin{CD}C_{0}@<{\alpha}<{}<\widehat{\mathscr{P}}_{u}@>{\beta}>{}>\mathscr{P}_{u},\end{CD}

where
\alpha
is defined by
g\mapsto
projection of
g^{-1}ug
on
C_{0}
,
and
\beta:g\mapsto gP
.

Let
K
be as above.
Let
\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0}
be the local system on
\mathscr{P}_{u}
defined by
the condition that
\alpha^{*}\mathscr{E}_{0}\simeq\beta^{*}\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0}
.
Then
\mathscr{H}^{a}_{u}K\simeq H^{a}(\overline{\mathscr{P}}_{u},D)
, where

\overline{\mathscr{P}}_{u}=\{gP\in G/P\mid g^{-1}ug\in\overline{C}_{0}U_{P}\}
, and

D
is the restriction of
K_{\overline{\mathscr{E}}_{1}}
on
\overline{\mathscr{P}}_{u}
.
It is known that
\operatorname{IC}(\overline{C}_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})
is clean, namely,

\operatorname{IC}(\overline{C}_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})
is
\mathscr{E}_{0}
extended by 0 on
\overline{C}_{0}-C_{0}
.
It follows that
K_{\overline{\mathscr{E}}_{1}}
coincides with
\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0}[r]
extended by 0
on
\overline{\mathscr{P}}_{u}-\mathscr{P}_{u}
, where
r=\dim Y
.
We obtain (see
[
L3
, V, (24.2.5)]
) that

(2.5.2)

\mathscr{H}^{a}_{u}K\simeq H^{a+r}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{u},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0}).

Since
\mathscr{H}^{a}_{u}K
has a structure of
\mathscr{W}_{L}
-module,
H^{a+r}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{u},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0})

has a structure of
\mathscr{W}_{L}
-module.
It is known by
[
L2
, 1.2 (b)]
that
\dim\mathscr{P}_{u}\leq(a_{0}+r)/2
.
Since
\mathscr{H}^{a_{0}}_{u}K\neq 0
, we see that

(2.5.3)

\dim\mathscr{P}_{u}=(a_{0}+r)/2.

The isomorphism
\varphi_{0}:F^{*}\mathscr{E}_{0}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\mathscr{E}_{0}
induces an isomorphism

\dot{\varphi}_{0}:F^{*}\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0}
. Then
\dot{\varphi}_{0}
induces an
action
\Phi
on
H_{c}^{a+r}(\mathscr{P}_{u},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0})
. In the case where
a=a_{0}
,

\Phi
is a scalar multiplication

q^{(a_{0}+r)/2}
times a map of finite order.

2.6.

We apply the discussion in 2.5 for
G=SL_{n}
, where

L
is of type
A_{d-1}+\cdots+A_{d-1}
with
m=n/d
-factors, and

C_{0}
is the regular unipotent class in
L
. For the moment, we don’t
consider the
\mathbf{F}_{q}
-structure.
Let
u\in C=C_{\lambda}
with
\lambda=d\mu
. Consider

\mathscr{P}_{u}=\{gP\in G/P\mid g^{-1}ug\in C_{0}U_{P}\}
. We also
consider
\mathscr{P}_{u_{0}}
by regarding
u_{0}\in C_{0}
as an element in
G

(of Jordan type
(d,\dots,d)
,
m
copies).
Let
\mathscr{F}
be the set of flags
D=(V_{d}\subset V_{2d}\subset\cdots\subset V_{(m-1)d})

with
\dim V_{kd}=kd
. Then
\mathscr{F}\simeq G/P
.
For
u\in C
as above, let
\mathscr{F}_{u}
be the set of
u
-stable flags
such that
u
induces a regular unipotent transformation on
V_{kd}/V_{(k-1)d}
.
Then
\mathscr{F}_{u}\simeq\mathscr{P}_{u}
.

Let
\mathscr{G}
be the set of subspaces
W
of
\dim W=d
. We have a natural map

\pi:\mathscr{F}\to\mathscr{G}
by
D=(V_{d},\dots,V_{(m-1)d})\mapsto V_{d}
.
Let
\mathscr{G}_{u}
be the set of
W\in\mathscr{G}
such that
W
is
u
-stable, and that

u|_{W}
is regular unipotent. Then
\pi
induces a surjective map

\pi:\mathscr{F}_{u}\to\mathscr{G}_{u}
.
Now
\mathscr{G}_{u}
can be identified with the variety
\mathbf{P}(\operatorname{Ker}x^{d})-\mathbf{P}(\operatorname{Ker}x^{d-1})

for
x=u-1
; for each
v\in\operatorname{Ker}x^{d}-\operatorname{Ker}x^{d-1}
, the space spanned by

v,xv,\dots x^{d-1}v
gives an element
V_{d}
in
\mathscr{G}_{u}
.

We fix
V_{d}\in\mathscr{G}_{u}
, and put
\overline{V}=V/V_{d}
. Let
\overline{u}\in SL(\overline{V})

be the element induced from
u\in SL(V)
. Then we have a natural isomorphism

(2.6.1)

\pi^{-1}(V_{d})\simeq\overline{\mathscr{P}}_{\overline{u}},

where
\overline{\mathscr{P}}_{\overline{u}}
is the variety defined for
\overline{u}\in SL(\overline{V})
.

Write
\lambda
as
\lambda=(a_{1}^{m_{1}},a_{2}^{m_{2}},\dots a_{h}^{m_{h}})
, with

0<a_{1}<a_{2}<\cdots<a_{h}
, where
m_{i}=\sharp\{k\mid\lambda_{k}=a_{i}\}
.
Put
W=\operatorname{Ker}x^{d}
and define, for
i=1,\dots,h
,
a subspace
W^{i}
of
W
by
W^{i}=(\operatorname{Ker}x^{d}\cap\operatorname{Im}x^{a_{i}-1})+\operatorname{Ker}x^{d-1}
.
Then we have a filtration of
W
,

W=W^{h}\supsetneq W^{h-1}\supsetneq\cdots\supsetneq W^{1}\supsetneq W^{0}=\operatorname{Ker}x^{d-1}.

Here we have
\dim W^{i}/W^{i-1}=m_{i}
for each
i
.
The following fact is easily checked.

(2.6.2) For each
v\in W^{i}-\operatorname{Ker}x^{d-1}
, define
V_{d}\in\mathscr{G}_{u}

as above. Then
\overline{u}\in SL(\overline{V})
has Jordan type
\lambda^{\prime}
,
where
\lambda^{\prime}
is a partition of
n-d
given by

\lambda^{\prime}=(a_{1}^{m_{1}},\dots,a_{i}-d,a_{i}^{m_{i}-1},\dots,a_{h}^{m_{h}}).

Let
\{v_{k,j}\}
be the Jordan basis of
V

associated to
u
.
Then the image of
\{v_{k,d}\mid 1\leq k\leq r\}
gives a basis of
\operatorname{Ker}x^{d}/\operatorname{Ker}x^{d-1}
,
where
r=\sum_{1\leq i\leq h}m_{i}=\dim\operatorname{Ker}x^{d}/\operatorname{Ker}x^{d-1}
.
For each
1\leq k\leq h
, define a subset
Z_{k}
of
W
by

Z_{k}=\{v_{k,d}+\sum_{s<k}b_{s}v_{s,d}\mid b_{s}\in\mathbf{k}\}\simeq\mathbf{A}^{k-1}.

Then
Z_{k}
is a locally closed subvariety of
\mathbf{P}(W)
, and

(2.6.3)

\mathbf{P}(W^{i})-\mathbf{P}(W^{i-1})=\bigsqcup_{k=m_{1}+\cdots+m_{i-1}+1}^{m_{1}+\cdots+m_{i}}Z_{k}

gives a paving of
\mathbf{P}(W^{i})-\mathbf{P}(W^{i-1})
by affine spaces.
The following result can be proved in a similar way as in Spaltenstein
[
Sp1
]
,
which corresponds to the case where
G=GL(V),d=1
, and
\mathscr{P}_{u}=\mathscr{B}_{u}
.

Lemma 2.7
.

We regard
Z_{k}
as a subvariety of
\mathscr{G}_{u}
. Let
V_{d}
be an element in
\mathscr{G}_{u}

generated by
v_{k,d}\ (1\leq k\leq r)
, and
\overline{u}
the element
in
SL(\overline{V})
for
\overline{V}=V/V_{d}
.
Then we have

(2.7.1)

\pi^{-1}(Z_{k})\simeq Z_{k}\times\pi^{-1}(V_{d})\simeq\mathbf{A}^{k-1}\times\overline{\mathscr{P}}_{\overline{u}}.

2.8.

If
D=(V_{d},\dots,V_{(m-1)d})\in\mathscr{F}_{u}
,
then
u_{k}=u|_{V_{kd}/V_{(k-1)d}}
induces a regular transformation
on
\overline{V}_{kd}=V_{kd}/V_{(k-1)d}
. Thus we can define a regular element

(u_{1},\dots,u_{m})
in
L
, which is conjugate to
u_{0}\in C_{0}
. It follows that

(2.8.1)

\mathscr{F}_{u}\subset\mathscr{F}_{u_{0}}.

We define a specific representative
u^{\prime}_{0}\in C_{0}
attached to
u
by

(2.8.2)

(u^{\prime}_{0}-1)v_{k,j}=\begin{cases}0&\quad\text{ if }j\equiv 1\mod d,\\
v_{k,j-1}&\quad\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

(Hence this
u^{\prime}_{0}
corresponds to
u^{\prime}_{\lambda}
for
u=u_{\lambda}
in the
notation of (2.3.2).)
We define
\mathscr{F}_{u^{\prime}_{0}}
,
\mathscr{G}_{u^{\prime}_{0}}
and
\pi_{0}:\mathscr{F}_{u^{\prime}_{0}}\to\mathscr{G}_{u^{\prime}_{0}}
,
similarly to
\mathscr{P}_{u},\mathscr{G}_{u}
, etc..
Put
x_{0}=u^{\prime}_{0}-1
. If we put
W=\operatorname{Ker}x^{d}
, and
V_{0}=\operatorname{Ker}(x_{0})^{d}
,
then we have
W\subset V_{0}
.

By (2.8.1),
\mathscr{P}_{u}
is regarded as a subvariety of
\mathscr{P}_{u^{\prime}_{0}}
.
The following result is proved as a corollary to Lemma 2.7.

Proposition 2.9
.

Let
u\in C
, and
u^{\prime}_{0}\in C_{0}
.

(i)

\mathscr{P}_{u}
has an affine paving.

(ii)

\mathscr{P}_{u_{0}^{\prime}}-\mathscr{P}_{u}
has an affine paving.

Proof.

We show (i). Since
\mathscr{G}_{u}\simeq\mathbf{P}(\operatorname{Ker}x^{d})-\mathbf{P}(\operatorname{Ker}x^{d-1})
,
by (2.6.3), we have

\mathscr{F}_{u}\simeq\bigsqcup_{k=1}^{h}\pi^{-1}(Z_{k}).

By induction, we may assume that
\overline{\mathscr{P}}_{\overline{u}}
has an affine paving.
Hence by Lemma 2.7,
\pi^{-1}(Z_{k})
has an affine paving. Thus (i) holds.

Next we show (ii). We have

\mathbf{P}(\operatorname{Ker}x^{d})-\mathbf{P}(\operatorname{Ker}x^{d-1})\subset\mathbf{P}(\operatorname{Ker}x_{0}^{d})-\mathbf{P}(\operatorname{Ker}x_{0}^{d-1}),

and so
\mathscr{G}_{u}\subset\mathscr{G}_{u^{\prime}_{0}}
. Let
X_{1}=\pi_{0}^{-1}(\mathscr{G}_{u})
, and

X_{2}=\pi_{0}^{-1}(\mathscr{G}_{u_{0}^{\prime}}-\mathscr{G}_{u})
. Since
\mathscr{P}_{u}\subset X_{1}
,
we have

\mathscr{P}_{u^{\prime}_{0}}-\mathscr{P}_{u}=(X_{1}-\mathscr{P}_{u})\sqcup X_{2}.

Here
X_{1}-\mathscr{P}_{u}=\bigsqcup_{k=1}^{h}(\pi_{0}^{-1}(Z_{k})-\pi^{-1}(Z_{k}))
, and by a similar
discussion as in Lemma 2.7, we have

(2.9.1)

\pi_{0}^{-1}(Z_{k})-\pi^{-1}(Z_{k})\simeq Z_{k}\times(\pi_{0}^{-1}(V_{d})-\pi^{-1}(V_{d})).

Since
\pi_{0}^{-1}(V_{d})-\pi^{-1}(V_{d})\simeq\overline{\mathscr{P}}_{\overline{u}^{\prime}_{0}}-\overline{\mathscr{P}}_{\overline{u}}
,
by induction and by (2.9.1),
\pi_{0}^{-1}(Z_{k})-\pi^{-1}(Z_{k})
has an affine paving.
Hence
X_{1}-\mathscr{P}_{u}
also has an affine paving.
On the other hand, by applying the discussion in the proof of Lemma 2.7 for
u^{\prime}_{0}
,
we see that
X_{2}
has an affine paving. Hence (ii) follows.
∎

2.10.

Consider the isomorphism

\pi_{0}^{-1}(Z_{k})-\pi^{-1}(Z_{k})\simeq Z_{k}\times(\overline{\mathscr{P}}_{\overline{u}^{\prime}_{0}}-\overline{\mathscr{P}}_{\overline{u}})

in (2.9.1)).
Recall that
\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0}
is the local system on
\mathscr{P}_{u}
, and let
\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0}^{\prime}

be the local system on
\overline{\mathscr{P}}_{\overline{u}}
defined similarly to
\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0}
.
Then by a similar discussion as in
[
Sh5
, I, (3.6.8)]
, we see that
the restriction of
\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0}
on
\pi_{0}^{-1}(Z_{k})-\pi^{-1}(Z_{k})
is isomorphic to

\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{l}\boxtimes\dot{\mathscr{E}}^{\prime}_{0}
. Similarly,
in the formula
\pi^{-1}(Z_{k})\simeq Z_{k}\times\overline{\mathscr{P}}_{\overline{u}}
in (2.7.1),
the restriction of
\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0}
on
\pi^{-1}(Z_{k})
is isomorphic to
\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{l}\boxtimes\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0}^{\prime}
.
(
\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{l}
is the constant sheaf on
Z_{k}\simeq\mathbf{A}^{k-1}
.)
It follows, from Proposition 2.9, that

(2.10.1)

H_{c}^{\operatorname{odd}}(\mathscr{P}_{u^{\prime}_{0}}-\mathscr{P}_{u},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0})=0,\qquad H_{c}^{\operatorname{odd}}(\mathscr{P}_{u},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0})=0.

We have the following result.

Proposition 2.11
.

Let
i:\mathscr{P}_{u}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{P}_{u^{\prime}_{0}}
be the closed immersion.

(i)

The induced map
i^{*}:H^{2a}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{u^{\prime}_{0}},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0})\to H^{2a}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{u},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0})

is surjective.

(ii)

Then map
i^{*}
is
\mathscr{W}_{L}
-equivariant.

Proof.

(i) follows from the cohomology long exact sequence obtained from
the closed immersion
\mathscr{P}_{u}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{P}_{u^{\prime}_{0}}
, by using (2.10.1).

We show (ii). Let
\mathfrak{g}
be the Lie algebra of
G
, and
\mathfrak{c}_{0}

the nilpotent orbit in
\mathfrak{g}
corresponding to
C_{0}
. We denote by
the same symbol
\mathscr{E}_{0}
the local system on
\mathfrak{c}_{0}
corresponding to

\mathscr{E}_{0}
on
C_{0}
. Let
\mathfrak{c}
be the
G
-orbit in
\mathfrak{g}
corresponding to
C
.
Take
x_{0}\in\mathfrak{c}_{0}
and
x\in\mathfrak{c}
. Let
\mathfrak{n}_{P}
be the nilpotent radical
of
\operatorname{Lie}P
. Similarly to the group case, we define varieties

\displaystyle\widetilde{X}

\displaystyle=\{(x,gP)\in\mathfrak{g}\times G/P\mid\operatorname{Ad}(g)^{-1}x\in\overline{\varSigma}+\mathfrak{n}_{P}\},

\displaystyle\overline{Y}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{g\in G}\operatorname{Ad}(g)(\overline{\varSigma}+\mathfrak{n}_{P}).

and define
\phi:\widetilde{X}\to\overline{Y}
by
(x,gP)\mapsto x
,
where
\overline{\varSigma}=\operatorname{Lie}Z_{L}\times\overline{\mathfrak{c}}_{0}
.
We can define a
\mathscr{W}_{L}
-complex
K=\phi_{*}K_{\overline{\mathscr{E}}_{1}}
on
\overline{Y}
as in the group case.
Put

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}_{x}=\{gP\in G/P\mid Ad(g)^{-1}x\in\mathfrak{c}_{0}+\mathfrak{n}_{P}\},

and similarly define
\mathscr{P}_{x_{0}}
. Let
\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0}
be the local system
on
\mathscr{P}_{x}
. Then
H^{i}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{x},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0})
has a structure of
\mathscr{W}_{L}
-module, and

H^{i}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{x},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0})\simeq H^{i}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{u},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0})
as
\mathscr{W}_{L}
-modules.
A similar property holds also for
\mathscr{P}_{x_{0}}
. Hence it is enough to show (ii)
by replacing
G
by
\mathfrak{g}
.

By Spaltenstein
[
Sp3
]
, under the condition that
p
is a good prime (hence
for any
p
in the case
G=SL_{n}
), that
there exists a 1-parameter subgroup
\lambda:\mathbf{k}^{*}\to G
and a linear subspace

\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\subset\mathfrak{g}
such that

\operatorname{Ad}(\lambda(t))x_{0}=t^{-c}x_{0}\qquad\text{ with }\quad c>0.

\operatorname{Ad}\lambda(t)
stabilizes
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
, and its weights on
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
are of the form

\xi(t)=t^{b}
with
b\geq 0
. Moreover,
\dim\boldsymbol{\Sigma}=\dim Z_{G}(x_{0})
.

Put
S=x_{0}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
. Then
S
is a transversal slice in
\mathfrak{g}
to the
G
-orbit

\mathfrak{c}_{0}
. Since
\mathfrak{c}_{0}\subset\overline{\mathfrak{c}}
,
S\cap\overline{\mathfrak{c}}
is a transversal slice
in
\overline{\mathfrak{c}}
to
\mathfrak{c}_{0}
.
Since
S\cap\overline{\mathfrak{c}}\neq\emptyset
, one can choose
x\in S
. Now
by considering the contraction to
x
by the
\mathbf{G}_{m}
-action on
S
, we have

H^{a}_{c}(S,K)\simeq\mathscr{H}^{a}_{x_{0}}K\simeq H_{c}^{a+r}(\mathscr{P}_{x_{0}},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0}),

and these isomorphisms are
\mathscr{W}_{L}
-equivariant.
On the other hand, the inclusion relation
\{x\}\hookrightarrow S
induces
a
\mathscr{W}_{L}
-equivariant map

H^{a}_{c}(S,K)\to H_{c}^{a}(\{x\},K)=\mathscr{H}^{a}_{x}K\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,H_{c}^{a+r}(\mathscr{P}_{x},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0}).

Hence we have a
\mathscr{W}_{L}
-equivariant map

H^{a+r}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{x_{0}},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0})\to H^{a+r}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{x},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0})
, which is nothing
but the map
i^{*}
in (ii). Thus (ii) is proved.
∎

2.12.

Under the setup in 2.6, we consider the
\mathbf{F}_{q}
-structure.
Assume that
F
is a Frobenius map on
G
, split or non-split type.
We choose
u=u_{\lambda}\in C^{F}
and
u_{0}^{\prime}=u_{\lambda}^{\prime}\in C_{0}^{F}
as in 2.3.
Then
\mathscr{P}_{u}
is an
F
-stable subvariety of
\mathscr{P}_{u_{0}^{\prime}}
, and
the map
