i^{*}:H^{2a}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{u_{0}^{\prime}},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0})\to H^{2a}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{u},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0})

in Proposition 2.11 is
\Phi
-equivariant.

Since
p
is good for
G
,

\mathscr{H}^{a}_{g}K
is
(a+r)
-pure for
g\in\overline{C}^{F}
and for all
a
(
[
L3
, V, (24.8.3)]
).
Hence the eigenvalues of
\Phi
on
H_{c}^{a+r}(\mathscr{P}_{u},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0})
are algebraic numbers
all of whose complex conjugate have absolute value
q^{(a+r)/2}
.
Now assume that
F
is split on
G
. Then it is possible to choose
Z_{k}
in Lemma 2.7
so that the isomorphism (2.7.1) is defined over
\mathbf{F}_{q}
. (This does not hold
if
F
is non-split.) Thus also possible to choose an affine paving of
\mathscr{P}_{u}

in Proposition 2.9 so that it is defined over
\mathbf{F}_{q}
.
In particular, the eigenvalues of
\Phi
on
H^{2a}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{u},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0})

are of the form
q^{b}
with
b\leq a
.
It follows that

Lemma 2.13
.

Assume that
F
is split. Then the eigenvalues of

\Phi
on
H_{c}^{2a}(\mathscr{P}_{u},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0})
coincide with
q^{a}
.

2.14.

Now assume that
F=F^{\prime}_{0}\sigma
is a non-split Frobenius map on
G
,
with respect to the
\mathbf{F}_{q}
-structure,
where
F^{\prime}_{0}
is a split Frobenius map, and
\sigma
is an automorphism
on
G
given by
g\mapsto n_{0}({}^{t}g^{-1})n_{0}^{-1}
;

n_{0}\in N_{G}(T)
is a representative of the longest element
in
W=N_{G}(T)/T
.
Here
Z_{G}\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{n^{\prime}}
, and
F
(resp.
F_{0}^{\prime}
) acts on
Z_{G}
via

x\mapsto x^{-q}
(resp.
x\mapsto x^{q}
) on
Z_{G}
.

We note that

(2.14.1) There exists a power
q_{0}
of a prime number
p_{0}
such that

p_{0}
is prime to
n

and that

q_{0}\equiv-q\pmod{n^{\prime}}.

In fact, since
q
is a power of
p
and
(p,n^{\prime})=1
,

-q
is prime to
n^{\prime}
. Hence by Dirichlet’s theorem on
arithmetic progression, there exists a prime number
p_{0}
such that

p_{0}\equiv-q\pmod{n^{\prime}}
. We may choose
p_{0}
large enough so that

p_{0}>n
. Hence (2.14.1) holds for
q_{0}=p_{0}
.

Let
G_{0}=SL_{n}
be a group different from
G
with a split Frobenius map
F_{0}:G_{0}\to G_{0}

with respect to an
\mathbf{F}_{q_{0}}
-structure.
We have
Z_{G}^{\wedge}\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{n^{\prime}}
and
Z_{G_{0}}^{\wedge}\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{n}
.
As an additive group
\mathbf{Z}_{n}\simeq\mathbf{Z}/n\mathbf{Z}
, we have a decomposition

\mathbf{Z}_{n}\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{n^{\prime}}\oplus\mathbf{Z}_{p^{a}}
for
n=p^{a}n^{\prime}
, and

\mathbf{Z}_{n^{\prime}}
is regarded as a
\mathbf{Z}
-submodule of
\mathbf{Z}_{n}
.

Lemma 2.15
.

The embedding
\mathbf{Z}_{n^{\prime}}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Z}_{n}
induces an embedding

(Z_{G}^{\wedge})^{F}\hookrightarrow(Z_{G_{0}}^{\wedge})^{F_{0}}
as stated in (1.17.1).

Proof.

The action of
F
on
Z_{G}^{\wedge}\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{n^{\prime}}
is given by

x\mapsto(-q)x
, and the action of
F_{0}
on
Z_{G_{0}}^{\wedge}\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{n}
is given by

x\mapsto q_{0}x
.
Hence

\mathbf{Z}_{n^{\prime}}^{F}=\{x\in\mathbf{Z}_{n^{\prime}}\mid(-q)x=x\},\qquad\mathbf{Z}_{n}^{F_{0}}=\{x\in\mathbf{Z}_{n}\mid q_{0}x=x\}.

The action of
F_{0}
on
\mathbf{Z}_{n}
preserves the decomposition

\mathbf{Z}_{n}=\mathbf{Z}_{n^{\prime}}\oplus\mathbf{Z}_{p^{a}}
.
Let
x\in\mathbf{Z}
be a representative of an element in
\mathbf{Z}_{n^{\prime}}^{F}\subset\mathbf{Z}_{n}
.
Then by (2.14.1) we have

\displaystyle q_{0}x\equiv(-q)x\equiv x\pmod{n^{\prime}},

and
x
gives an element in
\mathbf{Z}_{n^{\prime}}^{F_{0}}\subset\mathbf{Z}_{n}^{F_{0}}
.
Hence the natural inclusion
\mathbf{Z}_{n^{\prime}}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Z}_{n}
induces an embedding

\mathbf{Z}_{n^{\prime}}^{F}=\mathbf{Z}_{n^{\prime}}^{F_{0}}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Z}_{n}^{F_{0}}
.
Thus we have an embedding
(Z_{G}^{\wedge})^{F}\hookrightarrow(Z_{G_{0}}^{\wedge})^{F_{0}}
as asserted.
∎

2.16.

Let
\chi\in Z_{G}^{\wedge}
be an
F
-stable character. By Lemma 2.15,

\chi
is regarded as an
F_{0}
-stable character of
Z_{G_{0}}
.
Take
(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\in\mathscr{M}_{G}^{F}
pertaining to
\chi
.
Since
(\mathscr{M}_{G})_{\chi}
is a single class, we have

(\mathscr{M}_{G})_{\chi}\simeq(\mathscr{M}_{G_{0}})_{\chi}
.
Here
L
,
C_{0}
are both
F
and
F_{0}
-stable, and we obtain

(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\in\mathscr{M}_{G_{0}}
pertaining to
\chi
.
(Here
L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0}
are associated to

G_{0}
, but we use the same symbol by abbreviation.) We choose

u_{0}\in C_{0}^{F}
and
v_{0}\in C_{0}^{F_{0}}
as in 2.3.
Let
\rho_{0}\in A_{L}(u_{0})^{\wedge}
be the representation corresponding to
\mathscr{E}_{0}
.
Then the pullback of
\rho_{0}
coincides with
\chi
under the surjection

Z_{G}\to A_{L}(u_{0})^{\wedge}
, and we have
(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\in\mathscr{M}_{G}^{F}
.
Similarly, we have a representation
\rho_{0}^{\prime}\in A_{L}(v_{0})^{\wedge}
corresponding to

\mathscr{E}_{0}
, and
(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\in\mathscr{M}_{G_{0}}^{F_{0}}
.
We fix
\eta=(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\in(\mathscr{M}_{G})^{F}_{\chi}=(\mathscr{M}_{G_{0}})^{F_{0}}_{\chi}
.
Note that
F
and
F_{0}
satisfy the first and the second relation in (1.17.2).

We choose
u=u_{\lambda}\in C^{F}
and
u_{0}^{\prime}=u^{\prime}_{\lambda}\in C_{0}^{F}
in
G

as in 2.12. We also choose
v=v_{\lambda}\in C^{F_{0}}
and

v_{0}^{\prime}=v^{\prime}_{\lambda}\in C_{0}^{F_{0}}
in
G_{0}
.

We consider the variety
\mathscr{P}_{u_{0}^{\prime}}
, regarding
u_{0}^{\prime}
as an element in
G^{F}
.
Let
w_{0}
be the longest element in
\mathscr{W}_{L}\simeq S_{m}
. Then
F
acts on

H^{2a}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{u_{0}^{\prime}},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0})
via
\Phi
, and also we have an action of
w_{0}
on it.
We have the following result.

Lemma 2.17
.

Assume that
F
is non-split. Then eigenvalues of

\Phi w_{0}
on
H^{2a}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{u_{\lambda}^{\prime}},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0})
are equal to
\nu_{\lambda,\eta}(-q)^{a}
,
where
\nu_{\lambda,\eta}
is as in 2.3.

Proof.

By fixing
\eta\in(\mathscr{M}_{G})_{\chi}=(\mathscr{M}_{G_{0}})_{\chi}
, we put
\nu_{\lambda}=\nu_{\lambda,\eta}
.
Let
C^{\prime}
be the unipotent class of
G
containing
u_{0}^{\prime}
of type

(d,\dots,d)
. Then
A_{G}(u_{0}^{\prime})\simeq A_{L}(u_{0}^{\prime})
, and

\rho_{0}
is regarded as
\rho_{0}\in A_{G}(u_{0}^{\prime})^{\wedge}
.
We consider the functions
X_{i}
and

Y_{i}
for
i\in I_{\eta}^{F}
used in 1.17, which we denote by

X_{i,F}
and
Y_{i,F}
in order to indicate the functions on
G^{F}_{\operatorname{uni}}
.
Put
i_{0}=(u_{0},\rho_{0})\in I_{\eta}^{F}
.
We also consider similar functions on
(G_{0})^{F_{0}}_{\operatorname{uni}}
, which we denote by

X_{i,F_{0}}
and
Y_{i,F_{0}}
for
i\in(I_{0})_{\eta}^{F_{0}}
, and put

i_{0}=(v_{0},\rho_{0})\in(I_{0})_{\eta}^{F_{0}}
.
By (1.4.3), we have

\displaystyle(-1)^{a_{0}+r}\sum_{a}(-1)^{a+r}

\displaystyle\operatorname{Tr}\,(\Phi,H^{a+r}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{u^{\prime}_{\lambda}},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{E_{i}\in(\mathscr{W}_{L})^{\wedge}_{\operatorname{ex}}}\sum_{a}(-1)^{a+a_{0}}\operatorname{Tr}\,(\sigma_{E_{i}},E_{i})\operatorname{Tr}\,(\varphi_{E_{i}},\mathscr{H}^{a}_{u^{\prime}_{\lambda}}K_{E_{i}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{E_{i}}\operatorname{Tr}\,(\sigma_{E_{i}},E_{i})X_{i,F}(u^{\prime}_{\lambda})q^{(a_{0}+r)_{i}/2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{E_{i}}\operatorname{Tr}\,(\sigma_{E_{i}},E_{i})p_{i_{0},i}Y_{i_{0},F}(u^{\prime}_{\lambda})q^{(a_{0}+r)_{i}/2},

where
a_{0}+r
is with respect to
C^{\prime}
, and

(a_{0}+r)_{i}
denotes
a_{0}+r
with respect to
i=(C_{1},\mathscr{E}_{1})
.

Since
a_{0}+r
is even, this implies that

\displaystyle\sum_{a}(-1)^{a+r}\operatorname{Tr}\,(\Phi w_{0},H^{a+r}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{u^{\prime}_{\lambda}},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0}))=\sum_{E_{i}\in\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\wedge}}\operatorname{Tr}\,(\sigma_{E_{i}}w_{0},\widetilde{E}_{i})p_{i_{0},i}Y_{i_{0},F}(u^{\prime}_{\lambda})q^{(a_{0}+r)_{i}/2}.

Since
\widetilde{E}_{i}
is the preferred extension,
\sigma_{E_{i}}w_{0}
acts
on
E_{i}
as a scalar multiplication by
(-1)^{a_{E_{i}}}
.
By definition (see 2.3), we have
Y_{i_{0},F}(u^{\prime}_{\lambda})=\rho_{0}(c_{\lambda})=\nu_{\lambda}
.
Thus we have

(2.17.1)

\displaystyle\sum_{a}(-1)^{a}\operatorname{Tr}\,(\Phi w_{0},H^{a}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{u^{\prime}_{\lambda}},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0}))=\nu_{\lambda}\sum_{E_{i}\in\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\wedge}}(-1)^{a_{E_{i}}}(\dim E_{i})p_{i_{0},i}q^{(a_{0}+r)_{i}/2}.

On the other hand, we consider a similar situation for
(G_{0},F_{0})
.
Let
v^{\prime}_{\lambda}\in C^{\prime F_{0}}
be the corresponding element.
By definition in 2.3, we have
Y_{i_{0},F_{0}}(v^{\prime}_{\lambda})=Y_{i_{0},F_{0}}(v_{0})=1
.
We denote by
\Phi_{0}
the map
\Phi
on
H^{a}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{v_{0}^{\prime}},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0})
induced
from
F_{0}
on
\mathscr{P}_{v_{0}^{\prime}}
. Then by a similar discussion as above,
we have

(2.17.2)

\displaystyle\sum_{a}(-1)^{a}\operatorname{Tr}\,(\Phi_{0},H^{a}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{v^{\prime}_{\lambda}},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0}))=\sum_{E_{i}\in\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\wedge}}(\dim E_{i})p^{0}_{i_{0},i}q_{0}^{(a_{0}+r)_{i}/2}.

Now by Lemma 1.18 (ii),

p_{i_{0},i}=(-1)^{\delta_{i_{0}}+\delta_{i}}p_{i_{0},i}^{0}(-q)=(-1)^{\delta_{i}}p_{i_{0},i}^{0}(-q)

since
\delta_{i_{0}}=1
.
Moreover, since
(a_{0}+r)_{i}/2=d_{u_{1}}
for
u_{1}\in C_{1}
, we have

(-1)^{(a_{0}+r)_{i}/2}\cdot(-1)^{a_{E_{i}}}=(-1)^{\delta_{i}}
.
By comparing (2.17.1) and (2.17.2), we have

(2.17.3)

\displaystyle\sum_{a}(-1)^{a}

\displaystyle\operatorname{Tr}\,(\Phi w_{0},H^{a}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{u^{\prime}_{\lambda}},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0}))

\displaystyle=\nu_{\lambda}\bigg[\sum_{a}(-1)^{a}\operatorname{Tr}\,(\Phi_{0},H^{a}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{v^{\prime}_{\lambda}},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0}))\biggr](-q).

By (2.10.1),
H^{a}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{u_{0}^{\prime}},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0})=H^{a}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{v_{0}^{\prime}},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0})=0

if
a
is odd.
By Lemma 2.13, the eigenvalues of
\Phi_{0}
on
H^{2a}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{v_{0}^{\prime}},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0})

are equal to
q_{0}^{a}
. Then (2.17.3) implies that

(2.17.4)

\displaystyle\sum_{a}\operatorname{Tr}\,(\Phi w_{0},H^{2a}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{u^{\prime}_{\lambda}},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0}))=\nu_{\lambda}\sum_{a}\dim H^{2a}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{v^{\prime}_{\lambda}},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0})(-q)^{a}.

Note that
\dim H^{2a}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{u^{\prime}_{\lambda}},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0})=\dim H^{2a}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{v^{\prime}_{\lambda}},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0})

since
\mathscr{P}_{u^{\prime}_{\lambda}}\simeq\mathscr{P}_{v^{\prime}_{\lambda}}
, and
\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0}
on

\mathscr{P}_{u^{\prime}_{\lambda}}
and on
\mathscr{P}_{v^{\prime}_{\lambda}}

are both obtained from
\chi\in(Z_{G}^{\wedge})^{F}\hookrightarrow(Z_{G_{0}}^{\wedge})^{F_{0}}
, hence equal.
Since the absolute value of the eigenvalues
of
\Phi
on
H^{2a}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{u^{\prime}_{\lambda}},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0})
is equal to
q^{a}
(see 2.12),
the same is true also for the eigenvalues of
\Phi w_{0}
on it.
The lemma now follows from (2.17.4).
∎

We are now ready to prove the following result
which is
a generalization of Theorem 4.4 in
[
Sh5
, I]
. Theorem 4.4 was proved under the assumption
that
p
is large enough, here no restriction on
p
.

Proposition 2.18
.

Let
C=C_{\lambda}
. Take
u=u_{\lambda}\in C^{F}
and
v=v_{\lambda}\in C^{F_{0}}

the split elements defined in 2.3.
Let
a_{0}+r
be as in (1.4.2) with respect to
C
.

(i)

\Phi w_{0}
acts on
H^{a_{0}+r}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{u},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0})
as a scalar
multiplication
\nu_{\lambda}(-q)^{(a_{0}+r)/2}
.

(ii)

\Phi_{0}
acts on
H^{a_{0}+r}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{v},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0})
as a scalar
multiplication
q_{0}^{(a_{0}+r)/2}
.

Proof.

First we show (i). Let

u^{\prime}_{0}\in C_{0}^{F}
be the element as in 2.12. We consider the
varieties
\mathscr{P}_{u}
and
\mathscr{P}_{u_{0}^{\prime}}
as in 2.8.
Then we have a closed immersion
i:\mathscr{P}_{u}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{P}_{u^{\prime}_{0}}
. Here
\mathscr{P}_{u},\mathscr{P}_{u^{\prime}_{0}}

are
F
-stable, and
i
is
F
-equivariant.
By Proposition 2.11,
i^{*}:H^{2a}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{u^{\prime}_{0}},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0})\to H^{2a}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{u},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0})

is surjective, and
\mathscr{W}_{L}
-equivariant.
Hence
i^{*}
is
\Phi w_{0}
-equivariant.
By Lemma 2.17, the eigenvalues of
\Phi w_{0}
on
H^{2a}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{u^{\prime}_{0}},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0})

are
\nu_{\lambda}(-q)^{a}
.

By (2.5.3)
\dim\mathscr{P}_{u}=(a_{0}+r)/2
. It is known by the generalized Springer correspondence
that

(2.18.1)

\mathscr{H}^{a_{0}}_{u}K=H^{a_{0}+r}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{u},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0})\simeq V_{(u,\rho)}\otimes\rho

as
\mathscr{W}_{L}\times A_{G}(u)
-modules, where
V_{(u,\rho)}
is the irreducible
representation of
\mathscr{W}_{L}\simeq S_{m}
of type
\mu=\lambda/d
.
Since the action of
\Phi w_{0}
on
H^{a_{0}+r}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{u},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0})
commutes
with the action of
\mathscr{W}_{L}
,
\Phi w_{0}
acts on
H^{a_{0}+r}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{u},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0})
as
a scalar multiplication.
Thus
\Phi w_{0}
acts on
H^{a_{0}+r}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{u},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0})
as a scalar multiplication

\nu_{\lambda}(-q)^{(a_{0}+r)/2}
. This proves (i).

(ii) is proved similarly by using Lemma 2.13 instead of Lemma 2.17.
∎

As a corollary to Proposition 2.18, we have the following result, which is a generalization
of
[
Sh5
, I, Thm. 4.4]
.

Theorem 2.19
.

Let
C=C_{\lambda}
, and assume that
i=(C,\mathscr{E})\in I_{\eta}^{F}=(I_{0})_{\eta}^{F_{0}}

belongs to the series
\eta=(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\in(\mathscr{M}_{G})_{\chi}^{F}=(\mathscr{M}_{G_{0}})_{\chi}^{F_{0}}
.

(i)

Let
u=u_{\lambda}\in C^{F}
, and
u_{0}\in C_{0}^{F}
be as in 2.3.
Let
\widetilde{\rho}_{0}
be the trivial extension of
\rho_{0}\in A_{L}(u_{0})^{\wedge}
.
For each
(u,\rho)\leftrightarrow(C,\mathscr{E})
, let
\widetilde{\rho}
be the trivial extension of
\rho\in A_{G}(u)^{\wedge}
.
Then we have

\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u,\widetilde{\rho})=\nu_{\lambda,\eta}(-1)^{\delta_{i}}.

(ii)

Let
v=v_{\lambda}\in C^{F_{0}}
, and
v_{0}\in C_{0}^{F_{0}}
be
defined for
F_{0}
, similarly to
u,u_{0}
in (i).
Let
\widetilde{\rho}_{0},\widetilde{\rho}
be extensions of
\rho_{0}\in A_{L}(v_{0})^{\wedge},\rho\in A_{G}(v)^{\wedge}
,
defined similarly to (i). Then we have

\gamma(v_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},v,\widetilde{\rho})=1.

In particular,
u=u_{\lambda}\in C^{F}
,
v=v_{\lambda}\in C^{F_{0}}
are
split elements in the sense of 1.7.

Proof.

First we show (i). Take
i=(C,\mathscr{E})\in I^{F}
. Let

E=V_{(u,\rho)}\in\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\wedge}
for
(u,\rho)\leftrightarrow(C,\mathscr{E})
.
Then under the isomorphism (2.18.1),
the restriction of
\varphi:F^{*}K\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,K
on
E\otimes K_{E}
gives

\sigma_{E}\otimes\varphi_{E}
, and
\varphi_{E}|_{C}
is given by
q^{(a_{0}+r)/2}\psi

for
\psi:F^{*}\mathscr{E}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\mathscr{E}
. The function
Y_{i}

is defined by
\psi:F^{*}\mathscr{E}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\mathscr{E}
.
It follows that

(2.19.1)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Tr}\,(\Phi w_{0},H^{a_{0}+r}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{u},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0}))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Tr}\,(\varphi w_{0},\mathscr{H}^{a_{0}}_{u}K)

\displaystyle=q^{(a_{0}+r)/2}\operatorname{Tr}\,(\sigma_{E}w_{0},E)\operatorname{Tr}\,(\psi,\mathscr{E}_{u})

\displaystyle=q^{(a_{0}+r)/2}(-1)^{a_{E}}(\dim E)Y_{i}(u).

On the other hand, by Proposition 2.18 (i), we have

(2.19.2)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Tr}\,(\Phi w_{0},H^{a_{0}+r}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{u},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0}))=\nu_{\lambda,\eta}q^{(a_{0}+r)/2}(-1)^{(a_{0}+r)/2}\dim E.

Since
(a_{0}+r)/2=d_{u}
, and
\delta_{i}=a_{E}-d_{u}
, we have

(2.19.3)

Y_{i}(u)=\nu_{\lambda,\eta}(-1)^{\delta_{i}}.

Let
\widetilde{\rho}
be the trivial extension of
\rho\in A_{G}(u)^{\wedge}
. Then

Y_{i}(u)=\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u,\widetilde{\rho})Y_{i}^{0}(u)=\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u,\widetilde{\rho}).

By comparing this with (2.19.3), we obtain (i). (ii) is proved in a similar way as in (i)
by using Proposition 2.18 (ii).
∎

2.20.

In the setup in 2.14, we consider a special case where
p=p_{0}
.
Let
F:G\to G
be a non-split Frobenius map as in 2.14.
Assume that the order of
p
in the multiplicative group
(\mathbf{Z}/n^{\prime}\mathbf{Z})^{*}

is even. Then there exists
y\in\mathbf{Z}
such that
p^{y}\equiv-1\pmod{n^{\prime}}
.
(Note that this gives a restriction on
p
. If
p\equiv 1\pmod{n^{\prime}}
,
such a
p
does not exist.)
We define
q_{0}
by
q_{0}=qp^{y}
, a power of
p
,
and let
F_{0}:G\to G
be a split Frobenius map with respect to the
\mathbf{F}_{q_{0}}
-structure.
By a similar computation as in the proof of Lemma 2.15, we have, for
x\in\mathbf{Z}_{n^{\prime}}^{F}
,

(2.20.1)

q_{0}x=qp^{y}x\equiv(-q)x\equiv x\pmod{n^{\prime}}.

It follows that
\mathbf{Z}_{n^{\prime}}^{F}
coincides with
\mathbf{Z}_{n^{\prime}}^{F_{0}}
, and we have

(Z_{G}^{\wedge})^{F}\simeq(Z_{G}^{\wedge})^{F_{0}}
.
The previous discussion, such as Proposition 2.18, Theorem 2.19 holds in this case
with
G=G_{0}
.

We compare
(G,F)
and
(G,F_{0})
. Let
C
be a unipotent class in
G
. Then

C
is
F
and
F_{0}
-stable, and let
u\in C^{F}
(resp.
v\in C^{F_{0}}
) be a split
element in
G^{F}
(resp. in
G^{F_{0}}
).
It is easy to check that

(2.20.2)

|Z_{G}(u)^{F}|=(-1)^{\dim Z_{G}(u)}|Z_{G}(v)^{F_{0}}|(-q).

Also we note that

(2.20.3)
Z_{G}(u)\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{n^{\prime}_{\lambda}}
, and the natural surjection

\mathbf{Z}_{n^{\prime}}\to\mathbf{Z}_{n^{\prime}_{\lambda}}
gives an isomorphism
A_{G}(u)\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,A_{G}(v)
.
This isomorphism is compatible with the action of
F
on
A_{G}(u)
, and that of

F_{0}
on
A_{G}(v)
. In particular, we have an isomorphism
\widetilde{A}_{G}(u)\simeq\widetilde{A}_{G}(v)
.

By (2.20.3), one can define a bijection
\xi
from the set of

G^{F}
-conjugacy classes in
G^{F}_{\operatorname{uni}}
to the set of
G^{F_{0}}
-conjugacy classes in

G^{F_{0}}_{\operatorname{uni}}
by the condition that
v=\xi(u)
is split if
u
is split.

Let
\eta=(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\in(\mathscr{M}_{G})^{F}_{\chi}=(\mathscr{M}_{G})^{F_{0}}_{\chi}
.
Recall (see 1.4) that, for
w\in\mathscr{W}_{L}
, the generalized Green functions on
G^{F}_{\operatorname{uni}}

associated to
\varphi_{0}:F^{*}\mathscr{E}_{0}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\mathscr{E}_{0}
are
given by

(2.20.4)

\displaystyle Q^{G}_{L^{w},C^{w}_{0},\mathscr{E}^{w}_{0},\varphi^{w}_{0}}(g)=\sum_{E_{i}\in(\mathscr{W}_{L})^{\wedge}_{\operatorname{ex}}}\operatorname{Tr}\,(w\sigma_{E_{i}},\widetilde{E}_{i})X_{i}(g).

We also consider the generalized Green functions on
G^{F_{0}}_{\operatorname{uni}}

associated to
\varphi^{\prime}_{0}:F_{0}^{*}\mathscr{E}_{0}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\mathscr{E}_{0}
, which we denote by

Q^{0}_{L^{w},C^{w}_{0},\mathscr{E}^{w}_{0},\varphi^{\prime w}_{0}}
.
Here we assume that
\varphi_{0}:F^{*}\mathscr{E}_{0}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\mathscr{E}_{0}
(resp.
\varphi_{0}^{\prime}:F_{0}^{*}\mathscr{E}_{0}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\mathscr{E}_{0}
)
is defined by
the trivial extension
\widetilde{\rho}_{0}
of
\rho_{0}\in A_{L}(u_{0})^{\wedge}

(resp.
\widetilde{\rho}^{\prime}_{0}
of
\rho^{\prime}_{0}\in A_{L}(v_{0})^{\wedge}
), where

u_{0}\in C_{0}^{F}
,
v_{0}\in C_{0}^{F_{0}}
are split elements,
and we identify them by the isomorphism
\widetilde{A}_{L}(u_{0})\simeq\widetilde{A}_{L}(v_{0})
.

For any
g\in C_{\lambda}
, we put
\nu_{g,\eta}=\nu_{\lambda,\eta}
, where

\nu_{\lambda,\eta}
is defined as in 2.3.

In the case of
E_{6}
, the formula connecting Green functions
for
(G,F)
and
(G,F_{0})
was proved in
[
BS
]
.
The following is a generalization of their result.

Theorem 2.21
.

Assume that
G=SL_{n}
. Let
F
and
F_{0}
be as in 2.20.

(i)

There exists a bijection
\xi
from the set of
G^{F}
-conjugacy classes of
G^{F}_{\operatorname{uni}}

to the set of
G^{F_{0}}
-conjugacy classes of
G^{F_{0}}_{\operatorname{uni}}
satisfying the
equalities

Q^{G}_{L^{w},C_{0}^{w},\mathscr{E}_{0}^{w},\varphi_{0}^{w}}(g)=\nu_{g,\eta}Q^{0}_{L^{w_{0}w},C_{0}^{w_{0}w},\mathscr{E}_{0}^{w_{0}w},\varphi^{\prime w_{0}w}_{0}}(\xi(g))(-q)

for any
(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\in(\mathscr{M}_{G})^{F}_{\chi}=(\mathscr{M}_{G})^{F_{0}}_{\chi}
.

(ii)

The bijection
\xi
is characterized by the properties

(a)

|Z_{G}(g)^{F}|=(-1)^{\dim Z_{G}(g)}|Z_{G}(\xi(g))^{F_{0}}|(-q)
.

(b)

\operatorname{Tr}\,(\Phi w_{0},H^{a_{0}+r}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{g},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0}))=\nu_{g,\eta}(-1)^{(a_{0}+r)/2}\operatorname{Tr}\,(\Phi_{0},H^{a_{0}+r}_{c}(\mathscr{P}_{\xi(g)},\dot{\mathscr{E}}_{0}))
.

Proof.

We prove (i). By Lemma 1.18, (ii) and Theorem 2.19, we have

\displaystyle X_{i,F}(g)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in I^{F}}p_{ji}Y_{j,F}(g)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in I^{F}}(-1)^{\delta_{i}+\delta_{j}}p_{ji}^{0}(-q)\nu_{g,\eta}(-1)^{\delta_{j}}Y_{j,F_{0}}(\xi(g))

\displaystyle=\nu_{g,\eta}(-1)^{\delta_{i}}X_{i,F_{0}}(\xi(g))(-q).

Then by (1.4.4), we have

\displaystyle Q^{G}_{L^{w},C_{0}^{w},\mathscr{E}_{0}^{w},\varphi_{0}^{w}}(g)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in I^{F}}(-1)^{a_{E_{i}}}\operatorname{Tr}\,(ww_{0},E_{i})X_{i,F}(g)(-1)^{(a_{0})_{i}}q^{(a_{0}+r)_{i}/2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in I^{F}}(-1)^{d_{g}}\operatorname{Tr}\,(ww_{0},E_{i})\nu_{g,\eta}X_{i,F_{0}}(\xi(g))(-q)(-1)^{(a_{0})_{i}}q^{(a_{0}+r)_{i}/2}

\displaystyle=\nu_{g,\eta}Q^{0}_{L^{ww_{0}},C_{0}^{ww_{0}},\mathscr{E}_{0}^{ww_{0}},\varphi^{\prime ww_{0}}_{0}}(\xi(g))(-q)

since
d_{g}=(a_{0}+r)_{i}/2
for
g\in C
with
i=(C,\mathscr{E})
. Hence (i) holds.

Next we show (ii). Let
\xi
be the bijection defined in 2.20. For each class
C
,
if
g=u
is split in
C^{F}
, and
\xi(u)=v
is split in
C^{F_{0}}
, then
(a) holds, and (b) was proved by Proposition 2.18.
Since
\widetilde{A}_{G}(u)\simeq\widetilde{A}_{G}(v)
, these properties also hold for
g\in C^{F}
and

\xi(g)\in C^{F_{0}}
. Hence the equality holds for our choice of
\xi
given in 2.20.

Now assume that there exists a bijection
\xi_{1}
satisfying the properties (a) and (b).
Take a split element
v\in C^{F_{0}}
, and let
u_{1}\in C^{F}
be
such that
\xi_{1}(u_{1})=v
.
Then by (a), we have
\widetilde{A}_{G}(u_{1})\simeq\widetilde{A}_{G}(v)
.
By a similar discussion as in the proof of
Theorem 2.19, the equality (b) implies that

\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u_{1},\widetilde{\rho}_{1})=\nu_{u,\eta}(-1)^{\delta_{i}}
for

i=(C,\mathscr{E})\in I^{F}
. This implies that

\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u,\widetilde{\rho})=\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u_{1},\widetilde{\rho}_{1})
for any

\widetilde{\rho}\simeq\widetilde{\rho}_{1}
under the identification
\widetilde{A}_{G}(u)\simeq\widetilde{A}_{G}(u_{1})
.
Hence by Lemma 1.9,
u
and
u_{1}
are conjugate under
G^{F}
. Thus
\xi
is unique,
and (ii) is proved.
∎

Remark 2.22.

A root of unity
\nu_{u,\eta}
for
u\in C^{F}
and

\eta=(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\in\mathscr{M}^{F}_{G}
is defined by
\nu_{u,\eta}=\rho_{0}(c_{\lambda})

in 2.3.
c_{\lambda}\in A_{L}(u_{0})
was computed explicitly in
[
Sh5
, I, Lemma 4.17]
.
Thus
\nu_{u,\eta}
can be computed explicitly.

3.
Classical groups

3.1.

In this section, we assume that
G
is a classical group, not of type
A_{n}
, and

F
is a Frobenius map on
G
, split or non-split, such that
F^{2}
is split.
(The case where
G
is of type
D_{4}
, such that
F
is a non-split Frobenius map
with
F^{3}
split, is discussed in Section 10.)
First assume that
G=Sp_{2n}
or
SO_{N}
. The existence of split elements
was discussed in
[
Sh5
, II]
. Here we review those results.
Let
C
be an
F
-stable unipotent class
in
G
. For each
u\in C^{F}
,
A_{G}(u)
is abelian, and
F
acts trivially on
A_{G}(u)
.
The “split element”
u\in C^{F}
was defined in
[
Sh5
, II, §2]
in an explicit form.
Note that in the case where
G=SO_{2n}
with
F
non-split,
u
is chosen
in connection with preferred extensions of
\mathscr{W}_{L}
-modules (see
[
Sh5
, II, 2.10, 2.11]
).
The following result was proved in Theorem 4.2 and Theorem 4.3 in
[
Sh5
, II]
.

Theorem 3.2
.

Let
G=Sp_{2n}
or
SO_{N}
. Assume that
(C,\mathscr{E})\in\mathscr{N}_{G}^{F}
belongs to
to the series
(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\in\mathscr{M}_{G}^{F}
. Choose
(u_{0},\rho_{0})\leftrightarrow(C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})

with
u_{0}\in C_{0}^{F}
split.
Then for
(u,\rho)\leftrightarrow(C,\mathscr{E})
with
u\in C^{F}
split, we have

\gamma(u_{0},\rho_{0},u,\rho)=1
. In particular,
u
is a split element in the sense of
1.7.

3.3.

In the rest of this section, we assume that
G
is a spin group
\operatorname{Spin}_{N}
with
p\neq 2
.
The existence of split elements in
G
was discussed in
[
Sh5
, III]
. However it
contains several errors, and in this occasion we shall correct them.
The main points to be corrected are as follows.

(1) The classification of quadratic forms in
[
Sh5
, III, 2.2]

is not complete, and some cases are missing.
Note that as stated in Remark 2.8 in
[
Sh5
, III]
,
the case where
F
is non-split was excluded from the discussion there.
However we need to consider the non-split case also, which comes from the above
missing case.

(2) The definition of split elements in the case of spin groups (for
F
split)
given in
[
Sh5
, III, 3.2]
is wrong. Although it was defined up to
GL_{N}
-conjugate,
this should be corrected so that it is defined up to
G^{F}
-conjugate.
Actually, the proof of Lemma 3.10 in
[
Sh5
, III]
contains
some errors. Also, we need to define split elements in the non-split
F
case,
based on the above correction (1).

In the discussion below, we attach
∗
to indicate the corrected statement
of the original one, for example, Lemma
3.10^{*}
is the corrected version of
Lemma 3.10 in
[
Sh5
, III]
.

3.4.

First we consider the problem (1).
We follow the notation in
[
Sh5
, III, 2.1]
.
Let
V
be a vector space of dimension
N\geq 3
over
\mathbf{k}
with
\operatorname{ch}\mathbf{k}\neq 2
,
and
(\ ,\ )
a non-degenerate symmetric bilinear form on
V
. Let
C(V)
be the
corresponding Clifford algebra with an embedding
V\subset C(V)
, and
C^{+}(V)

the subalgebra of
C(V)
spanned by products of even number of elements in
V
.
The Spin group
\operatorname{Spin}(V)\simeq\operatorname{Spin}_{N}
is defined as the subgroup of the group of
units in
C^{+}(V)
consisting of all products
v_{1}v_{2}\cdots v_{a}
(with
a
even)
where
v_{i}\in V
satisfy
(v_{i},v_{i})=1
.
We have a natural isogeny
\beta:\operatorname{Spin}(V)\to SO(V)
.
Let
G=\operatorname{Spin}(V)
, and
Z_{G}
the center of
G
.
If
N
is odd, then
Z_{G}
has order 2; it is generated by
\varepsilon=(-1)
times the
unit element of
C(V)
. If
N
is even,
Z_{G}
has order 4;
it is generated by
\varepsilon
and by
\omega=v_{1}v_{2}\cdots v_{N}
, where
v_{1},\dots,v_{N}
is an
orthonormal basis of
V
. We have
\omega^{2}=\varepsilon^{N/2}
, hence
Z_{G}
is generated by

\omega
if
N\equiv 2\pmod{4}
, and
Z_{G}\simeq\operatorname{\!\langle\!}\omega\operatorname{\!\rangle\!}\times\operatorname{\!\langle\!}\varepsilon\operatorname{\!\rangle\!}
is
a product of two cyclic groups of order 2 if
N\equiv 0\pmod{4}
.
In any case,
\operatorname{Ker}\beta=\{1,\varepsilon\}
.

We consider the
\mathbf{F}_{q}
-structure of
G
. Assume that
V
is defined over
\mathbf{F}_{q}

with Frobenius map
F:V\to V
, and that the form
(\ ,\ )
is compatible with

F
-action, then
C(V)
has a natural
\mathbf{F}_{q}
-structure, which induces an
\mathbf{F}_{q}
-structure
on
G
.
If
N
is odd,
G
has a unique
\mathbf{F}_{q}
-structure where
F
is split,
while if
N
is even, it has two
\mathbf{F}_{q}
-structures where
F
is split or non-split.
Assume that
N
is even. In the discussion below, we use the theory of quadratic forms
over a finite field
\mathbf{F}_{q}
with
q
: odd. See
[
Gr
]
for details.
In 2.2 in
[
Sh5
, III]
,
the quadratic form on
V
is defined only in the case where
N\equiv 0\pmod{4}
.
We also need to consider the case where
N\equiv 2\pmod{4}
.
In the full generality, they are given as follows.
Assume that
N
is even. Let
e_{1},\dots e_{N}
be the orthogonal basis of
V

such that
F(e_{i})=e_{i}
. For
x=\sum_{i}x_{i}e_{i}
, we define the quadratic
form on
V
associated to
(\ ,\ )
by the following formulas.
Choose
\delta\in\mathbf{F}_{q}-\mathbf{F}_{q}^{2}
.

(3.4.1) (i) Assume that
N\equiv 0\pmod{4}
. Then put

(x,x)=\begin{cases}x_{1}^{2}+\cdots+x_{N}^{2}&\quad\text{ split case, }\\
x_{1}^{2}+\cdots+x_{N-1}^{2}+\delta x_{N}^{2}&\quad\text{ non-split case}\end{cases}

(ii) Assume that
N\equiv 2\pmod{4}
. If
q\equiv 1\pmod{4}
, then put

(x,x)=\begin{cases}x_{1}^{2}+\cdots+x_{N}^{2}&\quad\text{ split case, }\\
x_{1}^{2}+\cdots+x_{N-1}^{2}+\delta x_{N}^{2}&\quad\text{ non-split case,}\end{cases}

while if
q\equiv-1\pmod{4}
, we exchange the condition for splitness as

(x,x)=\begin{cases}x_{1}^{2}+\cdots+x_{N-1}^{2}+\delta x_{N}^{2}&\quad\text{ split case,}\\
x_{1}^{2}+\cdots+x_{N}^{2}&\quad\text{ non-split case. }\\
\end{cases}

The complexity in the case
N\equiv 2\pmod{4}
comes from the following fact.
The splitness of the
\mathbf{F}_{q}
-structure of
SO(V)
is defined by
using the hyperbolic basis of
V
, for
N=2n
, as

(3.4.2)

(x,x)=\begin{cases}x_{1}x_{n+1}+\cdots+x_{n}x_{2n}&\quad\text{ split case, }\\
x_{1}x_{n+1}+\cdots+x_{n-1}x_{2n-1}+x^{2}_{n}-\delta x_{2n}^{2}&\quad\text{ non-split case,}\end{cases}

see, for example,
[
Sh5
, II, 2.1]
, where the case
p=2
is discussed.
In studying
SO_{N}
, the expression in (3.4.2) is convenient,
but in studying
\operatorname{Spin}_{N}
,
the expression used in (3.4.1) is more convenient.
Thus we need to change (3.4.2) to the form in (3.4.1).
Note that the form
x_{n}x_{2n}
is equivalent to
x_{n}^{2}-x_{2n}^{2}
.
We have
-1\in\mathbf{F}_{q}^{2}
if and only if
q\equiv 1\pmod{4}
.
It follows that

(3.4.3)

\displaystyle x_{n}^{2}-x_{2n}^{2}

\displaystyle\simeq\begin{cases}x_{n}^{2}+x_{2n}^{2}&\quad\text{ if }q\equiv 1\pmod{4},\\
x_{n}^{2}+\delta x_{2n}^{2}&\quad\text{ if }q\equiv-1\pmod{4},\end{cases}

\displaystyle x_{n}^{2}-\delta x_{2n}^{2}

\displaystyle\simeq\begin{cases}x_{n}^{2}+\delta x_{2n}^{2}&\quad\text{ if }q\equiv 1\pmod{4},\\
x_{n}^{2}+x_{2n}^{2}&\quad\text{ if }q\equiv-1\pmod{4}.\end{cases}

(3.4.1) follows from (3.4.3).

The discussion in
[
Sh5
, III, 2.2]
should be modified as follows.
In the case where
N\equiv 0\pmod{4}
,
F(\omega)=-\omega
if
(G,F)
is non-split.
While in the case where
N\equiv 2\pmod{4}
, then

F(\omega)=-\omega
if, either
q\equiv 1\pmod{4}
and
(G,F)
is non-split, or

q\equiv-1\pmod{4}
and
(G,F)
is split. Otherwise
F(\omega)=\omega
.
Thus (2.2.1) in
[
Sh5
, III]
should be replaced by the following statement.

(2.2.1)
∗
Let
Z_{G}
be the center of
G
.

(i)

Assume that
N
is odd, then
F
acts trivially on
Z_{G}
.

(ii)

Assume that
N
is even. If
N\equiv 0\pmod{4}
, then

F
acts trivially on
Z_{G}
if
(G,F)
is split. If
N\equiv 2\pmod{4}
,
then
F
acts trivially on
Z_{G}
if, either
(G,F)
is split and
q\equiv 1\pmod{4}
,
or
(G,F)
is non-split and
q\equiv-1\pmod{4}
.
Otherwise,
F(\omega)=-\omega
, and
F
acts non-trivially on
Z_{G}
.

3.5.

We write a partition
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{k})
of
N

as
\lambda=(1^{m_{1}},2^{m_{2}},\dots)
, where
m_{i}
is the number of
j

such that
i=\lambda_{j}
. We define a set
X_{N}
by

X_{N}=\{\lambda\mid\text{ $m_{i}$ : even if $i$ : even,}\text{ $m_{i}\leq 1$ if $i$: odd}\}.

The generalized Springer correspondence pertaining to
\chi\in Z_{G}^{\wedge}

(see 1.2.1) is essentially the same as the case of

SO_{N}
if
\chi(\varepsilon)=1
, and is described by
[
LS2
, Thm. 4.14]
as a bijection

\lambda:\mathscr{N}_{\chi}\simeq X_{N}
(see also
[
Sh5
, III, Prop. 2.5]
) if
\chi(\varepsilon)=-1
.
Remark 2.7 in
[
Sh5
, III]
should be modified as follows.
In the 4th line of Remark 2.7, it is written as
“By Proposition 2.5, the Jordan type
\lambda
of
\beta(C)
is not contained in
X_{N}
”.
This is wrong, and actually we need to assume this.
Namely, this part should be
modified as follows (this is independent from (1), (2) in 3.3).

“Assume that the Jordan type
\lambda
of
\beta(C)
is not contained in
X_{N}
.
Since
\beta
gives a bijection
C^{F}\simeq\beta(C)^{F}
, one can find
u\in C^{F}

such that
\beta(u)=v^{\bullet}
. In this case, it is known by
[
L1
, 14.3]

and by Proposition 2.5 that
A_{G}(u)\simeq A_{\overline{G}}(v^{\bullet})
.
Thus the verification of the theorem is reduced to the case where

\chi(\varepsilon)=-1
, and to the determination of the split class in
C^{F}

for
C
of Jordan type
\lambda\in X_{N}
”.

3.6.

Remark 2.8 in
[
Sh5
, III]
should be modified as follows,
according to the correction in 3.4.

Remark
2.8^{*}
.
Assume that
(G,F)
is non-split. Then
N
is even, and

Z_{G}
is generated by
\varepsilon
and
\omega
. We consider the set
\mathscr{N}_{\chi}
in
Proposition 2.5. Then
\chi(\varepsilon)=-1
. By
(2.2.1)^{*}
, we have the following.
Assume that
N\equiv 0\pmod{4}
, then
F(\omega)=\omega
.
Assume that
N\equiv 2\pmod{4}
. Then
F(\omega)=-\omega
if
q\equiv 1\pmod{4}
,
and
F(\omega)=\omega
if
q\equiv-1\pmod{4}
.
If
F(\omega)=-\omega
, then

\chi(F(\omega))=\chi(-\omega)=\chi(\varepsilon)\chi(\omega)=-\chi(\omega).

This shows that
\chi
is not
F
-stable, and so
\mathscr{N}_{\chi}^{F}=\emptyset
.
Thus in the non-split case, we may assume that
N\equiv 2\pmod{4}
and that

q\equiv-1\pmod{4}
. In this case,
F
acts trivially on
Z_{G}
.

3.7.

Lemma 3.5 in
[
Sh5
, III]
should be modified as follows
(this is independent from the correction in 3.4.)

Lemma
3.5^{*}
.

If
q\equiv 1\pmod{4}
, then
F
acts trivially on
A_{G}(u)
. While
if
q\equiv-1\pmod{4}
, the action of
F
on
A_{G}(u)
is
not necessarily trivial.

In fact, the case where
q\equiv 1\pmod{4}
is discussed in
[
Sh5
, III]
.
We consider the case where
q\equiv-1\pmod{4}
.
Assume that
(G,F)
is of split type, and
N\equiv 0\pmod{4}
.
We follow the notation in
[
Sh5
, III, 3.1]
.
We write
\lambda_{j}=2m_{j}+1
.
In the case where
|I|=2
, we have
m_{1}+m_{2}
is odd.
Then
F(x_{1})=(-1)^{m_{1}+1+1}x_{1}
, and
F(x_{2})=(-1)^{m_{2}+1+2}x_{2}
.
It follows that
F(x_{1}x_{2})=x_{1}x_{2}
,
F
acts trivially on
A_{G}(u)
.
But if
|I|=3
, the condition is that
m_{1}+m_{2}+m_{3}
is odd.
In this case,
F(x_{1})=(-1)^{m_{1}+1+1}x_{1}
and
F(x_{2})=(-1)^{m_{2}+1+2}x_{2}
.
Since we can choose
m_{1},m_{2}
arbitrary, it occurs that

F(x_{1}x_{2})=-x_{1}x_{2}
. Hence
F
acts on
A_{G}(u)
non-trivially.

3.8.

We now consider the problem (2) in 3.3.
We apply the restriction formula to the parabolic subgroup
Q=MU_{Q}
, and
a unipotent class
C^{\prime}
of
M
.
Assume that
N\geq 7
.
Following
[
Sh5
, III, 3.6]
, we consider the case where
M
is a Levi subgroup
of
Q
such that
\beta(M)\simeq SO_{N-4}\times GL_{2}
.
Take
(u,\rho)\leftrightarrow(C,\mathscr{E})\in(\mathscr{N}_{G})_{\chi},(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})\leftrightarrow(C^{\prime},\mathscr{E}^{\prime})\in(\mathscr{N}_{M})_{\chi}
.
If
X_{u,u^{\prime}}\neq\emptyset
, then
u^{\prime}
satisfies the properties,

(i)

The projection of
\beta(u^{\prime})
on
GL_{2}
is regular.

(ii)

The projection of
\beta(u^{\prime})
on
SO_{N-4}
corresponds to the unipotent elements
of Jordan type in
X_{N-4}
under the correspondence

\lambda:(\mathscr{N}_{\operatorname{Spin}_{N-4}})_{\chi}\simeq X_{N-4}
.

We write
\beta(G)=\overline{G}
, and
\beta(u)=\overline{u}
.
Also put
\beta(Q)=\overline{Q},\beta(M)=\overline{M}
, and put
\lambda=\lambda(u),\lambda^{\prime}=\lambda(u^{\prime})
.
Thus
\lambda=(\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}\leq\cdots\leq\lambda_{k})
is a partition of
N
,
and
\lambda^{\prime}=(\lambda^{\prime}_{1}\leq\lambda^{\prime}_{2}\leq\cdots\leq\lambda^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}})
is a partition of
N-4
.
It is known, by Lemma 4.5 and Lemma 4.8 in
[
LS2
]
,
that the Young diagram of
\lambda^{\prime}
is obtained from
the Young diagram of
\lambda
by removing 4 squares by the following rules.

(I)
\lambda_{i}
is odd,
\lambda_{i}>\lambda_{i-1}+4
, and

\lambda_{j}^{\prime}=\begin{cases}\lambda_{j}-4&\quad\text{ if $j=i$, }\\
\lambda_{j}&\quad\text{ otherwise. }\end{cases}

(II)
\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{i+1}\geq\lambda_{i-1}+2
and

\lambda_{j}^{\prime}=\begin{cases}\lambda_{j}-2&\quad\text{ if $j=i,i+1$, }\\
\lambda_{j}&\quad\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

(III)
\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{i+1}\geq\lambda_{i-1}+4
and

\lambda_{j}^{\prime}=\begin{cases}\lambda_{j}-3&\quad\text{ if $j=i$, }\\
\lambda_{j}-1&\quad\text{ if $j=i+1$, }\\
\lambda_{j}&\quad\text{ otherwise. }\end{cases}

(IV)
\lambda_{i+1}-2=\lambda_{i}\geq\lambda_{i-1}+1
and

\lambda^{\prime}_{j}=\begin{cases}\lambda_{j}-1&\quad\text{ if $j=i$, }\\
\lambda_{j}-3&\quad\text{ if $j=i+1$, }\\
\lambda_{j}&\quad\text{ otherwise. }\end{cases}

(V)
\lambda_{i+2}=\lambda_{i+1}=\lambda_{i}+1
and

\lambda^{\prime}_{j}=\begin{cases}\lambda_{j}-1&\quad\text{ if $j=i,i+2$, }\\
\lambda_{j}-2&\quad\text{ if $j=i+1$, }\\
\lambda_{j}&\quad\text{ otherwise. }\end{cases}

We have the following lemma.

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
\overline{Y}_{\overline{u},\overline{u}^{\prime}}
be the variety defined in (1.12.1) with respect to

\overline{G},\overline{u}=\beta(u)
and
\overline{u}^{\prime}=\beta(u^{\prime})
.
Assume that there exists
\overline{y}\in\overline{Y}_{\overline{u},\overline{u}^{\prime}}^{F}
, and
take
y\in\beta^{-1}(\overline{y})
.
Then
y\in Y_{u,u^{\prime}}
.
If
y\notin Y_{u,u^{\prime}}^{F}
, put
u_{1}=huh^{-1}\in C^{F}
for

h\in G
such that
h^{-1}f(h)=\varepsilon
.
Then we have
hy\in Y_{u_{1},u^{\prime}}^{F}
, and
\beta(u_{1})
is
\overline{G}^{F}
-conjugate to
\overline{u}
.
Furthermore, the isomorphism
Z_{G}(u)\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,Z_{G}(u_{1}),x\mapsto hxh^{-1}
induces
an
F
-equivariant isomoprhism
A_{G}(u)\simeq A_{G}(u_{1})
.

Proof.

Assume that
u\in X_{N}
. Then
\varepsilon\notin Z_{G}^{0}(u)
by
[
L1
, 14.3]
, and so
the image of
\varepsilon
in
A_{G}(u)
is not equal to 1. Take
h\in G
such that

h^{-1}F(h)=\varepsilon
, and put
u_{1}=huh^{-1}
.
Then
u_{1}
is not necessarily
G^{F}
-conjugate to
u
.
But since
\overline{h}^{-1}F(\overline{h})=1
, we have
\overline{h}=\beta(h)\in\overline{G}^{F}
.
Hence
\overline{u}
and
\beta(u_{1})
are
\overline{G}^{F}
-conjugate.
Under the isomorphism
A_{G}(u)\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,A_{G}(u_{1})
, the action of
F
on
A_{G}(u_{1})

corresponds to the action of
F\varepsilon
on
A_{G}(u)
.
Since
\varepsilon
is central in
A_{G}(u)
, this isomorphism is
F
-equivariant.

Assume that there exists
\overline{y}\in\overline{G}
such that
\overline{y}\in\overline{Y}_{\overline{u},\overline{u}^{\prime}}^{F}
.
Then there exists
y\in\beta^{-1}(\overline{y})
such that
\beta(yQ)=\overline{y}\overline{Q}
and that

y^{-1}uy\in u^{\prime}_{1}U_{Q}
for some
u_{1}^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}
. This implies that

\overline{y}^{-1}\overline{u}\overline{y}\in\overline{u}_{1}^{\prime}U_{\overline{Q}}
. But since

\overline{y}^{-1}\overline{u}\overline{y}\in\overline{u}^{\prime}U_{\overline{Q}}
, we must have
\overline{u}_{1}^{\prime}=\overline{u}^{\prime}
,
and so
u_{1}^{\prime}=u^{\prime}
. It follows that
y\in Y_{u,u^{\prime}}
.
Since
y\in\beta^{-1}(\overline{y})
,
F(y)=y
or
F(y)=\varepsilon y
.
Assume that
F(y)\neq y
, then
F(y)=\varepsilon y
.
In this case, we consider
Y_{u_{1},u^{\prime}}
instead of
Y_{u,u^{\prime}}
.
Then we have
y^{-1}h^{-1}u_{1}hy\in u^{\prime}U_{Q}
.
Since
F(hy)=F(h)F(y)=\varepsilon h\cdot\varepsilon y=hy
, this implies that

hy\in Y_{u_{1},u^{\prime}}^{F}
. The lemma is proved.
∎

Remark 3.10.

In the course of the proof of Lemma 3.10 in
[
Sh5
, III]
, a property
“if
\overline{y}\in\overline{Y}_{\overline{u},\overline{u}^{\prime}}^{F}
, then there exists
y\in Y_{u,u^{\prime}}^{F}
” was used.
But this is not always the case, and if this does not hold, one needs to
replace
u
by
u_{1}
as in the above lemma. Thus the proof of Lemma 3.10 must be modified.
Also in Lemma 3.10, only the case where
F
acts trivially on
A_{G}(u)
and on
A_{M}(u^{\prime})

was discussed. By using Lemma 1.15, we can relax this condition, and actually
Lemma 3.10 in [loc. cit.] is not used in later discussions.

3.11.

Since Lemma 3.10 in
[
Sh5
, III]
does not hold in this form,
the definition of split elements given in
[
Sh5
, III, 3.2]
is not appropriate.
In view of Lemma 3.9, the choice of
u\in C^{F}
for a split
\beta(u)
should be more
delicate. We consider the setup in 3.8, and let
\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}(\rho,\rho^{\prime})
be as in 1.14.
In the following, by modifying the discussion
in
[
Sh5
, III, Prop. 3.11]
, we shall prove that there
exists
v\in C^{F}
such that
F
acts trivially on
\varepsilon_{v,u^{\prime}}(\rho,\rho^{\prime})
.

Now assume that
v\in C^{F}
, and that there exists
y\in Y_{v,u^{\prime}}^{F}
.
Then
y^{-1}vy\in Q
, and we have natural maps

(3.11.1)

A_{G}(v)\simeq A_{G}(y^{-1}vy)\leftarrow A_{Q}(y^{-1}vy)\to A_{M}(u^{\prime}).

We can identify
\mathcal{Q}=G/Q
with the variety
\mathscr{F}
of all two-dimensional totally
isotropic subspaces of
V
. Then as in
[
LS2
, 4.8]
,

\mathcal{Q}_{u,C^{\prime}}
in (1.12.1) can be identified with
the subvariety
\mathscr{F}_{u,C^{\prime}}
of
\mathscr{F}
consisting of all
E\in\mathscr{F}
such that

(i)

E
is
\overline{u}
-stable.

(ii)

\overline{u}|_{E}\neq 1
.

(iii)

The Jordan type of
\overline{u}|_{E^{\perp}/E}
is
\lambda^{\prime}
.

In the case where
F
is split, we can choose an
F
-stable basis of
V

associated to
u
as
in
[
Sh5
, III, 3.1]
. In particular, for each
\lambda_{j}
odd, we have a
vector space
V_{j}
of
\dim V_{j}=\lambda_{j}=h
with basis
e^{j}_{1},\dots,e^{j}_{h}
, and
for each
\lambda_{j}=\lambda_{j+1}
even, we have a vector space
V_{j}
of

\dim V_{j}=2\lambda_{j}=2h

with basis
e^{j}_{1},\dots,e^{j}_{h},e^{j+1}_{1},\dots,e^{j+1}_{h}
.

V
is expressed as a direct sum of those subspaces
V_{j}
.

In the setup in 3.8, assume that
\overline{u}
is split in
\beta(C)^{F}
,
and that
\overline{u}^{\prime}
is split in
\beta(C^{\prime})^{F}
.
Also assume that
\rho\in A_{G}(u)^{\wedge}_{\operatorname{ex}},\rho^{\prime}\in A_{M}(u^{\prime})^{\wedge}_{\operatorname{ex}}
, and
that an extension
\widetilde{\rho}^{\prime}
of
\rho^{\prime}
is fixed.
We consider the following cases. Note that as explained in
[
Sh5
, III, Remark 3.12]
,
the case (III) is not used in later discussions, and so is omitted.

Case I.
Take
u\in C^{F}
such that
\overline{u}=\beta(u)
is a split element in

\beta(C)^{F}
.
First assume that
F
is split, and let
e_{1},\dots,e_{h}
be the
F
-stable basis
of
V_{i}
given as above. Let
E=\operatorname{\!\langle\!}e_{1},e_{2}\operatorname{\!\rangle\!}
. Then it is easy to see
that
\mathscr{F}_{u,C^{\prime}}=\{E\}
.
Even if
F
is non-split, the variety
\mathscr{F}_{u,C^{\prime}}
is
F
-stable, hence

E
is an
F
-stable subspace of
V
under the isomorphism
\mathscr{F}\simeq G/Q
.
Then
GL(E)\times SO(E^{\perp}/E)
is
\overline{G}^{F}
-conjugate to
\overline{M}
.
Since
\overline{u}
is split in
\overline{G}
,
(\overline{u}|_{E},\overline{u}|_{E^{\perp}/E})
is

\overline{G}^{F}
-conjugate to a split element
\overline{u}^{\prime}
in
\beta(C^{\prime})^{F}
.
Hence there exists
\overline{y}\in\overline{Y}_{\overline{u},\overline{u}^{\prime}}^{F}
such that
\overline{y}\overline{Q}

corresponds to
E
,
and
\beta^{-1}(\overline{y})
is written as
\beta^{-1}(\overline{y})=\{y,\varepsilon y\}
.
If
y\in G^{F}
, we have
y\in Y_{u,u^{\prime}}^{F}
, and we put
v=u
.
But if
F(y)=\varepsilon y
, Lemma 3.9 shows that
hy\in Y_{u_{1},u^{\prime}}^{F}
,
and we put
v=u_{1}
.
In particular, for
v=u
or
u_{1}
in
C^{F}
, there exists

y\in Y_{v,u^{\prime}}^{F}
such that
\overline{y}\in Y_{\overline{v},\overline{u}^{\prime}}^{F}
.
Since
\mathscr{F}_{u,C^{\prime}}=\{E\}
, we have
Y_{v,u^{\prime}}=yZ_{M}(u^{\prime})U_{Q}
.
Hence
X_{v,u^{\prime}}\simeq A_{M}(u^{\prime})
. Now the structure of
A_{G}(u)
is
described in
[
L2
, 14.3]
(see also
[
Sh5
, III, 2.6]
).
In this case, (3.11.1) induces an isomorphism
A_{G}(v)\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,A_{M}(u^{\prime})
,
and
A_{G}(v)\times A_{M}(u^{\prime})
acts on
\varepsilon_{v,u^{\prime}}=\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{l}[A_{M}(u^{\prime})]
(the group algebra of

A_{M}(u^{\prime})
over
\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{l}
) as the
two-sided regular representation of
A_{M}(u^{\prime})
.
In particular,
\varepsilon_{v,u^{\prime}}(\rho,\rho^{\prime})\neq 0
only when
\rho=\rho^{\prime}
,
and in which case,
F
acts trivially on
\varepsilon_{v,u^{\prime}}(\rho,\rho^{\prime})=\widetilde{\rho}\otimes\widetilde{\rho}^{\prime*}

if we choose an extension
\widetilde{\rho}
by
\widetilde{\rho}=\widetilde{\rho}^{\prime}
.

Case II.
We write the basis of
V_{i}
in 3.11 as
e_{1},\dots,e_{h},f_{1},\dots,f_{h}

with
\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{i+1}=h
.
By the discussion in
[
Sh5
, III, 3.11]
,
\mathscr{F}_{u,C^{\prime}}
is expressed as

\mathscr{F}_{u,C^{\prime}}=\bigcup_{g\in G_{1}}g\{\operatorname{\!\langle\!}e_{1},e_{2}+\alpha f_{1}\operatorname{\!\rangle\!}\mid\alpha\in\mathbf{k}\},

where
G_{1}
is a subgroup of
Z_{\overline{G}}(\overline{u})
isomorphic to
Sp(M_{i})

(
M_{i}
is a subspace of
V
which is the direct sum of
V_{k}
such that
\lambda_{k}=h
).
Put
E=\operatorname{\!\langle\!}e_{1},e_{2}\operatorname{\!\rangle\!}
. Then
E\in\mathscr{F}_{u,C^{\prime}}^{F}
(since
h
is even,

E
is
F
-stable even if
F
is non-split). Since
\operatorname{\!\langle\!}e_{1},e_{2}+\alpha f_{1}\operatorname{\!\rangle\!}

is
Z_{\overline{G}}(\overline{u})
-conjugate to
E
,
\mathscr{F}_{u,C^{\prime}}

coincides with the
Z_{\overline{G}}(\overline{u})
-orbit of
E
.
Then there exists
\overline{y}\in\overline{Y}_{\overline{u},\overline{u}^{\prime}}^{F}
such that
\overline{y}\overline{Q}

corresponds to
E
.
By a similar discussion as in Case I, by replacing
u
by
v\in C^{F}
,
one can find
y\in Y_{v,u^{\prime}}^{F}
and
\beta(y)\in\overline{Y}^{F}_{\overline{v},\overline{u}^{\prime}}
.
Since
Z_{\overline{G}}(\overline{v})
acts
transitively on
\mathscr{F}_{v,C^{\prime}}
,

Y_{v,u^{\prime}}=Z_{G}(v)yZ_{M}(u^{\prime})U_{Q}
. Here the stabilizer of
E
in
G
is
equal to
Q^{\prime}=yQy^{-1}
. Since
E
is contained in
V_{i}
such that
\lambda_{i}
is even,
we have
A_{G}(y^{-1}vy)=A_{Q}(y^{-1}vy)
. Then (3.11.1) induces an isomorphism

A_{G}(v)\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,A_{M}(u^{\prime})
.
Thus we have
X_{v,u^{\prime}}\simeq A_{M}(u^{\prime})
, and
\varepsilon_{v,u^{\prime}}
coincides with the regular
representation
\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{l}[A_{M}(u^{\prime})]
. Hence

\varepsilon_{v,u^{\prime}}(\rho,\rho^{\prime})\neq 0
only when
\rho=\rho^{\prime}
, and if we choose an extension

\widetilde{\rho}=\widetilde{\rho}^{\prime}
,

F
acts trivially on

\varepsilon_{v,u^{\prime}}(\rho,\rho^{\prime})=\widetilde{\rho}\otimes\widetilde{\rho}^{\prime*}
.

Case IV.
First assume that
F
is split. Put
M_{i}=V_{i}\oplus V_{i+1}
,
where
\dim V_{i+1}=h
and
\dim V_{i+1}=h-2
with
h
odd.
Write the basis of
V_{i+1},V_{i}
in 3.11 as
e_{1},\dots,e_{h}
for
V_{i+1}
,
and
e_{1}^{\prime},\dots,e_{h-2}^{\prime}
for
V_{i}
.
Then by
[
Sh5
, III, 3.11]
, we have

\mathscr{F}_{u,C^{\prime}}=\{E=\operatorname{\!\langle\!}e_{1},e_{2}+e_{1}^{\prime}\operatorname{\!\rangle\!},E^{\prime}=\operatorname{\!\langle\!}e_{1},e_{2}-e_{1}^{\prime}\operatorname{\!\rangle\!}\}.

Here both of
E,E^{\prime}
are
F
-stable. Even in the case
F
is non-split,

\mathscr{F}_{u,C^{\prime}}
is written as
\mathscr{F}_{u,C^{\prime}}=\{E,E^{\prime}\}
with
E,E^{\prime}
:
F
-stable.

There exist
\overline{y}\in\overline{Y}^{F}_{\overline{u},\overline{u}^{\prime}}
such that

\overline{y}\overline{Q}
corresponds to
E
, and
\overline{y}^{\prime}\in\overline{Y}^{F}_{\overline{u},\overline{u}^{\prime}}

such that
\overline{y}^{\prime}\overline{Q}
corresponds to
E^{\prime}
. One can find

v_{1}\in C^{F}
such that
y\in Y_{v_{1},u^{\prime}}^{F}
, and
v_{2}\in C^{F}
such that

y^{\prime}\in Y_{v_{2},u^{\prime}}^{F}
for some
v_{1},v_{2}\in\{u,u_{1}\}
. We need to show that

v_{1}=v_{2}
. For this, we argue as follows.
We consider
a transformation
z\in SO(V_{i})
such that
z(e_{1}^{\prime})=-e_{1}^{\prime},z(e_{h-2})=-e_{h-2}
,
and
z(e_{j}^{\prime})=e_{j}^{\prime}
for any other
j
. Let
W
be a subspace of

V_{i}
spanned by
e_{1}^{\prime},e^{\prime}_{h-2}
, then
W
is
F
-stable, and the corresponding map

\beta_{1}:\operatorname{Spin}(W)\to SO(W)
is
F
-equivariant. Here
\operatorname{Spin}(W)=\operatorname{Spin}_{2}
, and
the center
Z_{1}
of
\operatorname{Spin}_{2}
is
\operatorname{\!\langle\!}\varepsilon_{1}\operatorname{\!\rangle\!}\times\operatorname{\!\langle\!}\omega_{1}\operatorname{\!\rangle\!}
,
where
\operatorname{Ker}\beta_{1}=\operatorname{\!\langle\!}\varepsilon_{1}\operatorname{\!\rangle\!}
. Thus we have
\beta_{1}(\omega_{1})=-1\in SO_{2}
.
Since
\operatorname{Spin}_{2}
is a subgroup of
\operatorname{Spin}_{N}
,
one can find
x\in G^{F}
such that
\beta(x)|_{V_{i}}
coincides with
z
. It follows that

x(E)=E^{\prime}
. Since
x\in G^{F}
, we see that
v_{1}=v_{2}
as asserted.
Put
v=v_{1}=v_{2}
.
Then
F
acts trivially on
\mathscr{F}_{v,C^{\prime}}=\{E,E^{\prime}\}
. Hence we have

X_{v,u^{\prime}}\simeq A_{M}(u^{\prime})\sqcup A_{M}(u^{\prime})
as in Case I.

Let
I
be the set of
i
such that
\lambda_{i}
is odd.
We follow the notation in
[
Sh5
, III, 2.6]
.
Then
A_{Q}(y^{-1}vy)\simeq A_{M}(u^{\prime})
, and by (3.11.1)
A_{M}(u^{\prime})

is regarded as a subgroup of
A_{G}(v)
such that

(3.11.2)

A_{G}(v)=A_{M}(u^{\prime})\sqcup x_{i}x_{i+1}A_{M}(u^{\prime})\sqcup x_{j}x_{i}A_{M}(u^{\prime})\sqcup x_{j}x_{i+1}A_{M}(u^{\prime})

for
x_{j}
with
j\notin\{i,i+1\}

(
\{x_{j}\mid j\in I\}
is the set of generators of
A_{G}(v)
, and

\{x_{j}\mid j\in I-\{i,i+1\}\}
is that of
A_{M}(u^{\prime})
).
Note that the action of
A_{G}(v)
on
\mathscr{F}_{v,C^{\prime}}=\{E,E^{\prime}\}
is
given as follows;
A_{M}(u^{\prime})
leaves
E,E^{\prime}
stable,

x_{i}x_{i+1}
leaves
E,E^{\prime}
stable, and
x_{j}x_{i},x_{j}x_{i+1}

permute
E
and
E^{\prime}
. Thus

\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}\simeq\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{l}[A_{M}(u^{\prime})]\oplus\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{l}[A_{M}(u^{\prime})],

and

A_{G}(v)
acts on
\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}
through the decomposition (3.11.2).
Also the action of
F
on
\varepsilon_{v,u^{\prime}}
is given by the action of
F
on
A_{M}(u^{\prime})

through (3.11.2).
Let
\rho
be the unique irreducible representation of
A_{G}(v)
pertaining to
\chi

as given in
[
L2
, 14.4]
(see also
[
Sh5
, III, 2.5]
),
and define
\rho^{\prime}
of
A_{M}(u^{\prime})
similarly.
Then
\rho|_{A_{M}(u^{\prime})}=\rho^{\prime}\oplus\rho^{\prime}
, and
\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}(\rho,\rho^{\prime})
coincides with

\rho\otimes\rho^{\prime*}
.
Assume given an extension
\widetilde{\rho}^{\prime}
of
\rho^{\prime}\in A_{M}(u^{\prime})^{\wedge}
.
There exists a unique extension

\widetilde{\rho}
of
\rho\in A_{G}(v)^{\wedge}
such that
\widetilde{\rho}|_{\widetilde{A}_{M}(u^{\prime})}=\widetilde{\rho}^{\prime}\oplus\widetilde{\rho}^{\prime}
.
Then
F
acts trivially on
\varepsilon_{v,u^{\prime}}=\widetilde{\rho}\otimes\widetilde{\rho}^{\prime*}
.

Case V.
Put
h=\lambda_{i+1}=\lambda_{i+2}
with
h
even. We write the basis of

V_{i+1}
as
e_{1},\dots,e_{h},f_{1},\dots,f_{h}
.
Then it is known by the discussion in
[
Sh5
, III, 3.11]
that

Z_{\overline{G}}(\overline{u})
acts transitively on
\mathscr{F}_{u,C^{\prime}}
,
and
E=\operatorname{\!\langle\!}e_{1},e_{2}\operatorname{\!\rangle\!}\in\mathscr{F}_{u,C^{\prime}}^{F}
.
Thus we may choose an element

v\in C^{F}
so that
y\in Y_{v,u^{\prime}}^{F}
with
\overline{y}\in\overline{Y}_{\overline{v},\overline{u}^{\prime}}^{F}
, where

\overline{v}
is split in
\beta(C)^{F}
.
In a similar way as in Case II, we have
Y_{v,u^{\prime}}=Z_{G}(v)yZ_{M}(u^{\prime})U_{Q}
.
Since
E=\operatorname{\!\langle\!}e_{1},e_{2}\operatorname{\!\rangle\!}
is contained in
V_{i+1}
with
i+1\notin I
,

x_{i}\in A_{\overline{G}}(\overline{v})
stabilizes
E
. It follows that

A_{G}(y^{-1}vy)=A_{Q}(y^{-1}vy)
,
and we have a natural map
\pi:A_{G}(v)\to A_{M}(u^{\prime})
by (3.11.1).
Hence
X_{v,u^{\prime}}\simeq A_{M}(u^{\prime})
, and
A_{G}(v)
acts on
X_{v,u^{\prime}}
through

\pi
.
But
\pi
is not surjective
(
A_{M}(u^{\prime})
is generated by
\{x_{j}\mid j\in I^{\prime}=I-\{i\}\sqcup\{i+2\}\}
).
Then
A_{G}(v)\times A_{M}(u^{\prime})
acts on
\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{l}[A_{M}(u^{\prime})]
through the homomorphism

A_{G}(v)\times A_{M}(u^{\prime})\to A_{M}(u^{\prime})\times A_{M}(u^{\prime})
.
Note that for
\rho\in A_{G}(v)^{\wedge}
,
\dim\rho
is determined by
|I|

(
[
L1
, Prop. 14.4]
, see also [III, Prop. 2.5]).
For
\rho^{\prime}\in A_{M}(u^{\prime})^{\wedge}_{\operatorname{ex}}
, the pullback
\rho=\pi^{*}(\rho^{\prime})
also pertains to
\chi
.
Since
|I|=|I^{\prime}|
,
\rho
gives an irreducible
representation of
A_{G}(v)
with
\dim\rho=\dim\rho^{\prime}
.
Let
\widetilde{\rho}^{\prime}
be an extension of
\rho^{\prime}\in A_{M}(u^{\prime})^{\wedge}
. We define
\widetilde{\rho}
as
the pullback of
\widetilde{\rho}^{\prime}
, then
F
acts trivially on

\varepsilon_{v,u^{\prime}}(\rho,\rho^{\prime})=\widetilde{\rho}\otimes\widetilde{\rho}^{\prime*}
.

3.12.

For an
F
-stable class
C
in
G
corresponding to
\lambda\in X_{N}
,
we define a split element
v\in C^{F}
as follows.
For
\lambda\in X_{N}
, there exists
\lambda^{\prime}\in X_{N-4}
such that
\lambda^{\prime}

is obtained from
\lambda
by the pattern in (I), (II), (IV) or (V)
(note that
X_{N}=\emptyset
if
N=2
).
We choose
\lambda^{\prime}
such that
\lambda_{i}
is as big as possible, first look for
\lambda^{\prime}
in (I),
then in (II), and so on until (V).
Let
\beta(C)
be the
F
-stable class in
\overline{G}=SO_{N}
.
By Theorem 3.2, there exists a split element
\overline{u}\in\beta(C)^{F}
(in the sense of 1.7).
Let
Q=MU_{Q}
be an
F
-stable parabolic subgroup of
G
such that

M
is an
F
-stable Levi subgroup as in 3.8,
and
C^{\prime}
be an
F
-stable unipotent class in
M
corresponding to
\lambda^{\prime}
. By induction,
we may assume that there exists a split element
u^{\prime}\in C^{\prime F}
.
Then
\overline{u}^{\prime}=\beta(u^{\prime})

is a split element in
\beta(C^{\prime})^{F}
.
By applying the discussion in 3.11,
one can find
v\in C^{F}
and
y\in Y_{v,u^{\prime}}^{F}
such that

\overline{v}
is
\overline{G}^{F}
-conjugate to
\overline{u}
.
We define
v\in C^{F}
as a split element in
C^{F}
.

The following result is a refinement of Theorem 3.13 in
[
Sh5
, III]
.
Note that the case
\chi(\varepsilon)=1
is covered by Theorem 3.2.

Theorem 3.13
.

Let
G=\operatorname{Spin}_{N}
. Assume that
(C,\mathscr{E})\in\mathscr{N}_{\chi}^{F}
belongs to

(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\in\mathscr{M}_{\chi}^{F}
pertaining to
\chi\in Z_{G}^{\wedge}
such that

\chi(\varepsilon)=-1
.
Choose
(u_{0},\rho_{0})\leftrightarrow(C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})
with
u_{0}\in C_{0}^{F}
split.
Assume that
(v,\rho)\leftrightarrow(C,\mathscr{E})
with
v\in C^{F}
split.

(i)

Recall that
\widetilde{A}_{G}(v)=\operatorname{\!\langle\!}\tau\operatorname{\!\rangle\!}\ltimes A_{G}(v)
.
For
\rho\in A_{G}(v)^{\wedge}_{\operatorname{ex}}
, there exists a unique extension

\widetilde{\rho}
such that
\tau
acts trivially on
\widetilde{\rho}
.

(ii)

Let
\widetilde{\rho}_{0},\widetilde{\rho}
be extensions of
\rho_{0},\rho
as in (i).
Then we have
\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},v,\widetilde{\rho})=1
.
In particular,
v\in C^{F}
is a split element in the sense of 1.7.

Proof.

First assume that
N\geq 7
. Let
M
be an
F
-stable Levi subgroup such that

\beta(M)\simeq GL_{2}\times SO_{N-4}
. Let
\lambda\in X_{N}
be the Jordan type of

\overline{v}\in\beta(C)
. By 3.12, there exists
\lambda^{\prime}\in X_{N-4}
such that

\lambda^{\prime}
is obtained from
\lambda
by the pattern in (I), (II), (V) or (V),
and a split element
u^{\prime}\in C^{\prime F}
, where
C^{\prime}
is a unipotent class in
M
,
and the Jordan type of
\overline{u}^{\prime}
is
\lambda^{\prime}
. Then by 3.11, there exists

\rho^{\prime}\in A_{M}(u^{\prime})^{\wedge}
such that
\rho\in A_{G}(u)^{\wedge}
is obtained from
\rho^{\prime}
.

First we show (i). By induction, we may assume that the extension
\widetilde{\rho}^{\prime}

of
\rho^{\prime}
has the property that
\tau
acts trivially on
\widetilde{\rho}^{\prime}
. Then

\widetilde{\rho}
is determined from
\widetilde{\rho}^{\prime}
by the procedure discussed in 3.11.
In particular, if
\tau
acts trivially on
\widetilde{\rho}^{\prime}
, then
\tau
acts trivially
on
\widetilde{\rho}
. Thus (i) holds.

Next we show (ii). Again by induction, we may assume that

\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u^{\prime},\widetilde{\rho}^{\prime})=1
. Then
\widetilde{\rho}
is determined from
\widetilde{\rho}^{\prime}
,
and the discussion in 3.11 shows that
F
acts trivially on
\varepsilon_{v,u^{\prime}}(\rho,\rho^{\prime})
.
By Lemma 1.15, we have
\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},v,\widetilde{\rho})=\delta^{-1}
under the notation there.
Here
\delta
is determined by comparing the preferred extensions of

V_{(u,\rho)}\in\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\wedge}
and
of
V_{(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})}\in{\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\prime}}^{\wedge}
. But in this case
\mathscr{W}_{L},\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\prime}
are isomorphic to the
Weyl groups of type
B_{m}
. Hence the preferred extensions are trivial,
and we have
\delta=1
. Thus
\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},v,\widetilde{\rho})=1
, and (ii) follows.

It remains to consider the case where
N\leq 6
. Assume that
N=6
(resp.
N=3
).
In this case,
X_{N}=\{(15)\}
(resp,
X_{N}=\{(3)\}
), and
the corresponding
(u,\rho)\leftrightarrow(C,\mathscr{E})
is a cuspidal pair by
[
LS2
, Cor. 4.9]
,
with
u
regular unipotent in
G
.
The natural map
Z_{G}\to A_{G}(u)
gives an isomorphism
Z_{G}\simeq A_{G}(u)
, hence

A_{G}(u)
is abelian. Moreover,
\rho\in A_{G}(u)^{\wedge}
coincides with
\chi\in Z_{G}^{\wedge}
.
By the definition of split elements in 1.7, the condition that
u
is split
(in the sense of 1.7) is given by the condition for
(u,\rho^{\prime})
with
\rho^{\prime}\neq\rho
.
Since
(u,\rho^{\prime})
does not belong to
X_{N}
, it is only concerned with
\overline{G}
.
In particular, we may choose a split element

\overline{u}\in\beta(C)^{F}
, and define
v\in C^{F}
by the condition that
\beta(v)=\overline{u}
.
Since we may choose
\varphi_{0}:F^{*}\mathscr{E}_{0}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\mathscr{E}_{0}
for
\mathscr{E}_{0}=\mathscr{E}
freely,
we choose
\widetilde{\rho}\in\widetilde{A}_{G}(v)^{\wedge}
such that
\tau
acts trivially on
\widetilde{\rho}

(note that
A_{G}(v)
is abelian). Hereafter, we define
\Xi_{G}
in (1.7.1) by using
those
\varphi_{0}
corresponding to
\widetilde{\rho}
for
\operatorname{Spin}_{6}
(resp.
\operatorname{Spin}_{3}
).
Then
v\in C^{F}
is a split element in the sense of 1.7, and (i), (ii) hold.

Next assume that
N=5
or 4.
In those cases, the discussion in 3.8 makes sense; we consider

Q=MU_{Q}
, where
M
is a Levi subgroup of
Q
such that
\beta(M)\simeq GL_{2}

(we regard
SO_{N-4}
as the unit group
\{1\}
).
We have
X_{5}=\{(5),(122)\}
and
X_{4}=\{(22),(13)\}
.
Let
u\in C^{F}
corresponding to
X_{N}
. If
N=5
, then

A_{G}(u)\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{2}
, and
the natural map
Z_{G}\to A_{G}(u)
gives an isomorphism
Z_{G}\simeq A_{G}(u)
.
If
N=4
,
A_{G}(u)
is abelian.

\mathscr{N}_{\chi}
corresponds to
(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\in\mathscr{M}_{\chi}
, where

L
is a Levi subgroup such that
\beta(L)=GL_{2}
.
(u_{0},\rho_{0})\leftrightarrow(C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})

is given by a regular unipotent element
u_{0}
in
L
, and the nontrivial character

\rho_{0}
of
A_{L}(u_{0})=Z_{L}=\mathbf{Z}_{2}
. Hence
M=L
.
If we regard
X_{1}=\{(1)\}
,
X_{0}=\{(-)\}
as the set corresponding to
u_{0}
,
we have a bijection
X_{1}\simeq(\mathscr{N}_{L})_{\chi},X_{0}\simeq(\mathscr{N}_{L})_{\chi}
.
The variety
\mathscr{F}_{u,C^{\prime}}
is defined similarly to 3.11, and
the discussion there is also applied to the present situation;
the cases (I), (II) for
N=5
, (II) and the cases (II), (IV) for
N=4
.
Hence the verification of (i), (ii) is reduced to the case where
G=SL_{2}
.
In this case, the existence of split elements
is given by Theorem 2.19.
∎

4.
Preliminaries on exceptional groups

4.1.

In the rest of this paper, we assume that
G
is almost simple, simply connected
of exceptional type.
The classification of unipotent classes in
G
were done by
Stuhler
[
St
]
for type
G_{2}
, by
Shinoda
[
Shi
]
for type
F_{4}
with
p=2
, by
[
Sh1
]
for
type
F_{4}
with
p\neq 2
, and by Mizuno
[
M1
]
,
[
M2
]
for

E_{6},E_{7}
and
E_{8}
.
In this paper, we follow the notation in
[
Sp5
]
, which is based
on Mizuno’s notation.

The following facts are known.

(4.1.1) Assume that
A_{G}(u)
is abelian.
If
F
acts trivially on
A_{G}(u)
for
u\in C^{F}
, then
F
acts trivially on

A_{G}(u_{1})
for any choice of
u_{1}\in C^{F}
, namely, the property that

F
acts trivially on
A_{G}(u)
is independent from the choice of
u\in C^{F}
.

In fact,
u_{1}\in C
is expressed as
u_{1}=u_{c}
, an element
obtained from
u
by twisting by
c\in A_{G}(u)
. Then the action of
F
on
A_{G}(u_{1})

corresponds to the action of
cF
on
A_{G}(u)
, where
c
acts as an inner automorphism.
Since
A_{G}(u)
is abelian, the action of
cF
coincides with that of
F
on
A_{G}(u)
.

(4.1.2) Any unipotent class
C
in
G
is
F
-stable.
For any class
C
, there exists a representative
u\in C^{F}
.

In fact, the former statement follows from the labeling of
unipotent classes of exceptional groups in terms of the
subsystems of the root system
\varDelta
of
G
(see 4.3 below).
If
C
is written as
C=C_{X}
with
X\subset\varDelta
, then

F(C_{X})=C_{F(X)}
gives the same unipotent class.
The latter statement follows from the general theory.

(4.1.3) Unless
G
is
E_{6}
, any class
C
has
a representative
u\in C^{F}
such that
F
acts trivially on
A_{G}(u)
.

In fact, it is checked, for all
A_{G}(u)
occurring in
exceptional groups not of type
E_{6}
, that the inequality

(*)

|A_{G}(u)/\!\!\sim_{F}|<|A_{G}(u)/\!\!\sim|

holds unless the action of
F
on
A_{G}(u)
is given by an inner automorphism,
where
A_{G}(u)/\!\!\sim_{F}\!
is the set of
F
-twisted conjugacy classes of
A_{G}(u)
, and

A_{G}(u)/\!\!\sim
is the set of conjugacy classes in
A_{G}(u)
.
But
|A_{G}(u)/\!\!\sim_{F}|
coincides with the number of
G^{F}
-orbits in
C^{F}
, and

|A_{G}(u)/\!\!\sim|
coincides with the number of
G^{F^{m}}
-orbits in
C^{F^{m}}
for a sufficiently
large
m
since
F^{m}
acts trivially on
A_{G}(u)
.
Hence (*) does not occur. It follows that
F
acts as an inner automorphism,
and there exists
\dot{a}\in Z_{G}(u)
such that
\dot{a}F
acts trivially on
A_{G}(u)
.
Then
F
acts trivially on
A_{G}(u_{1})
, where
u_{1}=u_{a}\in C^{F}
is a twisted element
of
u
by
a\in A_{G}(u)
.

(4.1.4) Assume that
G=E_{6}
. Then
Z_{G}\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{3}
(resp.
Z_{G}\simeq\{1\}
)
if
p\neq 3
(resp.
p=3
).
Assume that
p\neq 3
, and that
F
is a split Frobenius map (resp. non-split Frobenius map),
then
F
acts trivially on
\mathbf{Z}_{3}
if and only
q\equiv 1\pmod{3}

(resp.
q\equiv-1\pmod{3}
).
If
F
acts trivially on
Z_{G}
, then for any
C
, there exists
u\in C^{F}
such that

F
acts trivially on
A_{G}(u)
, otherwise, there exists a class
C
such that

F
acts non-trivially on
A_{G}(u)
for any choice of
u\in C^{F}
.

In fact, a similar argument as in (4.1.3) works also for this case.
In the case where
G=E_{6}
of adjoint type, (4.1.4) was given in

[
M1
]
,
[
BS
]
(see also the book of Liebeck-Seitz
[
LiS
, 22.2.3]
).
The simply connected case is easily obtained from the above results.

4.2.

For type
E_{6},E_{7},E_{8}
, the following labeling of the Dynkin diagram is used.

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\kern-3.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern
