1.0pt\raise-2.22223pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\bullet}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\kern 212.5pt\raise-10.80011pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-3.22223pt\hbox{$\textstyle{8}$}}}}}}}}}\ignorespaces

In later discussion, we use a precise description of the root systems
of type
E_{6},E_{7},E_{8}
, which we follow the notation in
[
Bo
]
.
Let
V=\mathbf{R}^{8}
be the euclidean space with the inner product
(\ ,\ )
,
and
\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{8}
an orthonormal basis of
V
. The set of
simple roots
\Pi=\{\alpha_{i}\mid i\in I\}
of type
E_{8}
is given by

\displaystyle\Pi

\displaystyle=\{\alpha_{1}=\frac{1}{2}(\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2}-\cdots-\varepsilon_{7}+\varepsilon_{8}),\quad\alpha_{2}=\varepsilon_{2}+\varepsilon_{1},\quad\alpha_{3}=\varepsilon_{2}-\varepsilon_{1},

\displaystyle\quad\alpha_{4}=\varepsilon_{3}-\varepsilon_{2},\quad\alpha_{5}=\varepsilon_{4}-\varepsilon_{3},\quad\alpha_{6}=\varepsilon_{5}-\varepsilon_{4},\quad\alpha_{7}=\varepsilon_{6}-\varepsilon_{5},\quad\alpha_{8}=\varepsilon_{7}-\varepsilon_{6}\},

where
I=\{1,2,\dots,8\}
corresponds to the labeling of the Dynkin diagram.
The set
\varDelta^{+}
of positive roots of type
E_{8}
is given by

\displaystyle\varDelta^{+}=\bigl\{\pm\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}\ (1\leq i<j\leq 8),\quad\frac{1}{2}(\varepsilon_{8}+\sum_{i=1}^{7}(-1)^{\nu(i)}\varepsilon_{i})\ \text{ with $\ \sum_{i=1}^{7}\nu(i)$ even}\bigr\}.

The set of simple roots
\Pi_{7}
(resp.
\Pi_{6}
) of type
E_{7}
(resp.
E_{6}
)
is given by
\Pi_{7}=\{\alpha_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq 7\}

(resp.
\Pi_{6}=\{\alpha_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq 6\}
),
and the set of positive roots
\varDelta^{+}_{7}
of type
E_{7}
, and
\varDelta^{+}_{6}
of type
E_{6}

are given as subsets of
\varDelta^{+}
by

\displaystyle\varDelta_{7}^{+}

\displaystyle=\bigl\{\pm\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}\ (1\leq i<j\leq 6),\quad\varepsilon_{8}-\varepsilon_{7},\quad\frac{1}{2}(\varepsilon_{8}-\varepsilon_{7}+\sum_{i=1}^{6}(-1)^{\nu(i)}\varepsilon_{i})\bigr\},

\displaystyle\varDelta_{6}^{+}

\displaystyle=\bigl\{\pm\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}\ (1\leq i<j\leq 5),\quad\frac{1}{2}(\varepsilon_{8}-\varepsilon_{7}-\varepsilon_{6}+\sum_{i=1}^{5}(-1)^{\nu(i)}\varepsilon_{i})\bigr\}.

Next consider the case of
F_{4}
. The labeling of the Dynkin diagram is given by

\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{2}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{3}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{4}

Following
[
Bo
]
, we give the root system of type
F_{4}
as follows.
Let
V=\mathbf{R}^{4}
be the euclidean space with the inner product
(\ \ )
,
and
\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{4}
an orthonormal basis of
V
.
The set of simple roots
\Pi=\{\alpha_{i}\mid i\in I\}
is given by

\Pi=\{\alpha_{1}=\varepsilon_{2}-\varepsilon_{3},\quad\alpha_{2}=\varepsilon_{3}-\varepsilon_{4},\quad\alpha_{3}=\varepsilon_{4},\quad\alpha_{4}=\frac{1}{2}(\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2}-\varepsilon_{3}-\varepsilon_{4})\},

where
I=\{1,2,3,4\}
corresponds to the labeling of the Dynkin diagram.
The set
\varDelta^{+}
of positive roots of type
F_{4}
is given by

\varDelta^{+}=\{\varepsilon_{i},\quad\varepsilon_{i}\pm\varepsilon_{j}\ (1\leq i<j\leq 4),\quad\frac{1}{2}(\varepsilon_{1}\pm\varepsilon_{2}\pm\varepsilon_{3}\pm\varepsilon_{4})\}.

4.3.

Here we explain the labeling of unipotent classes for exceptional groups
used in
[
Sp5
]
, which depends on
[
Sp2
, p.26]
.
Let
G
be a simply connected, almost simple group of exceptional type,
and
\varDelta
the root system associated to
G
and
T
, with
the vertex set
I
corresponding to the set of simple roots
\{\alpha_{i}\mid i\in I\}
.
For any subsystem
X
of

\varDelta
, we denote by
C_{X}
the unipotent class obtained from the regular
unipotent class of a reductive subgroup
G_{X}
of
G
. The unipotent class

C
in
G
is called type
X
if
C=C_{X}
.
The conjugacy class of the subsystem
X
in
\varDelta
determines a unique
unipotent class
C=C_{X}
except the following cases.

(a) In the case where
G
is not simply laced, we denote by
\widetilde{A}_{i}
the
subsystem
A_{i}
, where all vertices correspond to short roots, and use the notation

A_{i}
where all vertices correspond to long roots. The notation of this kind
is also used for the case of
F_{4}
, such as
A_{1}+\widetilde{A}_{2}
, etc.

(b) Assume that
G
is
E_{7}
(resp.
E_{8}
). In this case, there exist
two families
X=3A_{1},A_{3}+A_{1},4A_{1},A_{3}+2A_{1},A_{5},A_{5}+A_{1}

(resp.
X=4A_{1},A_{3}+2A_{1},2A_{3},A_{5}+A_{1},A_{7}
)
such that
C_{X}
represent two different classes in
G
.
In this case, we denote by
X^{\prime}
such as
(3A_{1})^{\prime}
, etc.,
the subsystem of
\varDelta
which is contained in
the subsystem
A_{7}\subset E_{7}
(resp. the subsystem
A_{8}\subset E_{8}
),
and denote by
X^{\prime\prime}
otherwise, such as
(3A_{1})^{\prime\prime}
, etc..
Here the subsystem
A_{7}
(resp.
A_{8}
) is obtained from the extended Dynkin diagram

\widetilde{E}_{7}
(resp.
\widetilde{E}_{8}
) by using the negative of highest root.

Moreover, for simplifying the labeling of unipotent classes,

(4A_{1})^{\prime\prime},(A_{3}+2A_{1})^{\prime\prime}
are written as

4A_{1}
,
A_{3}+2A_{1}
in the case of
E_{7}
, and
(4A_{1})^{\prime},(A_{3}+2A_{1})^{\prime},(2A_{3})^{\prime},(A_{7})^{\prime}

are written as
4A_{1},A_{3}+2A_{1},2A_{3},A_{7}
in the case
E_{8}

(since other cases do not appear in the classification of unipotent classes).

(c) In the case where
p
is a bad prime, we use a notation such as

X_{2}
(for
p=2
) or
X_{3}
(for
p=3
), in addition to
X
,
such as
(A_{3}+A_{2})_{2},A_{3}+A_{2}
for
E_{7}
with
p=2
.
In this case,
X_{2}
(or
X_{3}
) represents the class which has a representative
in the regular unipotent class in the subgroup
G_{X}
, and
X
represents
another class which has no intersection with
G_{X}
.
The class
A_{3}+A_{2}
has a representative in the semi-regular unipotent
class in the Levi subgroup of type
D_{6}
in the group
E_{7}
.

(d) We denote by
X(a_{i})
the subregular classes in the reductive group
G_{X}
.

This fits to the notation of the tables in
[
M2
]
.

4.4.

For a unipotent element
u
in
G
, the possibilities for
A_{G}(u)
are given as

1,\mathbf{Z}_{2},\mathbf{Z}_{3},\mathbf{Z}_{4},\mathbf{Z}_{5},\mathbf{Z}_{6}\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{2}\times\mathbf{Z}_{3},\quad\mathbf{Z}_{2}\times\mathbf{Z}_{2},\quad S_{3},S_{4},S_{5},\quad\mathbf{Z}_{2}\times S_{3},\quad D_{8},

where
\mathbf{Z}_{n}
is the cyclic group of order
n
,
S_{n}
is the symmetric
group of degree
n
, and
D_{8}
is the dihedral group of order 8.

Concerning with
A_{G}(u)^{\wedge}
, we follow the notation in
[
Sp5
]
. In particular,

\mathbf{Z}_{2}^{\wedge}=\{1,-1\}
, where
-1
is the non-trivial representation of
\mathbf{Z}_{2}
.

S_{3}^{\wedge}=\{1,\theta,\varepsilon\}
,
D_{8}^{\wedge}=\{1,\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime\prime},\theta\}
,
where
\varepsilon
is the sign representation,
\theta

is the unique irreducible representation such that
\deg\theta=2
,
and
\varepsilon^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}
(in the case
D_{8}
) are two additional representations
of degree 1.

(\mathbf{Z}_{2}\times S_{3})^{\wedge}=\{1,\theta,\varepsilon,-1,-\theta,-\varepsilon\}
where

-\rho
denotes
(-1)\otimes\rho
for
\rho\in S_{3}^{\wedge}
.

Concerning with
\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\wedge}
, we follow the notation in
[
Sp5
]
.
In particular, for
\mathscr{W}_{L}=W(G_{2})
, we write it as
\{1,\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{l},\varepsilon_{c},\theta^{\prime},\theta^{\prime\prime}\}
,
where
\varepsilon
is the sign representation, and
\varepsilon_{l}
(resp.
\varepsilon_{c}
) is the
long sign representation (resp. short sign representation).
\theta^{\prime},\theta^{\prime\prime}
are irreducible
representations of degree 2, where
\theta^{\prime}
is the reflection representation of
W(G_{2})

(
\theta^{\prime}=\phi_{2,1}
in the table of Carter’s book
[
C
, p.412]
). Note that in
[
Sp5
]
,

\theta^{\prime}
and
\theta^{\prime\prime}
are not distinguished. Here in order to distinguish them,
we use the results of
[
L5
]
,
[
H
]
(see Introduction).

4.5.

By induction, we may assume that Conjecture 1.10 holds for any reductive
subgroup of
G
with smaller semisimple rank.
In particular, for any
(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\in\mathscr{M}_{G}^{F}
such that
L\neq G
,
a split element
u_{0}\in C_{0}^{F}
, and the split extension

\widetilde{\rho}_{0}
corresponding to
\varphi_{0}:F^{*}\mathscr{E}_{0}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\mathscr{E}_{0}
is already determined.

Let
T
be an
F
-stable maximal torus of
G
.
For a unipotent class
C
in
G
, let
\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)
be the subset of
\mathscr{M}_{G}

consisting of
(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})
with
T\subset L\neq G
, such that

(C,\mathscr{E})
belongs to
(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})
for some
\mathscr{E}
.
Note that
\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)
always contains
(T,\{1\},1)
,
which corresponds to the part of the Springer correspondence among the generalized
Springer correspondence (1.1.4).
Also note that we have excluded
(G,C,\mathscr{E})\in\mathscr{M}_{G}
for a cuspidal pair
(C,\mathscr{E})
of
G
.

We consider the following unipotent class.

(4.5.1)
G=E_{7}
and
C
is a unipotent class
of type
D_{6}(a_{1})+A_{1}
in
G
, with
p\neq 2
.

We shall prove the following result, which asserts
that Conjecture 1.10 holds if
G
is an almost simple group of
exceptional type, possibly except one class in (4.5.1).

Theorem 4.6
.

Let
G
be an almost simple, simply connected group of exceptional type,
and
C
an (
F
-stable) unipotent class in
G
.
Assume that
C
is not the class in (4.5.1).
Then there exists a split element
u\in C^{F}
in the sense of 1.7.
More precisely,

(i)

Assume that
F
is split on
G
.

(a)

There exists an element in
C^{F}
, unless

G
is
E_{8}
and
C=D_{8}(a_{3})
with
q\equiv-1\pmod{3}
, such that

\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u,\rho)=1
for any
\rho\in A_{G}(u)^{\wedge}
.

(b)

Assume that
G
is
E_{8}
and
C=D_{8}(a_{3})
with
q\equiv-1\pmod{3}
.
Then
A_{G}(u)=S_{3}
, and there exists an element
u\in C^{F}
such that

\gamma(u_{0},\rho_{0},u,\rho)=\begin{cases}1&\quad\text{ if }\rho\neq\varepsilon,\\
-1&\quad\text{ if }\rho=\varepsilon,\end{cases}

where
\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)=\{(T,\{1\},1)\}
, and
(u_{0},\rho_{0})=(1,1)
.

(ii)

Assume that
G
is of type
E_{6}
, and
F
is non-split
on
G
. There exists an element
u\in C^{F}
such that

(4.6.1)

\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u,\widetilde{\rho})=\nu_{u,\eta}(-1)^{\delta_{E}},

where
E=V_{(u,\rho)}\in\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\wedge}
, and
\delta_{E}
is defined as in (1.7.4).

\nu_{u,\eta}
is a root of unity determined by the class
C
and by
\eta=(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})
.
If
L=T
or
L
is of type
D_{4}
, then
\nu_{u,\eta}=1
, while if
L
is of type

2A_{2}
with
p\neq 3
, then
\nu_{u,\eta}
is determined from the discussion
in Section 2 (for
G
of type
A_{5}
and
L
of type
2A_{2}
).

4.7.

The computation of Green functions is a part of the computation of
generalized Green functions. As explained in the introduction,
Green functions of exceptional groups were computed for any
p
.
In computing Green functions, Beynon-Spaltenstein [BS]
gave a notion of split elements (a similar kind of notion already appears
in
[
Sh2
]
,
[
Sh3
]
,
where those elements are called distinguished elements). Here we discuss the relation
between their definition of split elements and ours.

Let
(u,\rho)\leftrightarrow(C,\mathscr{E})
be the pair belonging to the series
(T,\{1\},1)\in\mathscr{M}_{G}
.
Let
Y_{u,u^{\prime}}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{u,C^{\prime}}
be the varieties defined in (1.12.1).
We follow the setup in 1.13. In particular,
F
acts naturally on
Y_{u,u^{\prime}}
and on
\mathcal{Q}_{u,C^{\prime}}
.
We consider the case where
Q=MU_{Q}
is
B=TU
, and
u^{\prime}\in T
is 1.
Then
Y_{u,1}=\{g\in G\mid g^{-1}ug\in B\}
, and

\mathcal{Q}_{u,\{1\}}=\{gB\in G/B\mid g^{-1}ug\in B\}
coincides
with the fixed point subvariety
\mathscr{B}_{u}
of the flag variety
\mathscr{B}=G/B
.
Thus the set
X_{u,1}
coincides with the set of irreducible components
(they have the same dimension) of
\mathscr{B}_{u}
, and so
\varepsilon_{u,1}
can be identified
with
H^{2d_{u}}(\mathscr{B}_{u})=H^{2d_{u}}(\mathscr{B}_{u},\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{l})
,
with
d_{u}=\dim\mathscr{B}_{u}
, and
X_{u,1}
corresponds to the natural basis
of
H^{2d_{u}}(\mathscr{B}_{u})
.
By the Springer correspondence,
H^{2d_{u}}(\mathscr{B}_{u})
is decomposed as

W\times A_{G}(u)
-module as

H^{2d_{u}}(\mathscr{B}_{u})\simeq\bigoplus_{\rho\in A_{G}(u)^{\wedge}}V_{(u,\rho)}\otimes\rho.

This gives the
A_{G}(u)
-module structure of
\varepsilon_{u,1}
, and the action of

F
on
X_{u,1}
coincides with the multiplication by
q^{d_{u}}
followed by
the permutation of the basis.

Assume that
(G,F)
is split.
Beynon-Spaltenstein defined a split element
u\in C^{F}
by the condition that

F
acts trivially on
X_{u,1}
, and on
A_{G}(u)
. This condition determines
the
G^{F}
-class of split elements uniquely if it exists, in the case where

G
is adjoint type, but not necessarily so in the simply connected case.
(Note that
\mathscr{B}_{u}
depends only on its image to the adjoint group.)
Assume that
F
acts trivially on
A_{G}(u)
.
Then
V_{(u,\rho)}\otimes\rho

is an irreducible
W\times A_{G}(u)
-module, and
F
acts trivially on

W\times A_{G}(u)
, hence
F
acts on
the isotypic component
V_{(u,\rho)}\otimes\rho
in
H^{2d_{u}}(\mathscr{B}_{u})

by a scalar multiplication
cq^{d_{u}}
for some
c\in\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{l}^{*}
.
By applying Lemma 1.15 to the case where
(u_{0},\rho_{0})=(1,1)
is the cuspidal pair
in
T
, we have the following.

(4.7.1) Assume that
F
acts trivially on
A_{G}(u)
, and
that
(u,\rho)
appears in the Springer correspondence. Let
c
be as above.
Then we have
\gamma(1,1,u,\rho)=c
.
In particular, if
u
is split in the sense of Beynon-Spaltenstein,
then we have
\gamma(1,1,u,\rho)=1
for any
(u,\rho)
belonging to
(T,\{1\},1)
.

In the case where
|\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)|=1
, generalized
Green functions associated to
(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})
for
L\neq G

coincide with Green functions. Hence if
u
is split in the
sense of
[
BS
]
, it is split in our sense. In the case where
p
is good,
they proved the existence of split elements
u\in C^{F}
, unless

G=E_{8}
and
C=D_{8}(a_{3})
. In the exceptional case,
|\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)|=1
,
and
A_{G}(u)\simeq S_{3}
. Thus there exists
u\in C^{F}
, unique up to
G^{F}
-conjugate,
such that
F
acts trivially on
A_{G}(u)
. For this
u
, they have computed

\gamma(1,1,u,\rho)=\pm 1
explicitly, which is given as in
Theorem 4.6, (i), (b). Thus
u
is split in our sense.

In the case where
p
is bad, Green functions were computed by Geck
[
G2
]
and
Lübeck
[
Lü
]
. From those computations, there exists
u\in C^{F}
such that

\gamma(1,1,u,\rho)=1
if
F
is split on
G
,
and that
\gamma(1,1,u,\rho)=\pm 1
as in Theorem 4.6 (ii) for
G=E_{6}
with non split
F
.
Thus we have

(4.7.2) Assume that
\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)=\{(T,\{1\},1)\}
. Then there exists
a split element
u\in C^{F}
.

4.8.

In view of Lemma 1.15, it is important to know the structure of
X_{u,u^{\prime}}
and
the Frobenius action on it. (Note that if
F
acts trivially on
X_{u,u^{\prime}}
, then

F
acts trivially on
\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}(\rho,\rho^{\prime})
for any
\rho,\rho^{\prime}
. This condition is
more convenient than considering
\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}(\rho,\rho^{\prime})
separately.)

In general, it is not so easy to determine the structure of
X_{u,u^{\prime}}
.
But as discussed in
[
Sp2
]
, there are two cases, (III), and (IV) in
[
Sp2
]
,
where
X_{u,u^{\prime}}
can be described explicitly. Actually, the analysis of those cases
played a crucial role when Spaltenstein determined the generalized
Springer correspondence for exceptional groups (
[
Sp2
]
).
In those cases, we can also
determine the Frobenius action on
X_{u,u^{\prime}}
.

From now on, in the remainder of the paper, we follow the setup in 1.13.
In particular,
L\subset P
are
F
-stable, and

M\subset Q
are also
F
-stable such that
L\subset M
and
P\subset Q
.
Assume that
C
(resp.
C^{\prime}
) is a unipotent class in
G
(resp. in
M
).
Recall that the class
C
in
G
is induced from
the class
C^{\prime}
in
M
if
C\cap u^{\prime}U_{Q}
is open dense
in
u^{\prime}U_{Q}
for
u^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}
(
[
LS1
]
). In that case, we write it as
C=\operatorname{Ind}_{M}^{G}C^{\prime}
.

The following lemma corresponds to the property (III) in
[
Sp2
]
.

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
C
(resp.
C^{\prime}
) be an
F
-stable unipotent class in
G
(resp. in
M
).
Assume that
C
is induced from
C^{\prime}
. Take
u\in C^{F}
and
u^{\prime}\in C^{\prime F}

such that
u\in u^{\prime}U_{Q}^{F}
.

(i)

We have
Z_{Q}^{0}(u)=Z_{G}^{0}(u)\subset Z_{M}(u^{\prime})U_{Q}
.
Let
N=Z_{Q}(u)/Z_{G}^{0}(u)\subset A_{G}(u)
, and

H=(Z_{M}^{0}(u^{\prime})U_{Q}\cap Z_{G}(u))/Z_{G}^{0}(u)\subset N
. Then

H
is normal in
N
, and
N/H
is naturally isomorphic to
A_{M}(u^{\prime})
.
Moreover,
X_{u,u^{\prime}}\simeq A_{G}(u)/H
, and under this identification,
the action of
A_{G}(u)\times A_{M}(u^{\prime})
on
X_{u,u^{\prime}}
corresponds to
the action of
A_{G}(u)\times N/H
on
A_{G}(u)/H
;

(a,nH):xH\mapsto axn^{-1}H
(
a,x\in A_{G}(u),n\in N
).

(ii)

Assume that
F
acts trivially on
A_{G}(u)
.
Then
F
acts trivially on
X_{u,u^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

The proof is based on the discussion in
[
Sp2
, 1.3 (III)]
.
First we show (i).
From the discussion in
[
LS1
]
, the first formula follows, and

Z_{Q}(u)
meets all the irreducible components of
Z_{M}(u^{\prime})U_{Q}
.
Then
H
is normal in
N
, and
N/H
is naturally isomorphic to
A_{M}(u^{\prime})
.
The latter statement of (i) was proved in
[
Sp2
]
by using

[
LS1
, Thm. 1.3 (d)]
.

If
F
acts trivially on
A_{G}(u)
, then
F
acts trivially on
H
.
Then (ii) follows from (i) since the isomoprhism
X_{u,u^{\prime}}\simeq A_{G}(u)/H
is
compatible with
F
-actions.
∎

4.10.

Next we consider the situation where (IV) in
[
Sp2
]
can be applied.
Let
M\subset G
be as in 4.8.
Let
C
(resp.
C^{\prime}
) be an
F
-stable unipotent class in
G
(resp. in
M
).
Take
u\in C^{F}
and
u^{\prime}\in C^{\prime F}
. Here we consider the special situation
that
u=u^{\prime}\in M^{F}
.
The following lemma is an
F
-version of (IV) in
[
Sp2
, 1.4]
.

Lemma 4.11
.

Assume that
u=u^{\prime}\in C^{\prime F}
, and that

F
acts trivially on
A_{G}(u)
.

(i)

A_{M}(u)
is naturally regarded as a subgroup of
A_{G}(u)
, hence

F
acts trivially on
A_{M}(u)
.

(ii)

X_{u,u^{\prime}}
is isomorphic to
A_{G}(u)
, where
A_{G}(u)\times A_{M}(u^{\prime})

acts on
A_{G}(u)
by
(a,b):x\mapsto axb^{-1}
, (
a,x\in A_{G}(u),b\in A_{M}(u^{\prime})
).
In particular,
F
acts trivially on
X_{u,u^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

As discussed in
[
Sp2
, 1.4]
,
A_{M}(u)
can be naturally embedded in
A_{G}(u)
;
let
S
be the connected center of
M
.
Then
M=Z_{G}(S)
, and so
Z_{M}(u)=Z_{G}(S,u)=Z_{Z_{G}(u)}(S)
.
On the other hand,
S\subset Z_{G}^{0}(u)
and since the centralizer
of a torus in the connected group is connected,
Z_{Z_{G}^{0}(u)}(S)
is connected.
Hence we have
Z_{M}^{0}(u)=Z_{Z_{G}^{0}(u)}(S)
.
Thus

A_{M}(u)=Z_{Z_{G}(u)}(S)/Z_{Z_{G}^{0}(u)}(S)=(Z_{G}(u)\cap M)/(Z_{G}^{0}(u)\cap M)\hookrightarrow Z_{G}(u)/Z_{G}^{0}(u).

Hence
A_{M}(u)\hookrightarrow A_{G}(u)
, and (i) follows.
It is known by
[
Sp2
, Lemma 1.4 (i)]
that
Y_{u,u^{\prime}}=Z_{G}(u)U_{Q}
. Thus

Y_{u,u^{\prime}}=\bigcup_{\alpha\in A_{G}(u)}\alpha Z_{G}^{0}(u)U_{Q},

and irreducible components of
Y_{u,u^{\prime}}
are all
F
-stable. Hence
F
acts
trivially on
X_{u,u^{\prime}}
.
Moreover, it was proved in
[
Sp2
, 1.4]
, that

X_{u,u^{\prime}}
and
A_{G}(u)
are isomorphic, compatible with
F
-actions,
as stated in (ii).
∎

By Lemma 4.11, we obtain the following.

Proposition 4.12
.

Assume that
L\subset M\neq G
,
and choose
u=u^{\prime}
a split element in
C^{\prime F}
.
Assume that
F
acts trivially on
A_{G}(u)
.
Let
(u,\rho)
be a pair belonging to
(L,u_{0},\rho_{0})\in\mathscr{M}^{F}_{G}
.
Then there exists an extension
\widetilde{\rho}
of
\rho
such that

\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u,\widetilde{\rho})
is explicitly computable.
In particular if
F
is split on
G
, we have

\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u,\widetilde{\rho})=1
.

Proof.

Since
\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}
is isomorphic to the regular representation of
A_{G}(u)
by
Lemma 4.11 (ii),
there exists
(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})\in\mathscr{N}^{F}_{M}
such that

\rho\otimes\rho^{\prime*}
appears in
\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}
.
By assumption,
F
acts trivially on
A_{G}(u)
, hence
F
acts trivially on
A_{M}(u^{\prime})
.
Since
u^{\prime}
is split in
C^{\prime F}
, we have

\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u^{\prime},\widetilde{\rho}^{\prime})=\nu^{\prime}
for a certain extension
\widetilde{\rho}^{\prime}
of
\rho^{\prime}
,
where
\nu^{\prime}
is explicitly computable.
Since
F
acts trivially on
X_{u,u^{\prime}}
by Lemma 4.11,
there exists an extension
\widetilde{\rho}
, determined uniquely from
\widetilde{\rho}^{\prime}
as in 1.14,
and we have
\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u,\widetilde{\rho})=\delta^{-1}\nu^{\prime}
by Lemma 1.15.
In the case where
F
is split on
G
, then
F
is split on
M
.
Since
\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u^{\prime},\widetilde{\rho}^{\prime})=1
and
\delta=1
, we obtain

\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u,\widetilde{\rho})=1
.
∎

Let
\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)
be as in 4.5.
As a corollary to Proposition 4.12, we have

Lemma 4.13
.

Assume that
(G,F)
is split. Assume that
\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)
is contained in

\{(T,\{1\},1),(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\}
. Let
M
be an
F
-stable Levi subgroup
such that
T\subset L\subset M\subsetneq G
.
Assume that for some
u_{1}\in C^{F}
,
A_{G}(u_{1})
is abelian, and
F
acts trivially
on
A_{G}(u_{1})
.
Let
u=u^{\prime}
be a split element in
M^{F}
.
Then
u
is a split element in
G^{F}
.

Proof.

Since
A_{G}(u)
is abelian, by our assumption on
u_{1}
,

F
acts trivially on
A_{G}(u)
(see (4.1.1)). Thus Proposition 4.12
can be applied, and we see that
\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u,\rho)=1
for any

(u,\rho)
belonging to
(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})
. But this argument simultaneously
works also for
(u,\rho)
belonging to
(T,\{1\},1)
. Hence

\gamma(1,1,u,\rho)=1
for
(u,\rho)
as above, and
u
is a split element in
C^{F}
.
∎

Remarks 4.14.

(i) For almost all cases

\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)\subset\{(T,\{1\},1),(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\}
(here
(L,C_{0})
is fixed, but

\mathscr{E}_{0}
on
C_{0}
is not unique in general).
The exception is the case where
G=E_{6}
and
C=E_{6}
, with
p=2
,
or the case where
G=E_{8}
and

C=E_{7},E_{8}
, with
p=2
. In those cases, two types of Levi subgroups
L
and
L^{\prime}
containing
T

appear in
\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)
.

(ii) Assume that
\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)=\{(T,\{1\},1),(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0}),(L^{\prime},C_{0}^{\prime},\mathscr{E}_{0}^{\prime})\}

(here
\mathscr{E}_{0},\mathscr{E}^{\prime}_{0}
are not unique in general).
If
T\subset L\subset L^{\prime}\neq G
, then the discussion of Lemma 4.13 can be applied, and
one can find a split element in
C^{F}
.
This is applied for the case
G=E_{8}
.
But for the case
G=E_{6}
this does not hold, and we need an additional argument
to show the existence of split elements.

4.15.

In the case where there does not exist
M\neq G
such that

C\cap M\neq\emptyset
, Lemma 4.13 can not be
applied, and we need a more precise computation. The following lemma
is useful for such a situation.
Let
Q=MU_{Q}
be as in 1.12 with
M\neq G
, and
C
(resp.
C^{\prime}
) be an
F
-stable unipotent
class in
G
(resp. in
M
). Take
u\in C^{F}
, and
u^{\prime}\in C^{\prime F}
, and assume that

(u,\rho)
belongs to
(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})
. Recall that
\mathscr{W}_{L}=N_{G}(L)/L
and

\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\prime}=N_{M}(L)/L
. We consider
V_{(u,\rho)}\in\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\wedge}
, and

V_{(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})}\in(\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\prime})^{\wedge}
for
\rho^{\prime}\in A_{M}(u^{\prime})^{\wedge}
.

Lemma 4.16
.

Under the notation in 4.15,
assume that the following conditions are satisfied.

(i)

V_{(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})}
appears in the restriction of
V_{(u,\rho)}
on
\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\prime}
.

(ii)

1\otimes 1
appears in
\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}
with multiplicity 1.

(iii)

\dim Z_{G}(u)=\dim Z_{M}(u^{\prime})
.

Assume that
u^{\prime}\in C^{\prime F}
is a split element such that
F
acts trivially on

A_{M}(u^{\prime})
. Take
u\in G^{F}
such that
Y_{u,u^{\prime}}^{F}\neq\emptyset
.
If
F
acts trivially on
A_{G}(u)
, then

\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u,\widetilde{\rho})=\delta^{-1}\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u^{\prime},\widetilde{\rho}^{\prime})
,
where
\delta
is as in Lemma 1.15.

Proof.

Since
V_{(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})}
appears in the restriction of
V_{(u,\rho)}
,
by the restriction formula (1.12.3),
\rho\otimes\rho^{\prime*}
appears in
\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}

with non-zero multiplicity. Since
1\otimes 1
appears in
\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}
with multiplicity 1,

A_{G}(u)\times A_{M}(u^{\prime})
acts transitively on
X_{u,u^{\prime}}
.
By (1.12.2),
\dim\mathcal{Q}_{u,C^{\prime}}\leq(\dim Z_{G}(u)-\dim Z_{M}(u^{\prime})/2
. Thus
the condition
\dim Z_{G}(u)=\dim Z_{M}(u^{\prime})
implies that
\dim\mathcal{Q}_{u,C^{\prime}}=0
.
Since
Y_{u,u^{\prime}}/Z_{M}(u^{\prime})U_{Q}\simeq\mathcal{Q}_{u,C^{\prime}}
,
Z_{M}^{0}(u^{\prime})U_{Q}
gives an irreducible
component of
Y_{u,u^{\prime}}
of dimension
d_{u,u^{\prime}}
.
Since
Y_{u,u^{\prime}}^{F}\neq\emptyset
, there exists
g\in G^{F}
such that

g^{-1}ug\in u^{\prime}U_{Q}
. Then
gZ_{M}^{0}(u^{\prime})U_{Q}\in X_{u,u^{\prime}}
, and

(4.16.1)

Y_{u,u^{\prime}}=\bigcup_{a\in A_{G}(u),b\in A_{M}(u^{\prime})}agbZ_{M}^{0}(u^{\prime})U_{Q}

gives a decomposition of
Y_{u,u^{\prime}}
into irreducible components, all of which
have the same dimension
d_{u,u^{\prime}}
.
Here
gZ_{M}^{0}(u^{\prime})U_{Q}\in X_{u,u^{\prime}}
is
F
-stable.
Since
F
acts trivially on
A_{G}(u)
and on
A_{M}(u^{\prime})
,
F
acts trivially on
X_{u,u^{\prime}}

by (4.16.1). Then Lemma 1.15 can be applied, and the lemma follows.
∎

4.17.

In later sections, we discuss each case of exceptional groups separately.
First we treat the case where
G
is not
E_{6}
, and after that
discuss the case
G=E_{6}
. This is because
in the case of
E_{6}
, the Frobenius actions on
A_{G}(u)
are more complicated
than in other cases (see 4.1).

5.
The case
E_{7}

5.1.

In this section, we assume that
G
is simply connected of type
E_{7}
.
In this case,
A_{G}(u)\simeq S_{3}\times\mathbf{Z}_{(2,p-1)}
if

C
is
D_{6}(a_{2})+A_{1}
, and
A_{G}(u)\simeq S_{3}
if
C=D_{4}(a_{1})
.
In all other cases,
A_{G}(u)
is abelian.

Let
(3A_{1})^{\prime\prime}
be the Dynkin diagram of the following type
(corresponding to white nodes),

(5.1.1)

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\kern-3.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern The set
\mathscr{M}_{G}
consists of

(T,\{1\},\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{l})
and
(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})
, together with cuspidal ones, where

L
is a Levi subgroup of type
(3A_{1})^{\prime\prime}
if
p\neq 2
, and of type
D_{4}
if
p=2
.

5.2.

For a given class
C
, we shall find a split element
u\in C^{F}
.
First consider the cases where Lemma 4.13 can be applied.
Consider the unipotent class
C
in the list

(5.2.1)

\displaystyle E_{7},\quad E_{7}(a_{1}),\quad E_{7}(a_{2}),\quad D_{6}+A_{1},\quad D_{6}(a_{1})+A_{1},\quad D_{6}(a_{2})+A_{1},\quad D_{4}(a_{1}).

In those cases, Lemma 4.13 cannot be applied;
in the former four cases, there does not exist a proper Levi subgroup

M
such that
C\cap M\neq\emptyset
, and
if
C=D_{6}(a_{2})+A_{1}
or
D_{4}(a_{1})
, then
A_{G}(u)
is not abelian.

Assume that
C
is in the remaining cases.
First assume that
p\neq 2
. In this case,
L
is of type
(3A_{1})^{\prime\prime}
.
If
|\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)|=2
, then
C
is in the list

\displaystyle D_{6},\quad D_{6}(a_{1}),\quad D_{5}+A_{1},\quad D_{6}(a_{2}),\quad(A_{5}+A_{1})^{\prime\prime},

\displaystyle D_{5}(a_{1})+A_{1},\quad A_{5}^{\prime\prime},\quad D_{4}+A_{1},\quad A_{3}+A_{2}+A_{1},\quad D_{4}(a_{1})+A_{1},

\displaystyle A_{3}+2A_{1},\quad(A_{3}+A_{1})^{\prime\prime},\quad A_{2}+3A_{1},\quad 4A_{1},\quad 3A_{1}^{\prime\prime}.

In those cases, one can find
M\supset L
such that
M\cap C\neq\emptyset
,
hence Lemma 4.13 can be applied. For other cases, since
|\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)|=1
,
there is no restriction on
M
, and so Lemma 4.13 can be applied (or (4.7.2) can be
applied).

Next assume that
p=2
. In this case
L
is of type
D_{4}
.
If
|\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)|=2
, then
C
is in the list

\displaystyle E_{6},\quad D_{6},\quad D_{6}(a_{1}),\quad D_{5}+A_{1},\quad D_{5},\quad D_{4}+A_{1},\quad D_{4}.

In those cases, one can find
M\supset L
such that
M\cap C\neq\emptyset
,
hence Lemma 4.13 can be applied. For other cases , since
|\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)|=1
,
Lemma 4.13 or (4.7.2) can be applied.

Thus except the classes listed in (5.2.1), Lemma 4.13 can be applied for
C
,
and one can find a split element in
C^{F}
.

5.3.

We consider the case where
C=E_{7},E_{7}(a_{1}),E_{7}(a_{2}),D_{6}+A_{1}
or
D_{6}(a_{1})+A_{1}
,
and apply Lemma 4.9 or Lemma 4.16 for it.

(1) Assume that
C=E_{7}
. Then
C
is a regular unipotent class in
G
,
and for
u\in C
,
A_{G}(u)\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{2(6,p)}
.
Assume that
(u,\rho)
belongs to
(L,u_{0},\rho_{0})\in\mathscr{M}_{G}
with
L\neq G
.
Then
V_{(u,\rho)}
is the trivial representation of
\mathscr{W}_{L}
.
Here
C=\operatorname{Ind}_{T}^{G}1
. Let
M
be a Levi subgroup of type
D_{6}
.
and put
C^{\prime}=\operatorname{Ind}_{T}^{M}1
. Then
C^{\prime}
is a regular unipotent class
in
M
. By Lemma 4.9,
A_{M}(u^{\prime})
is a subquotient of
A_{G}(u)
for
u^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}
.
Assume that
(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
belongs
(L,u_{0},\rho_{0})\in\mathscr{M}_{M}
.
Then
V_{(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})}
is the trivial
representation of
\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\prime}
. In particular,
V_{(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})}
appears in the restriction
of
V_{(u,\rho)}
to
\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\prime}
.

We choose a split element
u^{\prime}\in C^{\prime F}
, and take
u\in C^{F}
such that

u\in u^{\prime}U_{Q}
. Then
F
acts trivially on
A_{G}(u)
(note that
A_{G}(u)
is abelian)
and on
A_{M}(u^{\prime})
. By applying Lemma 4.9,
F
acts trivially on
X_{u,u^{\prime}}
.
Thus by Lemma 1.15,
\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u,\rho)=1
(in the notation
of Lemma 1.15, we have
\delta=1,\nu^{\prime}=1
since
F
is split on
G
and on
M
).
This holds simultaneously for
L
and
T
. Thus
u\in C^{F}
is a split element.

(2) Assume that
C=E_{7}(a_{1})
.
Then
A_{G}(u)\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{2}
, and
\dim Z_{G}(u)=9
.
Assume that
p\neq 2
. Then
L
is of type
(3A_{1})^{\prime\prime}
, and
\mathscr{W}_{L}=W(F_{4})
.
We have
V_{(u,-1)}=\chi_{2,1}\in\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\wedge}
. Consider the Levi subgroup
M
of type

D_{5}+A_{1}
. Then
\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\prime}=N_{M}(L)/L
corresponds to the subgroup of

W(F_{4})
of type
C_{3}
. The restriction
\chi_{2,1}|_{\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\prime}}
coincides with
the irreducible representation
\chi^{\prime}
of type
(12;-)
of
W(C_{3})
. Choose
u^{\prime}\in M
such that

V_{(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})}
coincides with
\chi^{\prime}
.
Then the Jordan type of
u^{\prime}
in
M
is equal to
((37),(2))
.
We have
A_{M}(u^{\prime})\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{2}
, and
\dim Z_{M}(u^{\prime})=9
.
The
A_{G}(u)\times A_{M}(u^{\prime})
-module
\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}
is decomposed as

(5.3.1)

\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}=(1\otimes 1)\oplus(-1\otimes-1).

Let
C^{\prime}
be the unipotent class in
M
containing
u^{\prime}
. We choose a split element

u^{\prime}\in C^{\prime F}
, and take
u\in C^{F}
such that
Y_{u,u^{\prime}}^{F}\neq\emptyset
.
Then by Lemma 4.16,
\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u,-1)=1
(note that
F
acts trivially on
A_{G}(u)

since
A_{G}(u)
is abelian). We also have

\gamma(1,1,u,1)=1
by (5.3.1).
Hence
u
is a split element.

In the case where
p=2
,
L
is of type
D_{4}
, and
\mathscr{W}_{L}=W(B_{3})
.
We consider
V_{(u,-1)}=(-;3)\in\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\wedge}
.
Let
M
be a Levi subgroup of type
D_{6}
. Then
\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\prime}=W(B_{2})
.
Let
C^{\prime}\subset M
be the class
((48),(11))
.
Then for
u^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}
, we have
A_{M}(u^{\prime})\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{2}
, and
\dim Z_{M}(u^{\prime})=9
.
The restriction of
(-;3)
on
\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\prime}
coincides with
(-;2)=V_{(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})}
,
and
\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}
can be decomposed as in (5.3.1).
Hence by a similar discussion using Lemma 4.16 as above,
one can find a split element
u\in C^{F}
.

(3) Assume that
C=E_{7}(a_{2})
. Then
A_{G}(u)\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{2}
, and
\dim Z_{G}(u)=11
.
Assume that
p\neq 2
. Then
V_{(u,-1)}\in\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\wedge}
corresponds to

\chi_{4,1}\in W(F_{4})^{\wedge}
.
Let
M
be a Levi subgroup of type
D_{5}+A_{1}
as in (2). Then

\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\prime}=W(C_{3})
, and we have
\chi_{4,1}|_{\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\prime}}=(3;-)+(2;1)
.
We choose
u^{\prime}\in M
of Jordan type
((1117),(2))
. Then
A_{M}(u^{\prime})\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{2}
, and

\dim Z_{M}(u^{\prime})=11
. We have
V_{(u^{\prime},-1)}=(2;1)
in
\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\prime}
, and

\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}
is decomposed as
in (5.3.1).
Hence by Lemma 4.16, a split element
u\in C^{F}
exists.

Next assume that
p=2
with
C=E_{7}(a_{2})
.
Then
L
is of type
D_{4}
, and
\mathscr{W}_{L}=W(B_{3})
. We have
A_{G}(u)\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{2}
,
and
V_{(u,-1)}=(2;1)\in\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\wedge}
. Let
M
be a Levi subgroup of type
D_{5}+A_{1}
.
Then
\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\prime}=W(\widetilde{A}_{1}+A_{1})
.
Consider
u^{\prime}
in
M
of type
((1126),(11)),(2))
.
(Here we follow the notation of
[
LS2
, 2.1]
for describing the
unipotent classes in

with

;
(1126)
is a partition of 10,
and

denotes
\varepsilon(2)=\varepsilon(6)=1
.)
Then
A_{M}(u^{\prime})\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{2}
, and
V_{(u^{\prime},-1)}
is contained in the restriction of

V_{(u,-1)}
. We have
\dim Z_{M}(u^{\prime})=11
, and
\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}
is decomposed as
in (5.3.1).
Hence by Lemma 4.16, a split element

exists.

(4) Assume that
C=D_{6}+A_{1}
with
p\neq 2
. Then
A_{G}(u)\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{2}\times\mathbf{Z}_{2}
,
and
\dim Z_{G}(u)=13
. Here
L
is of type
(3A_{1})^{\prime\prime}
and
\mathscr{W}_{L}=W(F_{4})
.
We have
V_{(u,\varepsilon^{\prime})}=\chi_{8,1}
and
V_{(u,\varepsilon^{\prime\prime})}=\chi_{1,2}
.
Let
M
be a Levi subgroup of type
D_{5}+A_{1}
, then
\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\prime}=W(C_{3})
.
Take
C^{\prime}\subset M
of Jordan type
((1135),(2))
. Then for
u^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}
,
we have
A_{M}(u^{\prime})\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{2}\times\mathbf{Z}_{2}
, and
\dim Z_{M}(u^{\prime})=13
.
We have a decomposition

(5.3.2)

\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}=(1\otimes 1)\oplus(\varepsilon\otimes\varepsilon)\oplus(\varepsilon^{\prime}\otimes\varepsilon^{\prime})\oplus(\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}\otimes\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}).

Then by Lemma 4.16, a split element
u\in C^{F}
exits.

(5) Assume that
C=D_{6}(a_{1})+A_{1}
with
p\neq 2
.
Note that this is the excluded case (4.5.1). As noticed below,
Lemma 4.16 can not be applied for this case.

A_{G}(u)\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{2}\times\mathbf{Z}_{2}
, and
\dim Z_{G}(u)=17
.
Here
L
is of type
(3A_{1})^{\prime\prime}
, and
\mathscr{W}_{L}=W(F_{4})
.
We have
V_{(u,\varepsilon^{\prime})}=\chi_{4,2}
and
V_{(u,\varepsilon^{\prime\prime})}=x_{2,3}
in
W(F_{4})
.
(Note that the table of the generalized Springer correspondence for
G=E_{7}
in

[
Sp2
]
has an error, which was corrected by Geck; in
[
Sp2
]
it
was given as
V_{(u,\varepsilon^{\prime\prime})}=\chi_{8,3}
and
V_{(u_{1},-1)}=\chi_{2,3}
for

u_{1}\in C_{1}=D_{5}+A_{1}
in
M
. This should be switched so that

V_{(u,\varepsilon^{\prime\prime})}=\chi_{2,3}
and
V_{(u_{1},-1)}=\chi_{8,3}
.)

Let
M
be a Levi subgroup of type
D_{6}
. Then
\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\prime}=W(B_{3})
.
We have
\chi_{4,2}|_{W(B_{3})}=(1;2)+(-;3)
, and
\chi_{2,3}|_{W(B_{3})}=(12;-)
.
In the Springer correspondence in
M
,
(1;2)
occurs in the class
C_{1}^{\prime}=(66;+)
,

(-;3)
occurs in
C_{2}^{\prime}=(39)
, and
(12;-)
occurs in
C_{3}^{\prime}=(1227)
.
Take
u_{1}^{\prime}\in C_{1}^{\prime},u_{2}^{\prime}\in C_{2}^{\prime},u_{3}^{\prime}\in C_{3}^{\prime}
. Then for
u^{\prime}=u_{1}^{\prime},u_{2}^{\prime}
or
u_{3}^{\prime}
,
we have
A_{M}(u^{\prime})\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{2}
,
and
\dim Z_{M}(u_{1}^{\prime})=13,\dim Z_{M}(u_{2}^{\prime})=9,\dim Z_{M}(u_{3}^{\prime})=15
.
We have
V_{(u_{1}^{\prime},-1)}=(1;2)
,
V_{(u_{2}^{\prime},-1)}=(-;3)
and
V_{(u^{\prime}_{3},-1)}=(21;-)
.
Moreover,

\varepsilon_{u,u_{1}^{\prime}}=(1\otimes 1)\oplus(\varepsilon^{\prime}\otimes-1),\quad\varepsilon_{u,u_{2}^{\prime}}=(1\otimes 1)\oplus(\varepsilon^{\prime}\otimes-1),

\varepsilon_{u,u_{3}^{\prime}}=(1\otimes 1)\oplus(\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}\otimes-1).

But since
\dim Z_{G}(u)>\dim Z_{M}(u^{\prime})
, Lemma 4.16 cannot be applied.
Actually, for any unipotent class
C^{\prime}
of any Levi subgroup
M
such that

V_{(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})}
appears in the restriction of
V_{(u,\rho)}
, we have

\dim Z_{G}(u)>\dim Z_{M}(u^{\prime})
. Hence there does not exist a pair
(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
such that
Lemma 4.16 can be applied.

Later in 6.3 (3), (6), we discuss the case where Lemma 4.16 can not be applied,
but still the existence of split elements is proved.
The discussion there does not hold in the present case, since we need to compare

A_{G}(u)\times A_{M}(u_{1}^{\prime})
-module
\varepsilon_{u,u_{1}^{\prime}}
and
A_{G}(u)\times A_{M}(u_{3}^{\prime})
-module

\varepsilon_{u,u_{3}^{\prime}}
. We can only show that there exists
u\in C^{F}
such that

\displaystyle\gamma(1,1,u,1)=1,\quad\gamma(1,1,u,\varepsilon)=1,\quad\gamma(u_{0},\rho_{0},u,\varepsilon^{\prime})=1,\quad\gamma(u_{0},\rho_{0},u,\varepsilon^{\prime\prime})=\pm 1.

From the above discussion, for
C=E_{7},E_{7}(a_{1}),E_{7}(a_{2}),D_{6}+A_{1},D_{6}(a_{1})+A_{1}

(possibly except
D_{6}(a_{1})+A_{1}
), a split element
u\in C^{F}
exists.

5.4.

We consider the case where
C=D_{4}(a_{1})
. In this case,
A_{G}(u)\simeq S_{3}

for
u\in C
. Since
|\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)|=1
, there exists a split element
u\in C^{F}

by (4.7.2).

5.5.

Finally consider the case where
C=D_{6}(a_{2})+A_{1}
.
In this case,
A_{G}(u)=S_{3}\times\mathbf{Z}_{(2,p-1)}
.
Then
\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)=\{(T,\{1\},1),(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\}

with
L
of type
(3A_{1})^{\prime\prime}
if
p\neq 2
, and

\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)=\{(T,\{1\},\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{l})\}
if
p=2
.
Thus by (4.7.2), we have only to consider the case where
p\neq 2
.

In this case, it is also possible to find a suitable Levi subgroup
M

and a unipotent class
C^{\prime}
in
M
, with
u^{\prime}\in C
, such that the condition
of Lemma 4.16 is satisfied for
\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}
. But since
A_{G}(u)
is not abelian,
if we choose a split element
u^{\prime}\in C^{\prime F}
, and take an element
u\in u^{\prime}U_{Q}

such that
Y_{u,u^{\prime}}^{F}\neq\emptyset
, one cannot check whether
F
acts trivially
on
A_{G}(u)
, and the discussion as in 5.3 breaks.
Thus in this case, we take the other way round; first we choose
u\in C^{F}

such that
F
acts trivially on
A_{G}(u)
, then define
u^{\prime}\in C^{\prime F}
from
u

for a certain Levi subgroup
M
. We check that
u^{\prime}
is a split element in
C^{\prime F}
,
and verify that
\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}
satisfies the condition in Lemma 4.16.
This is a similar way as Beynon-Spaltenstein determined
\gamma(1,1,u,\rho)
in the
case where
C
is the exceptional class in
G=E_{8}
.

We choose
u\in C^{F}
following
[
M2
, Table 2]
as follows.

(5.5.1)

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=x_{14}(1)x_{15}(1)x_{27}(1)x_{17}(1)x_{18}(1)x_{19}(1)x_{23}(1)

\displaystyle=x_{\alpha}(1)x_{3+2}(1)x_{\beta}(1)x_{4+1}(1)x_{5-2}(1)x_{6-3}(1)x_{5-1}(1),

where
\alpha=-1-2+3-4-5-6-7+8,\beta=-1-2-3-4+5-6-7+8
.
Here the first expression is due to Mizuno (
u=y_{47}
in Lemma 21 in
[
M2
]
),
and the second one is given by the root subgroup expression
x_{\alpha}(1)
for

\alpha\in\varDelta^{+}
in 4.2,
where we use a convention such as

\frac{1}{2}(\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2}-\cdots-\varepsilon_{7}+\varepsilon_{8})=1-2-\cdots-7+8
,
\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}=1+2
,
and so on.

We have
A_{G}(u)\simeq S_{3}\times\mathbf{Z}_{2}
, and
it is checked by [M2] that
F
acts trivially on
A_{G}(u)
.
Let
Q=MU_{Q}
be a parabolic subgroup of
G
such that

M
is a Levi subgroup of type
D_{6}
.
Then
M
contains
L
of type
(3A_{1})^{\prime\prime}
.
Put

(5.5.2)

u^{\prime}=x_{3+2}(1)x_{4+1}(1)x_{5-2}(1)x_{6-3}(1)x_{5-1}(1).

Then
u^{\prime}\in M^{F}
, and
u
is written as
u\in u^{\prime}U_{Q}
.
Let
C^{\prime}
be the unipotent class in
M
containing
u^{\prime}
.

In the discussion below, we follow the notation in Section 3.
Evaluating
u^{\prime}
in the natural 12 dimensional representation of
M

through the computation by CHEVIE,
we obtain a matrix of Jordan type
(2244)
. Now
M/Z^{0}M
is a quotient of
\widetilde{M}=\operatorname{Spin}_{12}
,
and for the element
v\in\operatorname{Spin}_{12}

such that
\beta(v)=\overline{v}\in SO_{12}
has Jordan type
(2244)
, we have

A_{\widetilde{M}}(v)\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{2}
by
[
L1
, 14.3]
(since
I=\emptyset
in the notation
there). It follows that

A_{M}(u^{\prime})\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{2}
or
A_{M}(u^{\prime})=\{1\}
.
We consider the variety
Y_{u,u^{\prime}}
, and the set
X_{u,u^{\prime}}
.
We show a lemma.

Lemma 5.6
.

Under the notation above,

(i)

A_{G}(u)\times A_{M}(u^{\prime})
acts transitively on
X_{u,u^{\prime}}
.

(ii)

A_{M}(u^{\prime})\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{2}
.

(iii)

The decomposition of
\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}
is given by

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=(1\otimes 1)\oplus(\theta\otimes 1)\oplus(-1\otimes-1)\oplus(-\theta\otimes-1)

for
A_{G}(u)^{\wedge}=(S_{3}\times\mathbf{Z}_{2})^{\wedge}=\{1,\theta,\varepsilon,-1,-\theta,-\varepsilon\}

and
A_{M}(u^{\prime})^{\wedge}=\{1,-1\}
.

Proof.

We have
\dim Z_{G}(u)=21
by
[
M2
]
, and
\dim Z_{M}(u^{\prime})=21
.
It follows that
\dim\mathcal{Q}_{u,C^{\prime}}=0
.
We have
V_{(u,1)}=315_{7}
. On the other hand,
for
W(D_{6})
, we have
V_{(u^{\prime},1)}=(\lambda;\lambda)_{+}
or
(\lambda;\lambda)_{-}
,
where
\lambda=(12)
is a partition of
3
, and
\pm
correspond to the
irreducible representation of
W(D_{6})
appearing in the restriction of
the irreducible representation
(\lambda;\lambda)\in W(B_{6})^{\wedge}
.
By a direct computation, we see that the condition

\operatorname{\!\langle\!}V_{(u,1)},V_{(u^{\prime},1)}\operatorname{\!\rangle\!}_{\,W(D_{6})}\neq 0

determines
V_{(u^{\prime},1)}
uniquely, and we have

\operatorname{\!\langle\!}V_{(u,1)},V_{(u^{\prime},1)}\operatorname{\!\rangle\!}_{\,W(D_{6})}=1
.
Hence the multiplicity of

1\otimes 1
in
\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}
is equal to 1, and as in the proof of Lemma 4.16,
we see that
A_{G}(u)\times A_{M}(u^{\prime})
acts transitively on
X_{u,u^{\prime}}
.
Hence (i) holds.

Next we show (ii). By (i),
Z_{G}(u)
acts transitively on
\mathcal{Q}_{u,C^{\prime}}
.
Since the stabilizer of
Q
in
Z_{G}(u)
coincides with
Z_{Q}(u)
, we have

\mathcal{Q}_{u,C^{\prime}}\simeq Z_{G}(u)/Z_{Q}(u)
as finite sets.
By
[
M2
, Lemma 21]
,
Z_{G}(u)\subset B\cup BwB
, where
w=s_{1}s_{2}s_{6}
.
Since
BwB\cap M=\emptyset
, we have
Z_{Q}(u)=Z_{B}(u)
.
By a direct computation, we see that
Z_{B}(u)=Z_{G}Z_{U}(u)
.
By [M2],
Z_{G}(u)\simeq S_{3}\times\mathbf{Z}_{2}
and

Z_{G}^{0}(u)=Z_{U}(u)
.
Hence
Z_{Q}(u)=Z_{G}Z^{0}_{G}(u)
, and we have

Z_{G}(u)/Z_{Q}(u)\simeq S_{3}
. It follows, that

(5.6.1)

\dim\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}=|Z_{G}(u)Q|=|S_{3}|=6.

Now by assuming that
A_{M}(u^{\prime})=\{1\}
, we will have a contradiction.
By
[
Sp2
]
, the generalized Springer correspondence for
C
is
given as follows;

(5.6.2)

\displaystyle V_{(u,\rho)}=\begin{cases}315_{7}&\quad\text{ for }\rho=1,\\
280_{9}&\quad\text{ for }\rho=\theta,\\
35_{13}&\quad\text{ for }\rho=\varepsilon,\\
\end{cases}\qquad V_{(u,\rho)}=\begin{cases}\chi_{12}&\quad\text{ for }\rho=-1,\\
\chi_{6,2}&\quad\text{ for }\rho=-\theta,\end{cases}

where the former is related to
\mathscr{W}_{T}=W(E_{7})
,
and the latter is related to
\mathscr{W}_{L}=W(F_{4})
.
Note that
(u,-\varepsilon)
is a cuspidal pair for
G
.

Suppose that
A_{M}(u^{\prime})=\{1\}
. Then the decomposition of
\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}

is only concerned with the Springer correspondence, and we have

(5.6.3)

\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}=\operatorname{\!\langle\!}V_{(u,1)},V_{(u^{\prime},1)}\operatorname{\!\rangle\!}\,(1\otimes 1)\oplus\operatorname{\!\langle\!}V_{(u,\theta)},V_{(u^{\prime},1)}\operatorname{\!\rangle\!}\,(\theta\otimes 1)\oplus\operatorname{\!\langle\!}V_{(u,\varepsilon)},V_{(u^{\prime},1)}\operatorname{\!\rangle\!}\,(\varepsilon\otimes 1).

Since

\operatorname{\!\langle\!}315_{7},V_{(u^{\prime},1)}\operatorname{\!\rangle\!}=1,\ \operatorname{\!\langle\!}280_{9},V_{(u^{\prime},1)}\operatorname{\!\rangle\!}=1,\ \operatorname{\!\langle\!}35_{13},V_{(u^{\prime},1)}\operatorname{\!\rangle\!}=0
,
we have
\dim\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}=\dim(1\oplus\theta)=3
. This contradicts (5.6.1),
and we obtain
A_{M}(u^{\prime})\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{2}
. (ii) is proved.

Finally we show (iii).
The subgroup
\mathscr{W}^{\prime}_{L}=N_{M}(L)/L
of
\mathscr{W}_{L}
is of type
B_{3}
.
Hence the generalized Springer correspondence for
u^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}
is given by

V_{(u^{\prime},1)}\in W(D_{6})^{\wedge},\qquad V_{(u^{\prime},-1)}\in W(B_{3})^{\wedge},

where
\rho^{\prime}=\pm 1\in A_{M}(u^{\prime})^{\wedge}
.
Now (iii) follows easily from the restriction formula by computing the
multiplicity of
V_{(u^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})}
in the restriction of
V_{(u,\rho)}
.
∎

5.7.

Note that the center of the spin group
\operatorname{Spin}_{12}

is isomorphic to
\mathbf{Z}_{2}\times\mathbf{Z}_{2}
, generated by
\omega
and
\varepsilon

(see Section 3). The half spin group
\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{Spin}_{12}

is defined as the quotient group
\operatorname{Spin}_{12}/\operatorname{\!\langle\!}\omega\operatorname{\!\rangle\!}
.
As
A_{M}(u^{\prime})\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{2}
by Lemma 5.6, the almost simple group

\overline{M}=M/Z^{0}M
is isomorphic to

\operatorname{Spin}_{12}
or
\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{Spin}_{12}
(since
A_{M}(u^{\prime})\simeq A_{\overline{M}}(u^{\prime})
, and
the centralizer of the element with Jordan type
(2244)
in
SO_{12}
is connected).
On the other hand, since the center of
\overline{M}
is isomorphic to
\mathbf{Z}_{2}
, we see
that
\overline{M}\simeq\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{Spin}_{12}
.

Since
A_{M}(u^{\prime})=\mathbf{Z}_{2}
,
C^{\prime F}
splits into two
M^{F}
-orbits.
Exactly one of them is a split class in
C^{\prime F}
.
In the case where
u^{\prime}
is split, we put
u_{1}^{\prime}=u^{\prime}
and
u_{1}=u
.
Assume that
u^{\prime}
is not split. In this case,
following the discussion in Section 3, we modify
u
and
u^{\prime}
as follows.
Now the center
Z_{G}
of
G
is
\mathbf{Z}_{2}
. We denote by
z

the generator of
Z_{G}
. Then
z\in Z_{M}(u^{\prime})
, and its image
gives the generator of
A_{M}(u^{\prime})
. Choose
h\in M
such that

h^{-1}F(h)=z
, and put
u_{1}^{\prime}=hu^{\prime}h^{-1}
. Then
u^{\prime}
and
u_{1}^{\prime}

give representatives of
M^{F}
-orbits in
C^{\prime F}
, and so
u_{1}^{\prime}\in C^{\prime F}

is a split element. We define
u_{1}
by
u_{1}=huh^{-1}
. Since
z\in Z_{G}(u)
,
we have
u_{1}\in C^{F}
. Since
z
is a central element,
F
acts trivially
on
A_{G}(u_{1})
.
Define
Y_{u_{1},u_{1}^{\prime}}
similarly to
Y_{u,u^{\prime}}
. Then
g\mapsto hgh^{-1}

gives an isomorphism
Y_{u,u^{\prime}}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,Y_{u_{1},u_{1}^{\prime}}
, which is compatible with
the actions of
A_{G}(u)\times A_{M}(u^{\prime})
and
A_{G}(u_{1})\times A_{M}(u_{1}^{\prime})
.
The following result proves the existence of split elements for

C=D_{6}(a_{2})+A_{1}
.

Proposition 5.8
.

Assume that
p\neq 2
, and
C=D_{6}(a_{2})+A_{1}
.
Let
u_{1},u_{1}^{\prime}
be as in 5.7, which we denote by

u,u^{\prime}
.
Then

(i)

F
acts trivially on
A_{G}(u)
and
A_{M}(u^{\prime})
. Furthermore,

F
acts trivially on
X_{u,u^{\prime}}
.

(ii)

u\in C^{F}
is a split element.

Proof.

By the construction of
u
and
u^{\prime}
,
F
acts trivially on

A_{G}(u)
and on
A_{M}(u^{\prime})
.
By a similar discussion as in the proof of Lemma 4.16,
Lemma 5.6 implies that
F
acts trivially on
X_{u,u^{\prime}}
.
Thus (i) holds.

We show (ii).
By our construction,
u^{\prime}
is a split element in
C^{\prime F}
.
By Lemma 4.16, we see that

\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u,\rho)=1
for
(u,\rho)
belonging to

(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})
or to
(T,\{1\},1)
, whenever
\rho\otimes\rho^{\prime*}

appears in
\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}
.
Hence by Lemma 5.6 (iii), we have

\gamma(1,1,u,1)=\gamma(1,1,u,\theta)=1,\quad\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u,-1)=\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u,-\theta)=1.

It remains to show that
\gamma(1,1,u,\varepsilon)=1
.
But since this is only related to the Springer correspondence,
we may replace
G
by its adjoint group
\overline{G}
, and
u
by its image
\overline{u}\in\overline{G}
.
Then
F
acts trivially on
A_{\overline{G}}(\overline{u})\simeq S_{3}
, and
\overline{u}
belongs to the
unique
\overline{G}^{F}
-orbit satisfying this property.
It is proved in
[
BS
]
that
\overline{u}
is a split element in the sense of
Beynon-Spaltenstein. Hence by (4.7.1), we have
\gamma(1,1,u,\varepsilon)=1
.
Thus
u\in C^{F}
is a split element.
∎

5.9.

Summing up the above discussion, we have proved that Theorem 4.6 holds for

G=E_{7}
.

6.
The case
E_{8}

6.1.

In this section, we assume that
G
is of type
E_{8}
.
In this case,
A_{G}(u)
is isomorphic to
D_{8}
for
C=D_{8}(a_{1})
(the case
p=2
),
to
S_{5}
for
C=2A_{4}
,
to
S_{3}\times Z_{(2,p)}
for
C=E_{7}(a_{2})+A_{1}
, and to
S_{3}
for

C=A_{8},D_{8}(a_{3})
(the case
p\neq 3
),
A_{5}+A_{2},D_{4}(a_{1})+A_{1},D_{4}(a_{1})
.

A_{G}(u)
is abelian for all other cases.
The set
\mathscr{M}_{G}
is given, except for cuspidal ones, by

\displaystyle\{(T,\{1\},1),(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0}),(L^{\prime},C_{0}^{\prime},\mathscr{E}_{0}^{\prime})\}\quad\text{ for $L=D_{4},L^{\prime}=E_{7}$ \text{ with } $p=2$},

\displaystyle\{(T,\{1\},1),(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\}\quad\text{ for $L=E_{6}$ with $p=3$},

\displaystyle\{(T,\{1\},1)\}\quad\text{ with $p\neq 2,3$},

where in the first case, there exist two
\mathscr{E}_{0}^{\prime}
for a given class
C_{0}^{\prime}
of

L^{\prime}=E_{7}
, and in the second case, there exist two
\mathscr{E}_{0}
for a given class

C_{0}
of
L=E_{6}
.

In the case where
p
is good, since
|\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)|=1
for any class
C
, the results
of Beynon-Spaltenstein can be applied, and the existence of split elements
is shown by (4.7.2).

Consider the class
C
in the list

(6.1.1)

\displaystyle E_{8},\quad E_{8}(a_{1}),\quad E_{8}(a_{2}),\quad E_{7}+A_{1}\ (p=2),\quad E_{7}(a_{1})+A_{1}\ (p=2),

\displaystyle D_{8}(a_{1})\ (p=2),\quad E_{7}(a_{2})+A_{1},\quad A_{8},\quad D_{8}(a_{3})\ (p\neq 3),\quad 2A_{4},

\displaystyle A_{5}+A_{2},\quad D_{4}(a_{1})+A_{1},\quad D_{4}(a_{1}).

In those cases, Lemma 4.13 cannot be applied; if

C=E_{8},E_{8}(a_{1}),E_{8}(a_{2}),E_{7}+A_{1},E_{7}(a_{1})+A_{1}
,
there does not exist a proper Levi subgroup
M
such that
M\cap C\neq\emptyset
,
and
A_{G}(u)
is not abelian for the latter
C
.

6.2.

Let
C
be the class which is not in the list (6.1.1).
First assume that
p=2
. In this case
L
is of type
D_{4}
and
L^{\prime}
is of type
E_{7}
.
If
|\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)|=2
, then

C
is in the list

(6.2.1)

\displaystyle E_{7}(a_{1}),\quad D_{7},\quad E_{7}(a_{2}),\quad E_{6}+A_{1},\quad(D_{7}(a_{1}))_{2},

\displaystyle E_{6},\quad D_{6},\quad(D_{5}+A_{2})_{2},\quad D_{6}(a_{1}),\quad D_{5}+A_{1},

\displaystyle D_{5},\quad(D_{4}+A_{2})_{2},\quad D_{4}+A_{1},\quad D_{4}.

In those cases, one can find a proper subgroup

M\supset L
such that
M\cap C\neq\emptyset
, hence
Lemma 4.13 can be applied. If
|\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)|=1
, Lemma 4.13 can be applied since
there is no restriction on
M
.
If
|\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)|\geq 3
, then
|\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)|=4
for
C=E_{7}
with
L=D_{4}
and
L^{\prime}=E_{7}
.
But in this case (an extension of) Lemma 4.13 can be applied for
M=E_{7}

since
T\subset L\subset L^{\prime}
(see Remark 4.14).

Next assume that
p=3
.
If
|\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)|=3
, then
C=E_{7},E_{6}+A_{1}
or
E_{6}
with
L=E_{6}
,
and
\mathscr{W}_{L}=W(G_{2})
. In those cases, Lemma 4.13 can be applied for

M=E_{7}\supset L=E_{6}
.
For other
C
, Lemma 4.13 can be applied since
|\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)|=1
.

Finally assume that
p\neq 2,3
. In this case we have
|\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)|=1
for any class
C
,
and Lemma 4.13 can be applied without referring
L
.

6.3.

We consider
C
in the list in (6.1.1).
If
C
is one of
A_{8},D_{8}(a_{3})\ (p\neq 3),2A_{4},A_{5}+A_{2},D_{4}(a_{1})+A_{1},D_{4}(a_{1})
,
then
A_{G}(u)\simeq S_{3}
or
S_{5}
, and,
|\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)|=1
.
In those cases, (4.7.2) can be applied,
and there exist split elements for
C
.

We consider the case where
C=E_{8},E_{8}(a_{1}),E_{8}(a_{2}),E_{7}+A_{1}
, or
E_{7}(a_{1})+A_{1}
.
Since we may assume that
|\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)|\neq 1
, we have only to consider
the case where
C=E_{8}\ (p=2,3),E_{8}(a_{1})\ (p=2,3)
,
E_{8}(a_{2})\ (p=2)

or
E_{7}+A_{1}\ (p=2,3)
,
E_{7}(a_{1})+A_{1}\ (p=2)
.

(1) First assume that
C=E_{8}
. Then
C
is a regular unipotent class in
G
,
and
A_{G}(u)\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{(60,p^{2})}
for
u\in C
.
Here
C=\operatorname{Ind}_{T}^{G}1
. Let
M
be a Levi subgroup
of type
E_{7}
, and put
C^{\prime}=\operatorname{Ind}_{T}^{M}1
. Then
C^{\prime}
is a regular unipotent class in
M
.
A similar discussion as in the case of 5.3 (1), by using Lemma 4.9, shows that
splits elements exist in
C^{F}
.

(2) Assume that
C=E_{8}(a_{1})
with
p=2
. Then
A_{G}(u)\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{4}
, and
the characters of order 4 correspond to cuspidal pairs. Hence we need to consider

-1\in A_{G}(u)^{\wedge}
, where
(u,-1)
belongs to the series
(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})

with
L
of type
D_{4}
. Let
M
be of type
E_{7}
, and
C^{\prime}=E_{7}(a_{1})
.
Then for
u^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}
, we have
A_{M}(u^{\prime})\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{2}
, and
\dim Z_{G}(u)=\dim Z_{M}(u^{\prime})=10
.
We have

(6.3.1)

\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}=(1\otimes 1)\oplus(-1\otimes-1).

By Lemma 4.16, there exists
u\in C^{F}
such that

\gamma(1,1,u,1)=1,\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u,-1)=1
.
Thus
u
is a split element in
C^{F}
.

(3) Assume that
C=E_{8}(a_{1})
with
p=3
.
We have
A_{G}(u)\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{3}
,
and for a non-trivial character
\zeta
of
\mathbf{Z}_{3}
,
(u,\zeta)
belongs to the
series
(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})
with
L
of type
E_{6}
.
In this case, one cannot find
C^{\prime}\subset M
such that
Lemma 4.16 can be applied. However, by modifying the discussion in the proof of
Lemma 4.16, we can
argue as follows. Let
M
be of type
E_{7}
, and
C^{\prime}=E_{7}
.
Then
A_{M}(u^{\prime})\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{3}
for
u^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}
.
\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}
can be decomposed as

(6.3.2)

\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}=(1\otimes 1)\oplus(\zeta\otimes\zeta^{*})\oplus(\zeta^{2}\otimes\zeta^{2*}).

But since

\dim Z_{G}(u)=10>\dim Z_{M}(u^{\prime})=8
,
Lemma 4.16 cannot be applied.

Now
F
acts on the set
X_{u,u^{\prime}}
as a permutation.
By (6.3.2),
A_{G}(u)\times A_{M}(u^{\prime})
acts transitively on
X_{u,u^{\prime}}
.
If
X_{u,u^{\prime}}^{F}\neq\emptyset
, since
F
acts trivially on
A_{G}(u)
and
A_{M}(u^{\prime})
,
all the elements in
X_{u,u^{\prime}}
are
F
-stable, and
F
acts trivially on
\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}
.
Then the conclusion of Lemma 4.16 holds, and
u\in C^{F}
is a split element.
Thus we assume that
X_{u,u^{\prime}}^{F}=\emptyset
.
By (6.3.2),
X_{u,u^{\prime}}
is a three point set, and
F
acts on
X_{u,u^{\prime}}=\{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\}

as a permutation
F:x_{1}\mapsto x_{2}\mapsto x_{3}\mapsto x_{1}
.
On the other hand, since
\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}
is a regular
A_{G}(u)
-module by (6.3.2),
there exists a generator
a
of
A_{G}(u)\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{3}
such that the action of
a
on
X_{u,u^{\prime}}

coincides with that of
F
on it.
Let
\omega
be a cubic root of unity in
\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{l}^{*}
such that
\zeta(a)=\omega
.
Then
\zeta\otimes\zeta^{*}
and
\zeta^{2}\otimes\zeta^{2*}
are both
F
-stable, and
the action of
F
on
\zeta\otimes\zeta^{*}
(resp. on
\zeta^{2}\otimes\zeta^{2*}
) is given by a scalar
multiplication by
\omega
(resp.
\omega^{2}
). It follows, by a similar discussion as in the proof of
Lemma 4.16, that

\gamma(1,1,u,1)=1,\quad\gamma(u_{0},\rho_{0},u,\zeta)=\omega,\quad\gamma(u_{0},\rho_{0},u,\zeta^{2})=\omega^{2}.

We consider
u_{z}\in C^{F}
obtained from
u
by twisting with
z\in A_{G}(u)
.
Here we choose
z
so that
\zeta(z)=\omega^{-1}
. Then

\displaystyle\gamma(u_{0},\rho_{0},u_{z},\zeta)

\displaystyle=\zeta(z)\gamma(u_{0},\rho_{0},u,\zeta)=\zeta(z)\omega=1,

\displaystyle\gamma(u_{0},\rho_{0},u_{z},\zeta^{2})

\displaystyle=\zeta^{2}(z)\gamma(u_{0},\rho_{0},u,\zeta^{2})=\zeta^{2}(z)\omega^{2}=1.

Since
\gamma(1,1,u_{z},1)=\gamma(1,1,u,1)=1
,
u_{z}
is a split element in
C^{F}
.

(4) Assume that
C=E_{8}(a_{2})
with
p=2
. Then
A_{G}(u)\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{2}
,
and for
-1\in A_{G}(u)^{\wedge}
,
(u,-1)
belongs to the series
(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})

with
L
of type
D_{4}
. .
We consider
M
of type
E_{7}
, and let
C^{\prime}=E_{7}(a_{2})
. For
u^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}
,
we have
A_{M}(u^{\prime})\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{2}
, and
\dim Z_{G}(u)=\dim Z_{M}(u^{\prime})=12
.

\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}
is decomposed in a similar way as in (6.3.1).
Hence Lemma 4.16 can be applied to show the existence of
split elements in
C^{F}
.

(5) Assume that
C=E_{7}+A_{1}
. First assume that
p=2
.
Then
A_{G}(u)\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{2}\times\mathbf{Z}_{2}
.
L
is of type
D_{4}
,
and
\mathscr{W}_{L}=W(F_{4})
. We have
V_{(u,\varepsilon^{\prime})}=\chi_{8,1}
and

V_{(u,\varepsilon^{\prime\prime})}=\chi_{1,2}
. Let
M
be a Levi subgroup of type
D_{7}
.
Then
\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\prime}=W(C_{3})
. Note that these two characters
\chi_{8,1}
and
\chi_{1,2}

in
W(F_{4})
are completely the same as those appeared in the case (4) in 5.3
for
G=E_{7}
.
Let
u^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}\subset M
of type
((1148),(11))
.
We have
\dim Z_{G}(u)=\dim Z_{M}(u^{\prime})=14
, and

A_{M}(u^{\prime})\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{2}\times\mathbf{Z}_{2}
.
Then
\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}
is decomposed as

(6.3.3)

\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}=(1\otimes 1)\oplus(\varepsilon\otimes\varepsilon)\oplus(\varepsilon^{\prime}\otimes\varepsilon^{\prime})\oplus(\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}\otimes\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}).

Hence a split element exists by Lemma 4.16.

Next assume that
p=3
. Then
A_{G}(u)\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{2}\times\mathbf{Z}_{3}
.

L
is of type
E_{6}
, and
\mathscr{W}_{L}=W(G_{2})
. We have
V_{(u,\zeta)}=\theta^{\prime}
,
where
\zeta
is an irreducible character of
\mathbf{Z}_{3}
of order 3.
Note that
(u,-\zeta),(u,-\zeta^{2})
are cuspidal pairs in
G
.
We consider a Levi subgroup
M
of type
E_{7}
, then
\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\prime}=W(A_{1})
.
Take
u^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}\subset M

such that
C^{\prime}=E_{6}
. Then
A_{M}(u^{\prime})\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{3}
, and we have

\dim Z_{G}(u)=\dim Z_{M}(u^{\prime})=14
.
\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}
is decomposed as

(6.3.4)

\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}=(1\otimes 1)\oplus(\zeta\otimes\zeta^{*})\oplus(\zeta^{2}\otimes\zeta^{*2}).

It follows, by Lemma 4.16, that there exists
u\in C^{F}
such that

\gamma(1,1,u,1)=1,\quad\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u,\zeta)=1,\quad\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u,\zeta^{2})=1.

Now assume that
\gamma(1,1,u,-1)=\nu
for a root of unity
\nu
. It follows from the
computation of Green functions by
[
Lü
]
, there exists
u_{1}\in C^{F}
such that

\gamma(1,1,u_{1},1)=1
and that
\gamma(1,1,u_{1},-1)=1
.
u_{1}
is written
as
u_{1}=u_{z}
for some
z\in A_{G}(u)
(a twisted element by
z
).
Since
\gamma(1,1,u_{1},-1)=-1(z)\gamma(1,1,u,-1)
,
we have
\nu=\pm 1
. If
\nu=1
, put
v=u
, then
v\in C^{F}
is a split
element. If
\nu=-1
, put
v=u_{z^{\prime}}
for
z^{\prime}=(-1,1)\in\mathbf{Z}_{2}\times\mathbf{Z}_{2}
.
We have
\gamma(1,1,u_{z^{\prime}},-1)=-1(z^{\prime})\gamma(1,1,u,-1)=1
, and since

\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u_{z^{\prime}},\rho)=\rho(z^{\prime})\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u,\rho)
for
\rho=\zeta
or
\zeta^{2}
,
we have
\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u_{z^{\prime}},\rho)=1
. Thus
v=u_{z^{\prime}}
is a split element.
In any case, a split element
v\in C^{F}
exists.

(6) Assume that
C=E_{7}(a_{1})+A_{1}
with
p=2
.
We have
A_{G}(u)\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{2}
.
L
is of type
D_{4}
, and
\mathscr{W}_{L}=W(F_{4})
.
In this case, there does not exist a class
C^{\prime}\subset M

such that Lemma 4.16 can be applied. However, we can argue as in the case (3).
We have
V_{(u,-1)}=\chi_{4,2}
.
Let
M
be a Levi subgroup of type
E_{7}
.
Then
\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\prime}=W(B_{3})
.
We have
\chi_{4,2}|_{W(B_{3})}=(1;2)+(-;3)
. In the Springer correspondence,

(1;2)
corresponds to the class
C^{\prime}=E_{6}
in
M
. Take
u^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}
.
Then
A_{M}(u^{\prime})\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{2}
.
We have
V_{(u^{\prime},-1)}=(1;2)
.
The
A_{G}^{\prime}u)\times A_{M}(u^{\prime})
-module
\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}
is decomposed as

(6.3.5)

\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}=(1\otimes 1)\oplus(-1\otimes-1).

In this case,
\dim Z_{M}(u^{\prime})=14<\dim Z_{G}(u)=18
, and
Lemma 4.16 can not be applied.
But
X_{u,u^{\prime}}
is a two point set, and
F
acts on it as a permutation.
If
F
acts trivially on
X_{u,u^{\prime}}
, then
u\in C^{F}
is a split element.
If
X_{u,u^{\prime}}^{F}=\emptyset
, then the action of
F
on
X_{u,u^{\prime}}
coincides
with the action of
A_{G}(u)\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{2}
on
X_{u,u^{\prime}}
. Thus by a similar
discussion as in (3), a twisted element
u_{z}\in C^{F}
(
z
is a generator of
A_{G}(u)
)
gives a split element in
C^{F}
.

6.4.

The remaining cases are
C=D_{8}(a_{1})
and
C=E_{7}(a_{2})+A_{1}
.
Since we may assume that
|\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)|\neq 1
, it is enough to consider
the case where
p=2
.
For these cases, we consider
Q=MU_{Q}
, where
M
is a Levi subgroup of type
E_{7}
.
In the discussion below, we use a notation of root systems as given in 4.2.
In particular, by comparing
\varDelta^{+}
and
\varDelta^{+}_{7}
, we see that
U_{Q}
is generated
by
x_{\alpha}(t)
where
\alpha
is contained in

(6.4.1)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}7\pm 1,\ 7\pm 2,\ 7\pm 3,\ 7\pm 4,\ 7\pm 5,\ 7\pm 6,\\
8\pm 1,\ 8\pm 2,\ 8\pm 3,\ 8\pm 4,\ 8\pm 5,\ 8\pm 6,\ 8+7,\\
\pm 1\pm 2\pm 3\pm 4\pm 5\pm 6+7+8,\quad\text{ with $-$ : even. }\end{cases}

First assume that
C=D_{8}(a_{1})
with
p=2
. Following Mizuno’s table
[
M2
]
,
we take
u=z_{44}\in C
as

(6.4.2)

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=x_{8}(1)x_{15}(1)x_{16}(1)x_{17}(1)x_{18}(1)x_{24}(1)x_{13}(1)x_{102}(1)

\displaystyle=x_{\alpha}(1)x_{3+2}(1)x_{4-1}(1)x_{4+1}(1)x_{5-2}(1)x_{5+1}(1)x_{6-4}(1)x_{7-5}(1),

where
\alpha=-1+2-3-4-5-6-7+8
.
Here
A_{G}(u)\simeq D_{8}
, and we have
A_{G}(u)^{\wedge}=\{1,\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime\prime},\theta\}
(see 4.4).
Note that
(u,\varepsilon)
is a cuspidal pair for
G
.
Let
C^{\prime}
be the class of
M
such that

C^{\prime}=D_{5}+A_{1}
, and take
u^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}
. Then we have
A_{M}(u^{\prime})\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{2}
,
and
\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}
is decomposed as

(6.4.3)

\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}=(1\otimes 1)\oplus(\varepsilon^{\prime}\otimes 1)\oplus(\theta\otimes-1).

In particular,
\theta\in A_{G}(u)^{\wedge}
appears in (6.5.3).
We have
\dim Z_{G}(u)=\dim Z_{M}(u^{\prime})=20
.
By the restriction formula, (6.5.3) implies that there exists
g\in G

such that
v=g^{-1}ug\in Q
and that the projection of
v
on
M
coincides with
u^{\prime}
.

For a given
u\in C^{F}
as in (6.5.2), we determine
g\in G^{F}
and
u^{\prime}\in C^{\prime F}
explicitly.
Let
v
be the conjugate of
u
by
g=x_{2+1}(1)x_{4-3}(1)\omega_{7-6}\in G^{F}
.
Then we have

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=gug^{-1}=x_{\alpha}(1)x_{3+2}(1)x_{4-1}(1)x_{4+1}(1)x_{5-2}(1)x_{6-5}(1)x_{7-4}(1)x_{7-3}(1).

Thus
v\in Q
, and let
u^{\prime}
be the projection of
v
to
M
. We have

(6.4.4)

\displaystyle u^{\prime}

\displaystyle=x_{\alpha}(1)x_{3+2}(1)x_{4-1}(1)x_{4+1}(1)x_{5-2}(1)x_{6-5}(1)

\displaystyle=x_{\alpha}(1)x_{3+2}(1)x_{4+1}(1)x_{5-2}(1)x_{6-5}(1)\times x_{4-1}(1).

Since
(\alpha,5-2)=-1,(\alpha,4+1)=-1,(\alpha,3+2)=0,(\alpha,6-5)=0
,
we see that
\{3+2,6-5,5-2,\alpha,4+1\}
gives a subsystem of

\varDelta(E_{7})
of type
D_{5}
,

\textstyle{(3+2)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(6-5)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(5-2)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\alpha\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(4+1)}

and
4-1
is orthogonal to all the above roots.
Thus
u^{\prime}
has type
D_{5}+A_{1}
, and
u^{\prime}\in C^{\prime F}
.
It is verified that
u^{\prime}
is a split element in
C^{\prime F}
. Since

F
acts trivially on
A_{G}(u)
and on
A_{M}(u^{\prime})
,
Lemma 4.16 can be applied, and by (6.4.3), we have

(6.4.5)

\gamma(1,1,u,1)=\gamma(1,1,u,\varepsilon^{\prime})=1,\quad\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u,\theta)=1.

On the other hand, by the computation of Green functions by
[
Lü
]
,
there exists
u_{1}\in C^{F}
such that
F
acts trivially on
A_{G}(u_{1})
, and that

\gamma(1,1,u_{1},\rho)=1
for any
\rho\in A_{G}(u_{1})^{\wedge}
such that
(u,\rho)
belongs to

(T,\{1\},1)
.
Since
A_{G}(u)\simeq D_{8}
, there exists exactly two
G^{F}
-classes in
C^{F}

such that
F
acts trivially on
A_{G}(v)
for
v
in such a class.
Thus
u_{1}
is obtained from
u

by twisting with

as
u_{1}=u_{z}
, where
z
is a central element. Then we have

\gamma(1,1,u,\varepsilon)=\varepsilon(z)
and
\gamma(1,1,u,\varepsilon^{\prime\prime})=\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}(z)
.
We have
Z(D_{8})=\{\pm 1\}
, and
\varepsilon(z)=\varepsilon(z^{\prime\prime})=1
(
-1
is a product of
two reflections corresponding to two short roots or two long roots). Hence

(6.4.6)

\gamma(1,1,u,\varepsilon)=1,\quad\gamma(1,1,u,\varepsilon^{\prime\prime})=1.

By (6.4.5) and (6.4.6),
u\in C^{F}
is a split element.

6.5.

Next consider the case where

.
Then

(u,-\varepsilon)

.
Let

[
M2
]
.
We have

(6.5.1)

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=x_{14}(1)x_{15}(1)x_{21}(1)x_{17}(1)x_{18}(1)x_{29}(1)x_{19}(1)x_{101}(1)

\displaystyle=x_{\alpha}(1)x_{3+2}(1)x_{\beta}(1)x_{4+1}(1)x_{5-2}(1)x_{5+2}(1)x_{6-3}(1)x_{7-2}(1),

Let

of

to

, explicitly.

Let

\displaystyle g_{1}ug_{1}^{-1}

\displaystyle=x_{\alpha}(1)x_{3+2}(1)x_{4+2}(1)x_{4+1}(1)x_{5+2}(1)x_{6-3}(1)x_{7-2}(1)

Now put
\gamma=1-2-3-4+5-6+7+8
. Then

\displaystyle w_{\gamma}(3+2)

\displaystyle=1+2+3-4+5-6+7+8,\quad w_{\gamma}(4+2)=1+2-3+4+5-6+7+8,

\displaystyle w_{\gamma}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\alpha,\quad w_{\gamma}(4+1)=4+1,\quad w_{\gamma}(5+2)=5+2,\quad w_{\gamma}(6-3)=6-3,

\displaystyle w_{\gamma}(7-2)

\displaystyle=-1-2+3+4-5+6+7-8=-(\alpha_{1}+(5+2)).

Note that
{}^{\omega_{\gamma}}x_{3+2}(1),{}^{\omega_{\gamma}}x_{4+2}(1)\in U_{Q}
,

.

, and let

Then we have

(6.5.3)

\displaystyle u^{\prime}

\displaystyle=x_{\alpha}(1)x_{4+1}(1)x_{5+2}(1)x_{6-3}(1)x_{\delta}(1),

If we put

;

\textstyle{(4+1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\xi\phantom{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(6-3)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\phantom{**}\alpha\phantom{**}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(5+2)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\phantom{*}\delta\phantom{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(-6-3)}

Since this is a simple system of type

, there exists

such that

In particular

.
Hence we have

\omega u^{\prime}\omega^{-1}=x_{2+1}(1)x_{2-1}(1)x_{3-2}(1)x_{4-3}(1)x_{5-4}(1),

where

is an element in

,
which is a product of elements

,
hence

. Note that since
