, we can ignore the signs in the computation.

Now it is verified that
\omega u^{\prime}\omega^{-1}
is a split element in
C^{\prime F}
.
Since
F
acts trivially on
A_{G}(u)
, and on
A_{M}(u^{\prime})
, by applying Lemma 4.16,
(6.5.2) implies that

\gamma(1,1,u,1)=\gamma(1,1,u,\theta)=1,\quad\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u,-1)=\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u,-\theta)=1.

On the other hand, by the computation of Green functions due to
[
Lü
]
,
there exists
u_{1}\in C^{F}
such that
F
acts trivially on
A_{G}(u_{1})
and
that
\gamma(1,1,u_{1},\rho)=1
for
\rho=1,\varepsilon,\theta
. As in the discussion
in 6.5,
u_{1}
is obtained from
u
as
u_{1}=u_{z}
,
by twisting by a central element
z\in A_{G}(u)
. Then we have

\gamma(1,1,u,\varepsilon)=\varepsilon(z)\gamma(1,1,u_{1},\varepsilon)=1
. Hence
u\in C^{F}
is a split element.

Summing up the above discussion, we have proved that Theorem 4.6 holds
for
G=E_{8}
.

7.
The case
F_{4}

7.1.

In this section, we assume that
G
is of type
F_{4}
.
Then
A_{G}(u)
is isomorphic to
D_{8}
(
p=2
) if
C=F_{4}(a_{2})
,
to
S_{4}
(
p\neq 2
),
S_{3}
(
p=2
) if
C=F_{4}(a_{3})
. In all other cases,

A_{G}(u)
is abelian. The set
\mathscr{M}_{G}
is given, except for cuspidal ones, by

\{(T,\{1\},1),(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\}
with
L
of type
B_{2}
in the case
p=2
,
and
\{(T,\{1\},1)\}
in the case
p\neq 2
.

Consider a list of unipotent classes
C
,

(7.1.1)

F_{4},\quad F_{4}(a_{1}),\quad F_{4}(a_{2}),\quad F_{4}(a_{3}).

For those
C
in the list, Lemma 4.13 cannot be applied since
there does not exist a proper Levi subgroup
M
such that
M\cap C\neq\emptyset
.

7.2.

If
p\neq 2
, since
|\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)|=1
for any class
C
, (4.7.2) implies
the existence of split elements. Thus in the discussion below, we assume that
p=2
.
In this case,
L
is of type
B_{2}
.
Let
C
be the class which is not in the list (7.1.1).
If
|\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)|=2
,
then
C
is in the list

(7.1.2)

\displaystyle B_{3},\quad C_{3},\quad(B_{2})_{2}.

In those cases, Lemma 4.13 can be applied with
M\supset L=B_{2}
.
For other
C
, Lemma 4.13 can be applied since
|\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)|=1
, and
A_{G}(u)
is
abelian.

7.3.

Assume that
C
is in the list (7.1.1).
For
C=F_{4}
,
since
C
is regular unipotent, a similar argument as in the case of
E_{7}

(see 5.3 (1)) can be applied. For
C=F_{4}(a_{1}),F_{4}(a_{3})
, since
|\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)|=1
,
again (4.7.2) can be applied.

Now assume that
C=F_{4}(a_{2})
. In this case,
A_{G}(u)\simeq D_{8}
and

|\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)|=2
, where
L=B_{2}
and
\mathscr{W}_{L}=W(B_{2})
.

(u,\varepsilon)
is a cuspidal pair for
G
.
In the discussion below, we follow the notation in 4.2. In particular,
we use a convention similar to 5.5, for example,

\frac{1}{2}(\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2}-\varepsilon_{3}-\varepsilon_{4})
is written as
1-2-3-4
.
We choose
u=x_{24}\in C^{F}
from Shinoda
[
Shi
]
, where

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=x_{2-4}(1)x_{3+4}(1)x_{1-2-3-4}(1)x_{1-2-3+4}(1).

Then
F
acts trivially on
A_{G}(u)
.
Let
Q=MU_{Q}
be a parabolic subgroup of
G
such that
M
is a Levi subgroup
of type
C_{3}
. For
g=x_{4}(1)\in G^{F}
, we have

gug^{-1}=x_{2-4}(1)x_{2}(1)x_{3+4}(1)x_{1-2-3-4}(1)\in MU_{Q}.

Let
u^{\prime}
be the projection of
gug^{-1}
on
M
.
Then we have

u^{\prime}=x_{3+4}(1)x_{1-2-3-4}(1)\in M^{F}.

Let
C^{\prime}
be the class in
M
containing
u^{\prime}
. Then
u^{\prime}
is
M^{F}
-conjugate
to
x_{3-4}(1)x_{4}(1)
, which is a regular element of the subgroup of
M
of
type
C_{2}
. Hence
u^{\prime}
is of type
((4),(1))
.

A_{M}(u^{\prime})\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{2}
, and one can check that
u^{\prime}
is a split element in
C^{\prime F}
.
Furthermore we have
\dim Z_{G}(u)=\dim Z_{M}(u^{\prime})=8
.

\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}
is decomposed as

(7.3.1)

\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}\simeq(1\otimes 1)\oplus(\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}\otimes 1)\oplus(\theta\otimes 1).

Now Lemma 4.16 can be applied, and by (7.3.1) we have

\gamma(1,1,u,1)=\gamma(1,1,u,\varepsilon^{\prime\prime})=1,\quad\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u,\theta)=1.

On the other hand, from the computation of Green functions due to Geck
[
G2
]
,
there exists
u_{1}\in C^{F}
such that
\gamma(1,1,u_{1},\rho)=1
for

\rho=1,\varepsilon^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}
.
By a similar discussion as in the case
G=E_{8}
with
C=D_{8}(a_{1})
(see 6.5),
we have
\gamma(1,1,u,\varepsilon^{\prime})=\gamma(1,1,u_{1},\varepsilon^{\prime})=1
. Hence
u
is a split element.

8.
The case
G_{2}

8.1.

Assume that
G=G_{2}
.
In this case,
|\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)|=1
for any class
C
, which works for any
p
.
Thus (4.7.2) is applied to show the existence of split elements.

9.
The case
E_{6}

9.1.

In this section, we assume that
G=E_{6}
, where
F
is split or non-split.

Note that any unipotent class
C
is
F
-stable (see (4.1.2)).

For
u\in C
,
A_{G}(u)
is abelian unless
C=D_{4}(a_{1})
, in which
case,
A_{G}(u)\simeq S_{3}
.

The set
\mathscr{M}_{G}
is given, except for cuspidal ones, by

\displaystyle\{(T,\{1\},1),(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0}),(L^{\prime},C_{0}^{\prime},\mathscr{E}_{0}^{\prime})\}\quad\text{ for $L=2A_{2},L^{\prime}=D_{4}$ with $p=2$,}

\displaystyle\{(T,\{1\},1),(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\}\quad\text{ for $L=2A_{2}$ with $p\neq 2,3$,}

\displaystyle\{(T,\{1\},1)\}\quad\text{ with }p=3,

where in the first and second case, there exist two
\mathscr{E}_{0}
for the class
C_{0}
of
L
.

Note that if three types of Levi subgroups
T,L
and
L^{\prime}
are involved in
\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)
,
then it is only the case where
C=E_{6}
.

9.2.

Take
u\in C^{F}
. The action of
F
on
A_{G}(u)
was explained
in (4.1.4). More precisely, we have the following.

Z_{G}\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{3}
(resp.
Z_{G}=1
) if
p\neq 3
(resp.
p=3
).
In the case where
Z_{G}=1
, for any unipotent class
C
, there exists
a representative
u\in C^{F}
such that
F
acts trivially on
A_{G}(u)
. So
the situation is similar to other types. Now assume that
p\neq 3
, and

Z_{G}\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{3}
. We have a natural map
\varphi:Z_{G}\to A_{G}(u)
. If
\varphi

is non-trivial, then
\varphi
gives an isomorphism
Z_{G}\simeq A_{G}(u)

except when

C=A_{5}+A_{1}
, and
A_{G}(u)\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{3}\times\mathbf{Z}_{2}
, in which case

\varphi
gives an isomorphism from
\mathbf{Z}_{3}
to the direct factor
\mathbf{Z}_{3}
in

A_{G}(u)
. (Note that in the case where
C=D_{4}(a_{1})
,
\varphi:Z_{G}\to S_{3}

is the trivial map.)
Now the action of
F
on
A_{G}(u)
is given by the action of
F
on
the image of
Z_{G}
in
A_{G}(u)
.

In the case where
p\neq 3
,
Z_{G}
is identified
with the subgroup
\{z\in\mathbf{k}^{*}\mid z^{3}=1\}
of
\mathbf{k}^{*}
,
and the action of
F
on
Z_{G}
is given by
z\mapsto z^{q}

(resp.
z\mapsto z^{-q}
) if
F
is split (resp. non-split).
Hence in the split case

F
acts trivially on
\mathbf{Z}_{3}
if and only if

q\equiv 1\pmod{3}
, and in the non-split case

F
acts trivially on
\mathbf{Z}_{3}
if and only if
q\equiv-1\pmod{3}
.

9.3.

Assume that
p\neq 3
, then
Z_{G}\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{3}
.
For
\chi\in Z_{G}^{\wedge}
, we consider the generalized Springer correspondence
(1.2.1) pertaining to
\chi
. The generalized Springer correspondence (1.1.4)
and its partition to
\chi
parts in (1.2.1) are compatible with the actions of
F
.
Since
F
permutes
Z_{G}^{\wedge}
,
F
permutes the sets

\mathscr{N}_{\chi}
. In particular, in considering
\mathscr{N}_{G}^{F}
and
\mathscr{M}_{G}^{F}
,
it is enough to focus on
\mathscr{N}_{\chi}
and
\mathscr{M}_{\chi}
for
F
-stable
\chi
.
In our case,
Z_{G}\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{3}
. If
F
acts non-trivially on
Z_{G}
, then

F
permutes two non-trivial characters
\chi,\chi^{\prime}
of
Z_{G}
, hence
permutes
\mathscr{N}_{\chi}
and
\mathscr{N}_{\chi^{\prime}}
. Thus as for
\mathscr{N}_{G}^{F}
,
it is enough to consider the correspondence in (1.2.1) for
\chi=1
.
Note that this is essentially the same as the generalized
Springer correspondence for the adjoint group of
G
.

9.4.

We shall prove Theorem 4.6 for
G
in a similar
way as in the previous sections. But
in the case where
F
is non-split, we need to use
Lemma 1.15 rather than lemmas in Section 4. Also we need a special care
if there does not exist
u\in C^{F}

such that
F
acts trivially on
A_{G}(u)
.

9.5.

First assume that
F
is split, and
q\equiv 1\pmod{3}
if
p\neq 3
.
Hence by 9.2, for each class
C
, there exists a representative

u\in C^{F}
such that
F
acts trivially on
A_{G}(u)
.

Consider the class
C
in the list

(9.5.1)

\displaystyle E_{6},\quad E_{6}(a_{1}),\quad A_{5}+A_{1},\quad D_{4}(a_{1}).

In those cases, Lemma 4.13 cannot be applied; if
C=E_{6},E_{6}(a_{1}),A_{5}+A_{1}
, there
does not exist a proper Levi subgroup
M
such that
C\cap M\neq\emptyset
,
and if
C=D_{4}(a_{1})
, then
A_{G}(u)
is not abelian.

Assume that
C
is in the remaining cases. First assume that
p\neq 3
.
In this case,
L
is of type
2A_{2}
or
D_{4}
. If
|\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)|\geq 2
, then
C
is in the list

(9.5.2)

\displaystyle A_{5},\quad 2A_{2}+A_{1},\quad 2A_{2},\quad D_{5},\quad D_{4},

where the former three cases are with respect to
L=2A_{2}
, and the latter two cases
are with respect to
L=D_{4}
.
In those cases, one can find
M\supset L
such that
M\cap C\neq\emptyset
,
hence Lemma 4.13 can be applied. If
|\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)|=1
, (4.7.2) is applied.

In the case where
p=3
, we have
|\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)|=1
for any
C
,
and (4.7.2) can be applied.

Thus unless
C
is in the list (9.5.1), split elements

u\in C^{F}
exist.

9.6.

Under the assumption in 9.5, we assume that
C
is in the list (9.5.1).

(1) First assume that
C=E_{6}(a_{1})
. In this case, for
u\in C^{F}
,

A_{G}(u)=1
if
p=3
, and
A_{G}(u)\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{3}
if
p\neq 3
. So we assume that

p\neq 3
. Then
L
is of type
2A_{2}
, and
\mathscr{W}_{L}
is of type
G_{2}
.
Let
\zeta=\chi\in\mathbf{Z}_{3}^{\wedge}
be a non-trivial character. Then
V_{(u,\zeta)}\in\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\wedge}

coincides with the short sign character
\varepsilon_{c}\in W(G_{2})^{\wedge}
.
Let
M
be a Levi subgroup of type
2A_{2}+A_{1}
, and
C^{\prime}
the class
of type
2A_{2}
. In this case
A_{M}(u^{\prime})\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{3}
for
u^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}
,
and

\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}
is decomposed as

(9.6.1)

\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}=(1\otimes 1)\oplus(\zeta\otimes\zeta^{*})\oplus(\zeta^{2}\otimes{\zeta^{2*}}).

Here
\dim Z_{G}(u)=\dim Z_{M}(u^{\prime})=8
.
Thus by Lemma 4.16, there exists a split element
u\in C^{F}
.

(2) Next assume that
C=A_{5}+A_{1}
. In this case,
|\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)|=1
if

p=3
. Hence we assume that
p\neq 3
. Then
A_{G}(u)\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{2}\times\mathbf{Z}_{3}
,
and
\dim Z_{G}(u)=12
.
Let
\zeta
be an irreducible character of
A_{G}(u)
of order 3.
Note that
(u,-\zeta),(u,-\zeta^{2})
are cuspidal pairs for
G
. Let
M
be a Levi subgroup
of type
A_{5}
, and choose
C^{\prime}\subset M
such that the Jordan type of

C^{\prime}
is
(33)
. Then for
u^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}
, we have
A_{M}(u^{\prime})\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{3}
, and

\dim Z_{M}(u^{\prime})=12
.
\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}
is decomposed as

(9.6.2)

\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}=(1\otimes 1)\oplus(\zeta\otimes\zeta^{*})\oplus(\zeta^{2}\otimes\zeta^{2*}).

From the computation of Green functions, there exists a split element
\overline{u}
in
the image of
C
for the adjoint group
\overline{G}
of
G
. We choose
u\in C^{F}

so that the image of
u
coincides with
\overline{u}
, and that
u\in u^{\prime}U_{Q}
, where

u^{\prime}\in C^{\prime F}
is a split element.
Then by Lemma 4.16, we have

\gamma(1,1,u,1)=1,\quad\gamma(u_{0},\rho_{0},u,\zeta)=1,\quad\gamma(u_{0},\rho_{0},u,\zeta^{2})=1.

Moreover, since
\overline{u}
is split, we have

\gamma(1,1,u,-1)=1.

Hence
u\in C^{F}
is a split element.

(3) Next assume that
C=D_{4}(a_{1})
. Since
|\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)|=1
,
a split element
u\in C^{F}
exists by (4.7.2).

(4) Finally assume that
C=E_{6}
.
If
|\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)|=1
, (4.7.2) can be applied. If only
L,T
or
L^{\prime},T
is involved in
the set
\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)
,
a similar discussion as in 5.3 (1) (the case
G=E_{7},C=E_{7}
) can be applied.
Hence we may assume that the three types
T,L,L^{\prime}
of Levi subgroups are
involved in
\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)
. In this case
p=2
.

Let
P=LU_{P}
(resp.
P^{\prime}=L^{\prime}U_{P^{\prime}}
) be a parabolic subgroup of
G
as in 9.1, and

C_{0}
(resp.
C_{0}^{\prime}
) the regular unipotent class in
L
(resp.
L^{\prime}
).
Let
P^{\prime\prime}=L^{\prime\prime}U_{P^{\prime\prime}}

be the parabolic subgroup of
G
such that

L^{\prime\prime}=L\cap L^{\prime}
is the Levi subgroup of
P^{\prime\prime}

with type
2A_{1}
. Let
C_{0}^{\prime\prime}
be the regular unipotent class in
L^{\prime\prime}
.
We choose a split element
u_{0}^{\prime\prime}\in C^{\prime\prime F}_{0}
.
Since
C=\operatorname{Ind}_{L^{\prime\prime}}^{G}C_{0}^{\prime\prime}
,
there exists
u\in u_{0}^{\prime\prime}U^{F}_{P^{\prime\prime}}
such that
u\in C^{F}
.
One can find
u_{0}\in C_{0}^{F}

such that
u\in u_{0}U_{Q}^{F}
.
Since
F
acts trivially on
A_{G}(u)
,
F
acts trivially on
A_{L}(u_{0})
.
Moreover,
A_{L}(u_{0})
is abelian.
By the transitivity of induction, we have
C_{0}=\operatorname{Ind}_{L^{\prime\prime}}^{L}C_{0}^{\prime\prime}
.
Since
u_{0}
is regular unipotent, by a similar discussion as in 5.3 (1),
one can apply Lemma 4.9 for
L
, we see that

u_{0}
is a split element in
C_{0}^{F}
. A similar discussion works also for
L^{\prime}
,
namely, one can find
u_{0}^{\prime}\in C_{0}^{\prime F}
such that
u\in u_{0}^{\prime}U_{P^{\prime}}^{F}
, and

u_{0}^{\prime}
becomes a split element in
C_{0}^{\prime F}
.
Then by applying Lemma 4.9 (and Lemma 1.15) for
G
and
L
, we have

\gamma(u_{0},\rho_{0},u,\rho)=1
for any
(u,\rho)
belonging to
(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})
.
Similarly, by applying Lemma 4.9 for
G
and
L^{\prime}
,
we have
\gamma(u_{0}^{\prime},\rho_{0}^{\prime},u,\rho)=1
for any pair
(u,\rho)
belonging to

(L^{\prime},C_{0}^{\prime},\mathscr{E}_{0}^{\prime})
.
Lemma 4.9 can be applied also for
G
and
T
, and we have

\gamma(1,1,u,\rho)=1
for
(u,\rho)
belonging to
(T,\{1\},1)
.
Thus
u
is a split element in
C^{F}
.

Summing up the above discussion, we have

Proposition 9.7
.

Assume that
G=E_{6}
, and that
F
is split on
G
.
Further assume that
q\equiv 1\pmod{3}
if
p\neq 3
. Then a split
element
u\in C^{F}
exists for any class
C
.

9.8.

Next consider the case where
F
is split on
G
,
and
q\equiv-1\pmod{3}
.
In this case, there exists
a class
C
such that
F
acts non-trivially on
A_{G}(u)
for any
choice of
u\in C^{F}
.
If
C
has a representative
u\in C^{F}
such that
F
acts trivially on

A_{G}(u)
, then the discussion in the proof of Proposition 9.7 can be applied,
and we obtain a split element in
C^{F}
.

We now consider
C
such that
F
acts non-trivially on

A_{G}(u)
for any choice of
u\in C^{F}
. Those
C
are given as follows.

(9.8.1)

\displaystyle C:\quad E_{6},\quad E_{6}(a_{1}),\quad A_{5}+A_{1},\quad A_{5},\quad 2A_{2}+A_{1},\quad 2A_{2}.

The corresponding
A_{G}(u)
are given as follows

(9.8.2)

\displaystyle A_{G}(u):\quad\mathbf{Z}_{3(2,p)},\quad\mathbf{Z}_{3},\quad\mathbf{Z}_{3}\times\mathbf{Z}_{2},\quad\mathbf{Z}_{3},\quad\mathbf{Z}_{3},\quad\mathbf{Z}_{3},

where the action of
F
on
A_{G}(u)
is given by the non-trivial action
of
F
on
\mathbf{Z}_{3}
(see 9.2). By 9.2, this action corresponds to the
action of
F
on
Z_{G}
.
Hence if
(u,\rho)\in\mathscr{N}_{G}^{F}
, then
\rho\in A_{G}(u)^{\wedge}_{\chi}
for the trivial
character
\chi\in Z_{G}^{\wedge}
(see 9.3).
It follows that
\rho
must be

1\in\mathbf{Z}_{3}^{\wedge}
or
\pm 1\in(\mathbf{Z}_{3}\times\mathbf{Z}_{2})^{\wedge}
.

(1) If
C\neq E_{6},E_{6}(a_{1}),A_{5}+A_{1}
, there exists
M\neq G
such that
M\cap C\neq\emptyset
.
Let
u=u^{\prime}\in C^{\prime F}\subset M^{F}
for
u\in C^{F}
, and
consider the set
X_{u,u^{\prime}}
. Note that the statement of Lemma 4.11 holds
if we ignore the action of
F
, even if
F
does not act trivially on
A_{G}(u)
.
Hence
A_{M}(u^{\prime})
is regarded as a subgroup of
A_{G}(u)
, compatible with
F
action,
and
X_{u,u^{\prime}}
is isomorphic to the set
A_{G}(u)
on which
A_{G}(u)\times A_{M}(u^{\prime})

acts from left and right. This isomorphism is compatible with
F
.

Now take a split element

u^{\prime}
in
C^{\prime F}
and let
\widetilde{\rho}^{\prime}
be the split extension of

\rho^{\prime}\in A_{M}(u^{\prime})^{\wedge}
to
\widetilde{A}_{M}(u^{\prime})
. In this case,
A_{M}(u^{\prime})
is abelian,
and
\widetilde{\rho}^{\prime}
is the trivial
extension of
\rho^{\prime}
.
Then by the description of
X_{u,u^{\prime}}
given above, we have the following.
Let
\rho=1
(for
A_{G}(u)\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{3}
), or
\rho=\pm 1
(for

A_{G}(u)\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{3}\times\mathbf{Z}_{2}
), then there exists a unique extension

\widetilde{\rho}
to
\widetilde{A}_{G}(u)
such that
F
acts trivially on the
isotypic subspace
\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}(\rho,\rho^{\prime})
in
\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}
.
In fact,
\widetilde{\rho}
is given by the trivial extension of
\rho
to
\widetilde{A}_{G}(u)
.
Since
F
is split on
M
, we have
\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u^{\prime},\widetilde{\rho}^{\prime})=1
.
Since
\delta=1
, by applying Lemma 1.15, we obtain

\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u,\widetilde{\rho})=1
. Hence
u
is a split element in
C^{F}
.

(2) Next assume that
C=E_{6}(a_{1})
or
A_{5}+A_{1}
.
The discussion in 9.6 (1), (2) can be applied
similarly, except the action of
F
.
We follow the notation in 9.6. Then
X_{u,u^{\prime}}\simeq A_{G}(u)
, and

F
acts non-trivially on it. As in the previous discussion, for a split
element
u^{\prime}\in C^{\prime F}
and the split extension
\widetilde{\rho}^{\prime}
of
\rho^{\prime}
,

F
acts trivially on
\varepsilon_{u,u^{\prime}}(\rho,\rho^{\prime})
for the trivial extension
\widetilde{\rho}

of
\rho=1
(resp.
\rho=\pm 1
) to
\widetilde{A}_{G}(u)
if
C=E_{6}(a_{1})
(resp.
if
C=A_{5}+A_{1}
).
Thus
u
is a split element in
C^{F}
by Lemma 1.15.

(3) Finally assume that
C=E_{6}
. Assume that
p=2
.
Then
A_{G}(u)\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{3}\times\mathbf{Z}_{2}
.
Following the notation in 9.7, we consider
C=\operatorname{Ind}_{D_{5}}^{E_{6}}C^{\prime}
.
In this case, we only consider
\rho=\pm 1\in A_{G}(u)^{\wedge}
, thus no
need to consider the part belonging to
(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})
for
L=2A_{2}
.
Hence the discussion in 9.7 is unnecessary,
and just by a similar discussion as in the case
C=E_{6}(a_{1})
,
we see that there exists a split element in
C^{F}
.
The case
p\neq 2
is dealt similarly.

Summing up the above discussion, we have

Proposition 9.9
.

Assume that
G=E_{6}
, and that
F
is split on
G
.
Further assume that
q\equiv-1\pmod{3}
. Then for any class
C
, there exists
a split element
u\in C^{F}
. For each
\rho\in A_{G}(u)^{\wedge}
, the split extension

\widetilde{\rho}
is given by the trivial extension.

9.10.

We now assume that
G=E_{6}
and
F
is non-split. Assume that
p
is good.
In
[
BS
]
, they have proved the existence of
u\in C^{F}
such that

F
acts on
H^{2d_{u}}(\mathscr{B}_{u})
as a scalar multiplication by
(-q)^{d_{u}}
.
This means that
\gamma(1,1,u,\rho)=(-1)^{\delta_{E}}
for
E=V_{(u,\rho)}\in W(E_{6})^{\wedge}
,
where
\delta_{E}=a_{E}-d_{u}
as in (1.7.4), whenever
(u,\rho)
belongs to
(T,\{1\},1)
.
By using the table of
a
-functions given in
[
L1
, 4.11]
,
a_{E}
coincides with
d_{u}

for
E=V_{(u,\rho)}
unless

(9.10.1)

E:\quad 15_{4}\ (C=A_{5}),\quad 10_{9}\ (C=2A_{2}+A_{1}),\quad 15_{16}\ (C=3A_{1}).

In those cases, we have

(d_{u},a_{E})=(4,3),(9,7),(16,15)
, accordingly.
It follows that

(9.10.2)

\gamma(1,1,u,\rho)=\begin{cases}-1&\quad\text{ if $V_{(u,\rho)}=15_{4}$ or $15_{16}$, }\\
1&\quad\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

This formula makes sense even if
p
is bad, and the existence of
u\in C^{F}

satisfying (9.10.2) was verified through the computation of Green functions due to
Geck
[
G2
]
.

In the case of generalized Springer correspondence,
L=2A_{2}\ (p\neq 3)
or

D_{4}\ (p=2)
appears. If
L=2A_{2}
, then
W_{L}\simeq W(G_{2})
and
\delta_{E}=1

for any
E\in\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\wedge}
. While if
L=D_{4}
, then
\mathscr{W}_{L}\simeq A_{2}
. In this case,
it is checked that
\delta_{E}=1
for any
E\in\mathscr{W}_{L}^{\wedge}
.

9.11.

In the case where
|\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)|=1
, the results in 9.10 fit
to (4.7.2), and split elements exist. Thus we may assume that

|\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)|\geq 2
, and
C
is in the following list.

(9.11.1)

\displaystyle E_{6},\ E_{6}(a_{1}),\ D_{5},\ A_{5}+A_{1},\ A_{5},\ D_{4},\ 2A_{2}+A_{1},\ 2A_{2}.

Since
F
is non-split, if
q\equiv-1\pmod{3}
, for any class
C
there
exists a representative
u
such that
F
acts trivially on
A_{G}(u)
. While
if
q\equiv 1\pmod{3}
, there exists a class
C
such that
F
acts non-trivially
on
A_{G}(u)
for any choice of
u\in C^{F}
(see 9.2).
The discussion in the proof of Proposition 9.7 and Proposition 9.9
basically works for almost all
C
by switching the condition

q\equiv\pm 1\pmod{3}
.

First assume that
C
is not in the list (9.5.1). If
p\neq 3
, then
C
is
in the list (9.5.2), and
following the discussion in 9.5, we find an
F
-stable
M
such that
M\cap C\neq\emptyset
.
However, in the list (9.5.2), we have to
exclude the case
C=D_{5}

since
F
-stable
M
does not exist in this case.
We discuss the case
C=D_{5}
, together with
C=A_{5}+A_{1}
, later in 9.13.

For the remaining cases, the discussion in 9.5 can be applied also
for the case
F
non-split as follows. Assume that
(u,\rho)
belongs to

(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})
with
L\neq T
, where
L=2A_{2}
or
D_{4}
. First assume that

L=2A_{2}
. We can choose a Levi subgroup
M
of type
A_{5}
or
2A_{2}+A_{1}
.
By applying the discussion in Section 2 for
M
, one can find
a split element
u^{\prime}\in C^{\prime F}
, and the split extension

\widetilde{\rho}^{\prime}
for
\rho^{\prime}\in A_{M}(u^{\prime})^{\wedge}_{\operatorname{ex}}

such that
\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u^{\prime},\widetilde{\rho}^{\prime})=\nu^{\prime}
, where
\nu^{\prime}
is given by

\nu^{\prime}=\nu_{\lambda,\eta}(-1)^{\delta_{i}}
as in Theorem 2.19, (i) for a certain
root of unity
\nu_{\lambda,\eta}
, and
(-1)^{\delta_{i}}=\pm 1
.
In particular, if
M=2A_{2}+A_{1}
, we have
\nu_{\lambda,\eta}=1
.
By modifying the discussion in the proof of Proposition 4.12, together with
Lemma 1.15, we see that there exists
u\in C^{F}
and an extension
\widetilde{\rho}

for each
\rho\in A_{G}(u)^{\wedge}_{\operatorname{ex}}
such that
\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u,\widetilde{\rho})=\nu^{\prime}
.
Here
\widetilde{\rho}
coincides with the trivial extension of

\rho
(note that
A_{G}(u)
is abelian).
Thus one can write as
\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u,\widetilde{\rho})=\nu_{u,\eta}(-1)^{\delta_{E}}
,
where
\nu_{u,\eta}
coincides with
\nu_{\lambda,\eta}
for
M
, and
u
is a split element
as given in Theorem 4.6 (ii).
Next assume that
L=D_{4}
. Since
C=D_{4}
, we can choose
M
of type
D_{4}
.
By applying the discussion in Section 3 for
u=u^{\prime}=u_{0}
, we see
that
\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u,\widetilde{\rho})=1
. Thus
u
is a split element.

9.12.

Assume that
C
is in the list (9.5.1), namely,

C=E_{6},E_{6}(a_{1})
or
D_{4}(a_{1})
(the case
C=A_{5}+A_{1}
will be discussed
in 9.13.) For
C=E_{6},E_{6}(a_{1})
, the discussion
in 9.6 (1) and (3) can be applied also for the case
F
is non-split. Note that
in 9.6 (1), we have chosen
M=2A_{2}+A_{1}
so that
M
is
F
-stable.
The case where
C=D_{4}(a_{1})
is already done since
|\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)|=1
.

Thus one can find a split element
u\in C^{F}
for
C
in the list (9.5.1).

9.13.

We consider the case where
C=D_{5}
or
A_{5}+A_{1}
.
These cases are dealt with by applying
(a variant of) Lemma 4.9.

For example, assume that
C=A_{5}+A_{1}
with
q\equiv-1\pmod{3}
.
Then
A_{G}(u)\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{3}\times\mathbf{Z}_{2}
.
We have
A_{G}(u)^{\wedge}=\{1,\zeta,\zeta^{2},-1,-\zeta,-\zeta^{2}\}
, where
\zeta
is a non-trivial
character of
\mathbf{Z}_{3}
.
We have
C=\operatorname{Ind}_{3A_{1}}^{E_{6}}1
. Let
Q=MU_{Q}
be such that

M
is of type
A_{5}
, and put
C^{\prime}=\operatorname{Ind}_{3A_{1}}^{A_{5}}1
.
Then
C=\operatorname{Ind}_{A_{5}}^{E_{6}}C^{\prime}
. The Jordan type of
u^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}
is given
by
(33)
as elements in
SL_{6}
, namely
C^{\prime}
is of type
2A_{2}
.
Then
Z_{M}(u^{\prime})\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{3}
.
We have
N=A_{G}(u)
, and so
H=\mathbf{Z}_{2}
. Hence

X_{u,u^{\prime}}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,A_{G}(u)/H\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{3}
with
A_{G}(u)\times A_{M}(u^{\prime})
-actions.
We choose a split element
u^{\prime}\in C^{\prime F}
as in Theorem 2.19.
By applying the discussion in Section 2 for
M
of type
A_{5}
, one can write as

\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u^{\prime},\widetilde{\rho}^{\prime})=\nu^{\prime}
, where
\nu^{\prime}=\nu_{\lambda,\eta}(-1)^{\delta_{i}}

as in Theorem 2.19.
By applying Lemma 1.15,
there exists an element
u\in C^{F}
such that
\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u,\widetilde{\rho})=\nu^{\prime}

for
\rho=\zeta
or
\zeta^{2}
.
(Note that
(u,-\zeta),(u,-\zeta^{2})
are cuspidal pairs for
G
.)
In particular,
\gamma(u_{0},\widetilde{\rho}_{0},u,\widetilde{\rho})
is expressed as in the form (4.6.1).

On the other hand, by 9.10, there exists
u_{1}\in C^{F}
such that

\gamma(1,1,u_{1},\rho_{1})=1
for
\rho_{1}=\pm 1
.
Here
u_{1}
is obtained from
u
as
u_{1}=u_{z}
by twisting by
z\in\mathbf{Z}_{2}
.
It follows that
\gamma(1,1,u,\pm 1)=\gamma(1,1,u_{1},\rho_{1})=1
.
Hence
u
is a split element.

For
C=A_{5}+A_{1}
with
p=3
or
C=D_{5}
, we have
A_{G}(u)\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{2}
,
and a similar discussion as above can be applied (in fact, it is simpler).

The case where
q\equiv 1\pmod{3}
is discussed in a similar way
as in 9.8.

Summing up the above discussion, we have the following.

Proposition 9.14
.

Assume that
G=E_{6}
, and that
F
is non-split on
G
.
Then for any class
C
, there exists a split element
u\in C^{F}
.
For each
\rho\in A_{G}(u)^{\wedge}
, the split extension
\widetilde{\rho}
is given by
the trivial extension of
\rho
.

Combining Proposition 9.7, Proposition 9.9 and Proposition 9.14,
we have proved that Theorem 4.6 holds for
G=E_{6}
.

9.15.

In the case where
G=E_{6}
, we shall prove a similar result as
Theorem 2.21. Recall that
Z_{G}\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{3}
(resp.
Z_{G}=1
) if
p\neq 3

(resp. if
p=3
). We define
F
and
F_{0}
as
in 2.20 by applying
n^{\prime}=3
as follows. Assume that
p\not\equiv 1\pmod{3}
,
and that
F
is a non-split
Frobenius with respect to the
\mathbf{F}_{q}
-structure.
We put
q_{0}=qp
if
p\equiv-1\pmod{3}
, and
q_{0}=q
if
p=3
.
(Note that if
p\equiv 1\pmod{3}
, the following discussion does not hold.)
Let
F_{0}
be the split Frobenius map with respect to the
\mathbf{F}_{q_{0}}
-structure.
Then
q\equiv\pm 1\pmod{3}
if and only if
q_{0}\equiv\mp 1\pmod{3}
.

Here
L
is of type
2A_{2}
or
D_{4}
, and
\mathscr{W}_{L}\simeq W(G_{2})
or
S_{3}
,
accordingly. It is easy to see that the formulas in (1.17.1) hold for

F
and
F_{0}
. Hence Lemma 1.18 holds for
G=E_{6}
.

For each class
C
in
G
, choose a split element
u\in C^{F}
, and
v\in C^{F_{0}}
.
From the construction of split elements for
C^{F}
and for
C^{F_{0}}
, it is easy
to see that the action of
F
on
A_{G}(u)
corresponds to the action of
F_{0}

on
A_{G}(v)
. Hence we have
\widetilde{A}_{G}(u)\simeq\widetilde{A}_{G}(v)
.
Thus one can define a bijection
\xi
from the set of
G^{F}
-conjugacy classes in

G_{\operatorname{uni}}^{F}
to the set of
G^{F_{0}}
-conjugacy classes in
G_{\operatorname{uni}}^{F_{0}}
, by
attaching a split element
v=\xi(u)\in C^{F_{0}}
to a split element
u\in C^{F}
.

Note that
\mathscr{M}_{G}^{F}
and
\mathscr{M}_{G}^{F_{0}}
are naturally identified
(the action of
F
on
Z_{G}
coincides with that of
F_{0}
).
The following result is a generalization of
[
BS
, 4, Thm.]
in the case
where
p
is good.

Theorem 9.16
.

Assume that
G=E_{6}
. Let
F
and
F_{0}
be as in 9.15.
Then similar properties as in Theorem 2.21 hold also for
G
.

Proof.

We prove the statements (i) and (ii) in Theorem 2.21.
We follow the notation in Theorem 2.19 and Theorem 2.21. As in (2.19.3),
we have

(9.16.1)

Y_{j,F}=\nu_{u,\eta}(-1)^{\delta_{j}}Y_{j,F_{0}}

for any
j\in I^{F}=I^{F_{0}}
.
Since Lemma 1.18 holds for
G
, the discussion of the proof of Theorem 2.21 (i)
also holds for
G
. Thus (i) is proved.

Next we show (ii). We prove that the bijection
\xi
satisfies the properties (a) and (b).
Since
(a_{0}+r)/2=d_{u}
, the relation (b) in (ii)
is nothing but the relation (9.16.1). Thus (b) holds.
For (a), since
\widetilde{A}_{G}(u)=\widetilde{A}_{G}(\xi(u))
for a split
u
, it is enough to show
that

(9.16.2)

|Z_{G}(u)^{F}|=(-1)^{\dim Z_{G}(u)}|Z_{G}(\xi(u))^{F_{0}}|(-q)

for a split
u\in C^{F}
.

In the case where
C=D_{4}(a_{1})
, then
Z_{G}(u_{1})\simeq S_{3}
for
u_{1}\in C
,
and a split element
u\in C^{F}
is determined by the condition that

F
acts trivially on
A_{G}(u)
. Similarly, a split element
v\in C^{F_{0}}

is determined by the condition that
F_{0}
acts trivially on
A_{G}(v)
.
Then (9.16.2) is verified directly. If
C\neq D_{4}(a_{1})
, then
A_{G}(u_{1})
is
abelian.

If
|Z_{G}(u_{1})^{F}|
have a common value for any choice of
u_{1}\in C^{F}
,
then
|Z_{G}(v_{1})^{F_{0}}|
have a common value for any choice of
v_{1}\in C^{F_{0}}
.
Thus in this case, (9.16.2) is also checked directly.

We now consider the case where
C\neq D_{4}(a_{1})
, and
|Z_{G}(u_{1})^{F}|

(resp.
|Z_{G}(v_{1})^{F_{0}}|
) have different values for
u_{1}\in C^{F}

(resp. for
v_{1}\in C^{F_{0}}
).
By using the tables in
[
M1
]
, one can check that
such a class
C
is uniquely determined, namely
C=A_{2}
.
Since
A_{G}(u)\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{2}
, there exists two elements

v_{1},v_{2}\in C^{F_{0}}
such that

|Z_{G}(v_{1})^{F_{0}}|=2q_{0}^{26}(q_{0}^{2}-1)^{2}(q_{0}^{3}-1)^{2},\quad|Z_{G}(v_{2})^{F_{0}}|=2q_{0}^{26}(q_{0}^{4}-1)(q_{0}^{6}-1).

Similarly, there exists two elements
u_{1},u_{2}\in G^{F}
such that

|Z_{G}(u_{1})^{F}|
and
|Z_{G}(u_{2})^{F}|
are obtained from them (or by switching from them)
by substituting
-q
to
q_{0}
.
Since
|\mathscr{M}_{G}(C)|=1
, by using the computation of Green functions,
one can show that
v_{1}\in C^{F_{0}}
is a split element,
and the split element
u_{1}\in C^{F}
is determined by the condition that

|Z_{G}(u_{1})^{F}|=2q^{26}(q^{2}-1)(q^{3}+1)^{2}
.
Hence (9.16.2) holds for
C
.

We have verified that our bijection
\xi
satisfies the condition (a), (b) in Theorem 2.21.
The uniqueness of
\xi
is proved in a similar way as in Theorem 2.21.
∎

10.
The case
{}^{3}D_{4}

10.1.

Assume that
G
is a simply connected, almost simple group of
type
D_{4}
, with non-split Frobenius map
F
such that
F^{3}
is split.
In this case, if
(L,C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})\in\mathscr{M}_{G}
with
L\neq T
,
then
L
is of type
2A_{1}
if
p\neq 2
, and is of type
D_{4}
(namely,

(C_{0},\mathscr{E}_{0})
is cuspidal in
G
) if
p=2
. In the former case,
L
is not

F
-stable, hence
\mathscr{M}_{G}^{F}=\{(T,\{1\},1)\}
. Thus we have only to
consider the Green functions on
G_{\operatorname{ad}}
.

Green functions of
G^{F}
(for any
p
) was first computed by Spaltenstein
[
Sp3
]

with respect to
Deligne-Lusztig’s Green functions, in the course of the computation of unipotent characters
of
G^{F}
. In the case where
p=2
, it is also easy to compute them as in the case of
exceptional groups. From those results, it can be checked, for an
F
-stable class
C
in
G
,
that there exists
u\in C^{F}
such that
\gamma(1,1,u,\rho)=1
for any
\rho\in A_{G}(u)^{\wedge}

such that
(u,\rho)
belongs to
(T,\{1\},1)
. It follows that split elements exist for
G
.
